$$

$$

This is the **pipeline/store-and-forward model**, assuming equal transmission times and negligible propagation/queuing delay.

Absolutely. Let's start **CS 606T – Advanced Computer Networks, Chapter 1** from the foundation and build toward the switching techniques.

# Chapter 1 — Introduction & Switching Techniques

## Part 1: Why do we need Internetworking?

Let's start with the fundamental problem your professor mentioned:

> **"Not all networks are directly connected."**

Imagine two computers:

```text
Computer A ───────────── Computer B
```

A point-to-point link works perfectly here.

But now imagine thousands or millions of computers:

```text
A ── B
│    │
C ── D ── E
     │
     F ── G ── H
```

We cannot practically create a direct physical link between every pair of hosts.

### Why?

Because different networks have different limitations.

### 1. Point-to-point link

A point-to-point link connects only **two devices**.

For example:

```text
A ================= B
```

If we have 1000 computers and want every computer directly connected to every other computer, the number of links becomes enormous.

The number of possible direct connections is:

$$
\frac{n(n-1)}{2}
$$

For $n=1000$:

$$
\frac{1000(999)}{2}=499,500
$$

Clearly, this doesn't scale.

---

### 2. Ethernet limitations

A local Ethernet network can connect many hosts, but it still has practical limitations in size, bandwidth, and physical/network architecture.

So we can't simply keep extending one Ethernet network indefinitely.

---

### 3. Wireless limitations

Wireless communication has another problem:

> **Radio signals have limited range.**

For example:

```text
        Wireless range
       <------------>

          AP
       /  |  \
      /   |   \
     A    B    C
```

A device outside the radio range cannot directly communicate with the access point.

---

# The Solution: Internetworking

Instead of trying to create one gigantic network, we connect **multiple smaller networks together**.

For example:

```text
 Network A          Network B          Network C

 A ─ B ─ C          D ─ E ─ F          G ─ H
      │                   │                │
      └─── Switch ───── Router ─────── Router
```

This idea is called:

> **Internetworking**

### Definition

**Internetworking is the process of connecting different networks and network technologies so that they can communicate and operate as a larger interconnected network.**

The Internet is the most important example.

---

# Part 2: Switches and Routers

Now we have a problem:

> If networks are separate, what device connects them?

Your professor introduces two important devices:

### Switch

A **switch** primarily connects devices/networks of the same or compatible link technology and forwards traffic toward the appropriate output.

Example:

```text
        Host A
          │
          │
Host B ─ Switch ─ Host C
          │
          │
        Host D
```

A switch receives a packet on one input and determines the appropriate output.

---

### Router

A **router** connects different networks and forwards packets between them.

Example:

```text
Network A                    Network B

A ─ B ─ C                    X ─ Y ─ Z
      │                          │
      └────── Router ────────────┘
```

Think of it this way:

> **Switch → mainly connects nodes within a network/link technology**

> **Router → connects different networks**

Don't memorize this as an absolute rule for every modern device, but it is the conceptual distinction your introductory material is establishing.

---

# Part 3: What exactly does a switch do?

Your professor gives this important definition:

> A switch is a **multi-input, multi-output device** that transfers packets from an input to one or more outputs.

Let's visualize it.

```text
                Output 1
                   ↑
                   │
Input 1 ────┐      │
Input 2 ────┤   SWITCH ──── Output 2
Input 3 ────┤      │
Input 4 ────┘      ↓
                Output 3
```

Suppose a packet arrives:

```text
Packet
+-----------------------+
| Destination = Host D  |
| Data                  |
+-----------------------+
```

The switch needs to answer:

> **"Which output should I send this packet to?"**

That's the fundamental problem of **network switching**.

---

# Part 4: How does a switch know where to send a packet?

This is one of the most important concepts in this chapter.

The switch examines information in the **packet header**.

Simplified:

```text
+-------------------------+
| Header                  |
| Destination information |
+-------------------------+
| Data                    |
+-------------------------+
```

The identifier in the header is used to make the forwarding decision.

The switch then consults some form of **forwarding table**.

For example:

| Destination | Output |
|---|---|
| A | 1 |
| B | 2 |
| C | 3 |
| D | 4 |

Suppose:

```text
Destination = C
```

The switch looks up:

```text
C → Output 3
```

and forwards the packet there.

---

# Part 5: Two Fundamental Approaches

Now we reach the central concept of your chapter.

There are two fundamental ways of forwarding traffic:

```text
                    Network Switching
                          │
             ┌────────────┴────────────┐
             │                         │
      Connectionless            Connection-oriented
             │                         │
      Packet/Datagram            Circuit/VC
       Switching                  Switching
```

### Approach 1 — Connectionless

Each packet contains enough information to independently determine where it should go.

This is:

> **Datagram / Packet Switching**

### Approach 2 — Connection-oriented

A connection/path is established before data transfer.

This leads to:

> **Circuit Switching**

and, in packet networks:

> **Virtual Circuit Switching**

---

# Part 6: Packet / Datagram Switching

This is our first switching technique.

The fundamental idea is extremely simple:

> **Every packet carries the destination address, and each packet is forwarded independently.**

Consider:

```text
A → S1 → S2 → S3 → B
```

Suppose A wants to send three packets:

```text
P1
P2
P3
```

Each packet contains something like:

```text
+-----------------------+
| Destination = B       |
+-----------------------+
| Data                  |
+-----------------------+
```

The network doesn't necessarily establish a dedicated path first.

Each switch makes its forwarding decision based on the packet.

---

## Example

Suppose:

```text
        S2
       /  \
      /    \
A → S1      S4 → B
      \    /
       \  /
        S3
```

Initially:

```text
P1 → S1 → S2 → S4 → B
```

But if the forwarding information changes, another packet might travel:

```text
P2 → S1 → S3 → S4 → B
```

So:

$$
P_1 \neq P_2 \text{ necessarily follow the same path}
$$

This is a **very important characteristic** of datagram switching.

---

# Part 7: Forwarding Table

How does S1 know where to send the packet?

It has a forwarding table.

Example:

| Destination | Next hop |
|---|---|
| B | S2 |
| C | S3 |
| D | S4 |

Packet:

```text
Destination = B
```

S1 checks:

```text
B → S2
```

So:

```text
S1 → S2
```

Then S2 performs its own lookup.

This continues until the packet reaches the destination.

---

# Part 8: Why are forwarding tables difficult?

This is another point directly from your professor's slides.

Imagine a tiny network:

```text
A ─ B ─ C
    │
    D
```

Easy.

But imagine the Internet:

```text
          ┌───────┐
      ┌───┤ Router├───┐
      │   └───────┘   │
      │       │       │
 ┌────┴───┐   │   ┌───┴────┐
 │ Router │───┘   │ Router │
 └────────┘       └────────┘
      │               │
     ...             ...
```

Now there are huge numbers of destinations and paths.

Additionally, topology can change:

```text
Before:

A → B → C → D

After:

A → B → X → D
```

Therefore, forwarding information must adapt to network changes.

This is why routing and forwarding become major topics in advanced computer networks.

---

# Part 9: Store-and-Forward

Your professor explicitly mentions:

> **Store and forward the packets**

What does this mean?

Suppose:

```text
A → S1 → S2 → B
```

A packet arrives at S1.

S1 doesn't simply magically teleport it to S2.

Conceptually:

### Step 1

Receive the packet.

```text
A ──→ [S1]
```

### Step 2

Store/buffer the packet.

```text
[S1]
┌─────────────┐
│ Packet      │
│ in buffer   │
└─────────────┘
```

### Step 3

Transmit it on the outgoing link.

```text
[S1] ─────────→ S2
```

This introduces **transmission delay** at each hop and potentially **queuing delay** if the output link is busy.

---

# Part 10: Best-Effort Delivery

Your professor also says:

> **Best effort delivery**

This means the network attempts to deliver the packet, but the basic packet-switching service does not necessarily guarantee:

- delivery
- fixed delay
- bandwidth
- absence of congestion
- fixed path

For example:

```text
A → R1 → R2 → B
       X
    congestion
```

The packet may experience delay or potentially be dropped depending on the network.

Therefore:

> **Best effort ≠ guaranteed delivery**

---

# Part 11: Important Characteristics of Datagram Switching

You should remember these six points:

### 1. Connectionless

No dedicated connection is established before sending each packet.

### 2. Destination address in every packet

Each packet contains the information needed for forwarding.

### 3. Independent forwarding

Each packet is treated independently.

### 4. Packets can take different paths

```text
P1 → Path A
P2 → Path B
P3 → Path A
```

### 5. Store-and-forward

Routers/switches receive and forward packets.

### 6. Best effort

The network does not inherently guarantee fixed delivery characteristics.

---

Answer these **without notes**. I'll check your understanding before we move to circuit switching.


What information does the router use to determine where to forward it?

### Q5
What does **store-and-forward** mean?

### Q6
What does **best-effort delivery** mean?

### Q7 — Tricky
Suppose the network has this topology:

```text
A → R1 → R2 → B
     │
     ↓
     R3 ─────→ B
```

P1 travels:

```text
A → R1 → R2 → B
```

P2 travels:

```text
A → R1 → R3 → B
```

Is this possible in **datagram switching**? Why?

### Q8 — Important
What's the fundamental difference between:

**connectionless switching** and **connection-oriented switching**?

Send me your **8 answers**. I'll evaluate them like a professor and then we'll move to **Circuit Switching**, where we'll build the concept from the same A→B example.

Sure. Here are the **8 answers in exam-ready form**, with enough explanation to understand the concept rather than just memorize it.

# CS 606T — Chapter 1: Answers

### Q1. Why can't we simply connect every computer directly to every other computer in a global network?

**Answer:**

A point-to-point link connects only two devices. If every computer had to be directly connected to every other computer, the number of links would grow rapidly.

For $n$ devices, the number of direct links required would be:

$$
\frac{n(n-1)}{2}
$$

For example, with 1,000 devices:

$$
\frac{1000(999)}{2}=499,500
$$

Therefore, directly connecting every device is **expensive, complex, and not scalable**.

Instead, networks use **switches and routers to interconnect smaller networks**, which leads to the concept of **internetworking**.

**Key point:**  
> Directly connecting every host does not scale; intermediate networking devices allow large networks to be built efficiently.

---

### Q2. What is the fundamental purpose of internetworking?

**Answer:**

**Internetworking** is the process of connecting different networks and network technologies so that they can communicate with each other and operate as one larger interconnected network.

For example:

```text
Network A ── Router ── Network B ── Router ── Network C
```

The fundamental purpose is to enable communication between hosts that belong to **different networks**.

**Exam definition:**

> Internetworking enables multiple heterogeneous networks to be interconnected to form a large-scale network such as the Internet.

---

### Q3. A packet arrives at a router with `Destination = X`. What information does the router use to determine where to forward it?

**Answer:**

The router examines the **destination information in the packet header** and consults its **forwarding table/routing table** to determine the appropriate next hop or output interface.

For example:

| Destination | Next Hop |
|---|---|
| X | R3 |
| Y | R5 |
| Z | R2 |

If:

```text
Destination = X
```

the router performs a lookup:

```text
X → R3
```

and forwards the packet toward R3.

**Key point:**

> The packet header provides the destination information, while the forwarding table determines the appropriate outgoing path/interface.

---

### Q4. In datagram switching, if packets P1 and P2 are sent from A to B, must they follow the same path?

**Answer:**

**No.**

In datagram/packet switching, every packet is forwarded **independently**.

Therefore:

```text
P1: A → R1 → R2 → B

P2: A → R1 → R3 → B
```

is possible.

This can happen because forwarding decisions may change due to:

- routing-table changes
- network failures
- congestion
- changing network conditions

Consequently, packets can potentially arrive at the destination through **different paths and at different times**.

**Important:** Because packets may follow different paths, they may also arrive **out of order**.

---

### Q5. What does store-and-forward mean?

**Answer:**

**Store-and-forward** means that a switch or router first **receives the packet and stores/buffers it**, and then forwards it through the appropriate outgoing link.

For example:

```text
A ──→ R1 ──→ R2 ──→ B
```

At R1:

```text
1. Receive packet
       ↓
2. Store/buffer packet
       ↓
3. Determine output
       ↓
4. Transmit packet to R2
```

The packet therefore has to be received before it can be transmitted onto the next link.

**Consequence:**  
Store-and-forward introduces **transmission delay at each hop** and potentially **queuing delay** if the outgoing link is busy.

---

### Q6. What does best-effort delivery mean?

**Answer:**

**Best-effort delivery** means that the network attempts to deliver packets to their destination but does not inherently guarantee successful delivery or fixed performance.

It does not necessarily guarantee:

- delivery
- fixed delay
- fixed bandwidth
- no packet loss
- no congestion
- a fixed path

For example:

```text
A → R1 → R2 → B
          ↑
       congestion
```

Congestion can cause packets to experience additional delay or potentially be dropped.

**Exam definition:**

> Best-effort delivery provides an attempt to deliver packets without guaranteeing delivery, delay, bandwidth, or other performance characteristics.

---

### Q7. Is this possible in datagram switching?

```text
P1: A → R1 → R2 → B

P2: A → R1 → R3 → B
```

**Answer:**

**Yes, this is possible in datagram switching.**

Datagram switching is **connectionless**, and each packet is forwarded independently.

Therefore:

```text
P1 → R2
P2 → R3
```

can occur if the forwarding decisions at R1 differ for the two packets.

For example, the forwarding information could change between the arrival of P1 and P2.

### Important consequence

The packets may:

- follow different paths
- experience different delays
- arrive out of order

For example:

```text
P1 sent first ───────────────→ arrives at 20 ms

P2 sent second ───────→ arrives at 10 ms
```

So P2 could potentially arrive before P1.

**This is a very important characteristic of datagram switching.**

---

### Q8. What is the fundamental difference between connectionless and connection-oriented switching?

**Answer:**

The fundamental difference is **whether a connection/path is established before data transmission**.

| Connectionless | Connection-oriented |
|---|---|
| No connection setup required | Connection/path is established first |
| Each packet is forwarded independently | Packets follow an established logical/physical connection |
| Destination information is carried in packets | Connection/VC identifier can be used after setup |
| Packets may take different paths | Packets generally follow the established path |
| Example: Datagram switching | Examples: Circuit switching, Virtual Circuit switching |

### Simple analogy

**Connectionless:**

> "Here is the destination. Figure out how to get there."

**Connection-oriented:**

> "First establish a route between us. Then send the data through that established connection."

---

# ⭐ 8 Things You Should Be Able to Recall

If your professor asks about **Datagram/Packet Switching**, remember:

```text
             DATAGRAM SWITCHING
                    │
       ┌────────────┼────────────┐
       ↓            ↓            ↓
 Connectionless   Independent   Destination
                  packets       in packet
       │            │            │
       └────────────┼────────────┘
                    ↓
             Forwarding Table
                    ↓
             Store-and-Forward
                    ↓
              Best Effort
                    ↓
          Different paths possible
                    ↓
             Possible reordering
```

### One-line exam answer

> **Datagram switching is a connectionless packet-switching technique in which every packet carries destination information and is independently forwarded using forwarding tables; consequently, packets may follow different paths and experience different delays.**

Next, the natural step is **Circuit Switching**—and this is where the distinction between **connection setup, dedicated path, resource reservation, transmission delay, and propagation delay** becomes important.

# CS 606T — Chapter 1
## Part 2: Circuit Switching

Now we move from **connectionless packet switching** to **connection-oriented switching**.

The key idea is:

> **Before sending data, establish a dedicated path between the source and destination.**

---

# 1. Why do we need Circuit Switching?

Consider two hosts:

```text
A ── R1 ── R2 ── R3 ── B
```

With **datagram switching**, every packet can independently make forwarding decisions.

Circuit switching takes a different approach.

Before transmitting the actual data:

```text
A ── R1 ── R2 ── R3 ── B
      ═════════════════
       Dedicated path
```

A connection is first established.

Once the circuit is established, data can flow through that circuit.

---

# 2. Basic Working of Circuit Switching

Circuit switching generally has **three phases**:

```text
       Circuit Switching
              │
      ┌───────┼────────┐
      ↓       ↓        ↓
   Setup   Data      Teardown
            Transfer
```

### Phase 1 — Circuit Setup

A requests communication with B.

```text
A → R1 → R2 → R3 → B
```

The network establishes a path.

For example:

```text
A ── R1 ── R2 ── R3 ── B
     ═════════════════
       Circuit
```

Resources are allocated along this path.

---

### Phase 2 — Data Transfer

Now A sends data:

```text
A ═══> R1 ═══> R2 ═══> R3 ═══> B
```

The established circuit is used for the communication.

---

### Phase 3 — Circuit Teardown

When communication finishes, the circuit is released.

```text
Circuit established
       ↓
Data transfer
       ↓
Circuit released
```

The resources can then be used by other connections.

---

# 3. The Most Important Idea: Dedicated Resources

This is what makes circuit switching fundamentally different from datagram switching.

Suppose a link has:

$$
10\text{ Mbps}
$$

A circuit might reserve part of this capacity for a particular communication.

Conceptually:

```text
10 Mbps link

┌──────────────────────────────┐
│ A → B │ C → D │ E → F │ ... │
└──────────────────────────────┘
```

Resources are allocated to established circuits.

Depending on the circuit-switched system, this can involve:

- bandwidth
- transmission capacity
- switching resources

The important idea is:

> **Resources are reserved for the duration of the circuit.**

---

# 4. Why is it called a "Circuit"?

Because the network creates a logical/physical communication path that behaves like a dedicated circuit between the communicating endpoints.

Think about a traditional telephone call.

When you call someone:

```text
You → telephone network → Other person
```

The network establishes the connection first.

Then you talk.

When the call ends, the connection is released.

That's the classic circuit-switching model.

---

# 5. Example

Suppose:

```text
A ── S1 ── S2 ── S3 ── B
```

A wants to communicate with B.

### Step 1: Setup

The network establishes:

```text
A → S1 → S2 → S3 → B
```

and reserves resources.

### Step 2: Data

Now:

```text
A → DATA → S1 → DATA → S2 → DATA → S3 → DATA → B
```

The path remains associated with this communication.

### Step 3: Teardown

When finished:

```text
A → release → network
```

The reserved resources are released.

---

# 6. Circuit Switching vs Datagram Switching

This is one of the **most important comparisons for your exam**.

| Feature | Circuit Switching | Datagram Switching |
|---|---|---|
| Connection | Connection-oriented | Connectionless |
| Setup | Required | Not required |
| Path | Established before data | Determined independently |
| Resource reservation | Yes | Generally no |
| Packet forwarding | Along established circuit | Independent |
| Path changes during communication | Generally no | Possible |
| Delay | More predictable after setup | Can vary |
| Congestion | Can prevent setup/resource allocation | Can cause queuing/loss |
| Resource utilization | Can be inefficient for bursty traffic | Efficient for bursty traffic |
| Example | Traditional telephone network | IP network |

---

# 7. The Big Advantage: Predictable Performance

Once the circuit is established and resources are reserved, the communication can have more predictable performance.

Suppose:

```text
A ═══════════════════ B
       dedicated
       resources
```

Other users cannot simply take the resources allocated to that circuit.

This makes circuit switching suitable for applications requiring relatively predictable continuous communication.

Your professor's surgical-robot example is based on this idea.

---

# 8. But There Is a Major Disadvantage

Consider this situation:

```text
A ═══════════════════ B
       circuit
```

Suppose A sends data for only 1 second and then remains silent for 30 seconds.

The circuit may still remain reserved during that period.

```text
Time →

Data:       ███
Idle:          ─────────────────
Resource:    RESERVED
```

The reserved capacity isn't necessarily available for other users.

This is **inefficient for bursty traffic**.

---

# 9. Why Packet Switching Handles Bursty Traffic Better

Imagine an online shopping website.

```text
User 1: ████────────────████
User 2: ──██───────────────
User 3: █████──────████─────
```

Traffic is not continuous.

Packet switching allows network resources to be statistically shared:

```text
Shared link
────────────────────────────
 User 1 ──┐
 User 2 ──┼──→ Shared capacity
 User 3 ──┘
────────────────────────────
```

When one user isn't transmitting, other users can use the capacity.

Therefore:

> **Packet switching is generally well suited to bursty data traffic.**

---

# 10. Circuit Switching Has Setup Delay

This is important for numerical problems.

Before data can be transmitted:

```text
Setup
  ↓
Data transmission
  ↓
Teardown
```

Suppose setup takes:

$$
50\text{ ms}
$$

Then the sender cannot start normal data transmission until the circuit setup phase is completed.

Therefore total time includes setup time.

---

# 11. Transmission Delay vs Propagation Delay

Your professor's numerical question uses these concepts.

### Transmission Delay

Time required to put all bits of a packet/file onto the link.

$$
\boxed{T_{trans}=\frac{L}{R}}
$$

where:

- $L$ = number of bits
- $R$ = transmission rate in bits/sec

Example:

File:

$$
5\text{ MB}
$$

Bandwidth:

$$
10\text{ Mbps}
$$

Using the professor's decimal convention:

$$
5\text{ MB}=5\times10^6\text{ bytes}
$$

Therefore:

$$
5\times10^6\times8
=40\times10^6\text{ bits}
$$

Then:

$$
T_{trans}
=
\frac{40\times10^6}{10\times10^6}
=4s
$$

---

### Propagation Delay

Time required for the signal to physically travel through the link.

$$
\boxed{T_{prop}=\frac{d}{v}}
$$

where:

- $d$ = distance
- $v$ = propagation speed

For the professor's example:

$$
d=2000\text{ km}
$$

$$
v=200000\text{ km/s}
$$

Therefore:

$$
T_{prop}
=
\frac{2000}{200000}
=
0.01s
$$

$$
=10ms
$$

---

# 12. Professor's Circuit-Switching Numerical

Given:

$$
R=10Mbps
$$

$$
d=2000km
$$

$$
v=200000km/s
$$

$$
Setup=50ms
$$

$$
File=5MB
$$

### Step 1 — Setup

$$
T_{setup}=0.05s
$$

### Step 2 — Propagation

$$
T_{prop}=\frac{2000}{200000}
=0.01s
$$

### Step 3 — Transmission

$$
5MB=40\times10^6bits
$$

$$
T_{trans}
=
\frac{40\times10^6}{10\times10^6}
=4s
$$

### Total

Under the simplified model used in the PPT:

$$
T_{total}
=
T_{setup}+T_{prop}+T_{trans}
$$

$$
=0.05+0.01+4
$$

$$
\boxed{T_{total}=4.06s}
$$

---

# 13. A Very Important Conceptual Trap

Don't confuse:

### Transmission delay

> How long does it take to **push the bits onto the link?**

with:

### Propagation delay

> How long does it take for the **signal to travel across the link?**

Imagine a 2,000 km cable.

```text
Sender                                  Receiver
  A                                        B
  │========================================│
        2000 km
```

If you're transmitting a huge file:

**Transmission delay** depends on:

$$
\frac{\text{data size}}{\text{bandwidth}}
$$

while **propagation delay** depends on:

$$
\frac{\text{distance}}{\text{propagation speed}}
$$

Increasing file size affects transmission delay, **not propagation delay**.

Increasing distance affects propagation delay, **not transmission delay**.

---

# 14. When Is Circuit Switching Useful?

Conceptually, circuit switching is attractive when communication is:

- continuous
- long-lived
- relatively predictable
- sensitive to variation in network performance

Classic example:

> **Traditional telephone calls**

Your professor's example:

> **Remote surgical robot**

is intended to illustrate the need for predictable communication characteristics.

However, remember that a real remote-surgery system would require much more than simply choosing circuit switching; the scenario is an educational illustration of the networking property.

---

# 15. Circuit Switching — Exam Definition

Memorize the **concept**, not just the sentence:

> **Circuit switching is a connection-oriented switching technique in which a dedicated communication path and associated resources are established between the source and destination before data transmission. The resources remain allocated for the duration of the connection and are released when the communication ends.**

---

# 16. Mental Model

If you remember only one thing:

### Datagram

```text
"Send each packet independently."

A → ? → ? → ? → B
```

### Circuit

```text
"First establish the path, then use it."

A ═══════════════════ B
      established
       circuit
```

---

Sure. Here are all **8 answers**, with the reasoning you should know for the exam.

# CS 606T — Circuit Switching: Answers

### Q1. What are the three phases of circuit switching?

**Answer:**

Circuit switching has three main phases:

1. **Circuit Setup**
2. **Data Transfer**
3. **Circuit Teardown**

```text
Setup
  ↓
Data Transfer
  ↓
Teardown
```

During **setup**, a path and required resources are established.

During **data transfer**, the actual information is transmitted over the established circuit.

During **teardown**, the circuit is released and its resources become available for other connections.

---

### Q2. Why does circuit switching require a setup phase?

**Answer:**

Circuit switching requires setup because the network must first establish a **dedicated communication path** and allocate the required resources before data transmission begins.

For example:

```text
A ── R1 ── R2 ── R3 ── B
     ═════════════════
       Established
        circuit
```

The setup phase determines the path and reserves resources along it.

Only after successful setup can the source begin normal data transmission.

**Exam point:**

> Setup introduces an initial delay, but after setup the communication can have more predictable performance.

---

### Q3. Why can circuit switching waste bandwidth when traffic is bursty?

**Answer:**

In circuit switching, resources are reserved for the connection even when the user is temporarily not transmitting data.

For example:

```text
Time →

Data:      ████───────████
Circuit:   ███████████████
           ↑
      Resources remain
        allocated
```

During the idle period, the reserved capacity may not be used by that connection but is still allocated to it.

Therefore, circuit switching can be inefficient for **bursty traffic**, such as web browsing, where data arrives in bursts separated by idle periods.

Packet switching can use **statistical multiplexing**, allowing other users to utilize the shared capacity.

---

### Q4. A link has bandwidth 20 Mbps. A file is 10 MB. Ignoring all other delays, what is its transmission delay?

Given:

$$
R=20Mbps
$$

$$
L=10MB
$$

Convert MB to bits:

$$
10MB=10\times10^6\text{ bytes}
$$

Since:

$$
1\text{ byte}=8\text{ bits}
$$

$$
L=10\times10^6\times8
$$

$$
L=80\times10^6\text{ bits}
$$

Transmission delay:

$$
T_{trans}=\frac{L}{R}
$$

$$
=\frac{80\times10^6}{20\times10^6}
$$

$$
\boxed{T_{trans}=4s}
$$

### Answer: **4 seconds**

---

### Q5. A signal travels 3,000 km at 200,000 km/s. What is its propagation delay?

Given:

$$
d=3000km
$$

$$
v=200000km/s
$$

Propagation delay:

$$
T_{prop}=\frac{d}{v}
$$

$$
=\frac{3000}{200000}
$$

$$
=0.015s
$$

Convert to milliseconds:

$$
0.015\times1000=15ms
$$

### Answer:

$$
\boxed{15ms}
$$

---

### Q6. Which delay depends on data size: transmission delay or propagation delay?

**Answer:**

$$
\boxed{\text{Transmission delay}}
$$

Transmission delay is:

$$
T_{trans}=\frac{L}{R}
$$

where $L$ is the amount of data in bits.

Therefore, increasing the file/packet size increases transmission delay.

Propagation delay is:

$$
T_{prop}=\frac{d}{v}
$$

It depends on:

- distance $d$
- propagation speed $v$

It does **not** depend on the amount of data being transmitted.

### Easy memory trick:

> **Transmission → Data size + bandwidth**

> **Propagation → Distance + signal speed**

---

### Q7. If the distance between two hosts doubles but bandwidth remains unchanged, which changes?

Options:

- Transmission delay
- Propagation delay
- Both
- Neither

**Answer:**

$$
\boxed{\text{Propagation delay}}
$$

Because:

$$
T_{prop}=\frac{d}{v}
$$

If distance doubles:

$$
d\rightarrow2d
$$

then:

$$
T_{prop}\rightarrow2T_{prop}
$$

But transmission delay is:

$$
T_{trans}=\frac{L}{R}
$$

Since neither file size $L$ nor bandwidth $R$ changed:

$$
T_{trans}=\text{unchanged}
$$

### Therefore:

> **Propagation delay doubles; transmission delay remains unchanged.**

---

### Q8. A user establishes a circuit but then remains completely idle for 20 seconds. Why can this be inefficient compared with packet switching?

**Answer:**

Circuit switching reserves resources for the established connection for its duration.

Suppose:

```text
Circuit established
       ↓
Data
       ↓
20 seconds idle
       ↓
More data
       ↓
Circuit released
```

During the 20-second idle period, the resources remain associated with that circuit even though the user isn't transmitting.

This can result in **underutilization of network capacity**.

In packet switching, resources are shared dynamically:

```text
User A ──┐
User B ──┼──→ Shared link
User C ──┘
```

When User A is idle, User B or User C can potentially use the available capacity.

Therefore:

> **Circuit switching can be inefficient for bursty traffic, whereas packet switching is generally more efficient because network resources are statistically shared.**

---

# ⭐ Most Important Formulas So Far

Keep these three formulas very clear:

### 1. Transmission Delay

$$
\boxed{T_{trans}=\frac{L}{R}}
$$

**Depends on:** data size and bandwidth.

---

### 2. Propagation Delay

$$
\boxed{T_{prop}=\frac{d}{v}}
$$

**Depends on:** distance and propagation speed.

---

### 3. Simplified Circuit-Switching Total Time

For the type of problem in your professor's PPT:

$$
\boxed{
T_{total}=T_{setup}+T_{prop}+T_{trans}
}
$$

But remember that **the exact end-to-end delay formula depends on the assumptions of the problem**—for example, whether propagation occurs during setup and whether acknowledgments/teardown are included.

---

## 🔥 One important distinction to remember

| | Circuit Switching | Datagram Switching |
|---|---|---|
| Setup | Required | Not required |
| Path | Established first | Determined per packet |
| Resources | Reserved | Dynamically shared |
| Delay | More predictable after setup | Variable |
| Bursty traffic | Less efficient | Generally efficient |
| Packets | Follow established circuit | Independently forwarded |

The next topic is **Virtual Circuit Switching**, where we combine the idea of an **established logical path** with the **resource-sharing nature of packet-switched networks**.

# CS 606T — Chapter 1
# Part 3: Virtual Circuit Switching

Now we come to the **third switching technique** in your professor's chapter.

So far:

```text
                    Network Switching
                           │
             ┌─────────────┴─────────────┐
             │                           │
       Connectionless              Connection-oriented
             │                           │
       Datagram                    ┌──────┴──────┐
       Switching                   │             │
                              Circuit         Virtual
                              Switching       Circuit
```

The important thing about **Virtual Circuit (VC) switching** is that it combines ideas from both approaches.

---

# 1. Why do we need Virtual Circuits?

Let's recall the two techniques.

### Datagram switching

```text
P1 ──→ Path 1 ──→ B
P2 ──→ Path 2 ──→ B
P3 ──→ Path 1 ──→ B
```

Advantages:

- No connection setup
- Efficient sharing of network resources
- Good for bursty traffic

But:

- Packets may take different paths
- Variable delay
- Packets may arrive out of order
- Each packet needs destination information

---

### Circuit switching

```text
A ═══════════════════ B
      Dedicated
       circuit
```

Advantages:

- Established path
- Predictable behavior
- Resources can be reserved

But:

- Requires setup
- Resources can be wasted during idle periods
- Dedicated physical resources can be inefficient for bursty data

---

## Virtual circuit switching tries to combine useful ideas from both.

The basic idea is:

> **Establish a logical path before sending data, but carry the data using packets over shared physical network infrastructure.**

---

# 2. What is a Virtual Circuit?

A **virtual circuit** is a logical connection established between a source and destination through a packet-switched network.

It is called "virtual" because the path is **logical rather than necessarily being a physically dedicated circuit**.

Imagine:

```text
Physical network:

       R1 ───── R2
      /  \     /  \
     /    \   /    \
    A      R3       B
     \    /   \    /
      ───R4───────
```

A virtual circuit might establish:

```text
A → R1 → R2 → B
```

This becomes the logical path for that connection.

Other users can still use the same physical links.

---

# 3. Three Phases of Virtual Circuit Switching

Just like circuit switching, VC switching generally has three phases:

```text
             Virtual Circuit
                    │
       ┌────────────┼────────────┐
       ↓            ↓            ↓
    Setup        Transfer      Teardown
```

### Phase 1 — VC Setup

The network establishes a logical path.

```text
A → R1 → R2 → R3 → B
```

Each intermediate device establishes the appropriate VC state.

---

### Phase 2 — Data Transfer

Packets are sent along the established virtual circuit.

```text
P1 ───────────────→ B
P2 ───────────────→ B
P3 ───────────────→ B
```

The packets normally follow the same logical path.

---

### Phase 3 — Teardown

When communication finishes, the virtual circuit state is removed.

```text
VC established
      ↓
Data transfer
      ↓
VC released
```

---

# 4. The Most Important Concept: VC Identifier

This is where VC switching becomes technically interesting.

In datagram switching, a packet might contain:

```text
+---------------------+
| Destination Address |
+---------------------+
| Data                |
+---------------------+
```

Every router examines the destination address.

In virtual circuit switching, after setup, packets can carry a **Virtual Circuit Identifier (VCI)**.

For example:

```text
+-------------------+
| VCI = 17          |
+-------------------+
| Data              |
+-------------------+
```

The router can use:

$$
VCI = 17
$$

to determine the appropriate outgoing interface and next VC identifier.

---

# 5. Example of VCI Translation

Suppose:

```text
A ── R1 ── R2 ── B
```

During setup, the network might establish:

```text
A → R1 → R2 → B
```

and assign identifiers:

```text
A → R1 : VCI 10
R1 → R2: VCI 25
R2 → B : VCI 7
```

Notice something important:

> The VCI doesn't necessarily remain the same across every link.

A router can perform a mapping:

```text
Incoming
VCI 10
   │
   ↓
  R1
   │
   ↓
Outgoing
VCI 25
```

Then:

```text
R2:

Incoming VCI 25
       │
       ↓
Outgoing VCI 7
```

So the forwarding state can look like:

| Incoming Interface | Incoming VCI | Outgoing Interface | Outgoing VCI |
|---|---:|---|---:|
| 1 | 10 | 2 | 25 |
| 2 | 25 | 3 | 7 |

This is a **very important concept** for understanding VC networks.

---

# 6. Datagram vs Virtual Circuit

Let's compare the packet header.

### Datagram

Each packet carries the destination address.

```text
P1:
Destination = B

P2:
Destination = B

P3:
Destination = B
```

Each packet is independently forwarded.

---

### Virtual Circuit

Packets carry a VC identifier.

```text
P1:
VCI = 10

P2:
VCI = 10

P3:
VCI = 10
```

The network already knows:

```text
VCI 10 → established path
```

So forwarding can be simpler/faster than doing a full destination lookup at every hop, depending on the implementation.

---

# 7. Very Important: "Virtual" Does NOT Mean "No Path"

A common misunderstanding is:

> "Since it is virtual, packets can take any path."

❌ **That's not the intended idea.**

A virtual circuit establishes a **logical path**.

For example:

```text
A → R1 → R3 → R5 → B
```

After setup, packets belonging to that VC generally follow this logical path.

So:

```text
P1 → R1 → R3 → R5 → B
P2 → R1 → R3 → R5 → B
P3 → R1 → R3 → R5 → B
```

rather than independently selecting arbitrary routes.

---

# 8. Does a Virtual Circuit Reserve Physical Bandwidth?

This is where you need to be careful with the simplified explanation in your professor's PPT.

A virtual circuit establishes a **logical connection**, but it does **not necessarily reserve a dedicated physical link/bandwidth in the same way as traditional circuit switching**.

Think:

### Circuit switching

```text
Physical resources
════════════════════
Reserved for A → B
```

### Virtual circuit

```text
Shared physical infrastructure

A ──┐
B ──┼──── Shared links ────
C ──┘
```

The VC provides a logical path through the shared packet network.

Some VC technologies can provide resource guarantees or reservations, but **VC establishment itself should not automatically be equated with dedicated physical bandwidth**.

This distinction is important for higher-level networking questions.

---

# 9. Why Is VC Useful?

Suppose an organization has:

```text
Branch A ───── Internet/shared fiber ───── Branch B
```

They want:

- a logical connection
- packets to follow an established path
- ordered delivery behavior associated with the connection
- shared physical infrastructure

A virtual circuit can provide the logical connection without requiring a dedicated physical cable between A and B.

---

# 10. Example

Suppose:

```text
London ─ R1 ─ R2 ─ R3 ─ Tokyo
```

A virtual circuit is established:

```text
London
   │
   ↓
  R1
   │
   ↓
  R2
   │
   ↓
  R3
   │
   ↓
 Tokyo
```

Now suppose London sends:

```text
P1
P2
P3
P4
```

The packets belong to the same VC.

Conceptually:

```text
P1 ─┐
P2 ─┤
P3 ─┼──→ established logical path ──→ Tokyo
P4 ─┘
```

The physical links are still part of a shared network.

---

# 11. Comparison of All Three Techniques

This is something you should **definitely know for your exam**.

| Property | Circuit Switching | Datagram Switching | Virtual Circuit |
|---|---|---|---|
| Connection setup | Yes | No | Yes |
| Connection type | Dedicated circuit | Connectionless | Logical connection |
| Physical resource reservation | Typically yes | No | Not inherently |
| Packet concept | Continuous circuit/data stream | Independent packets | Packets belonging to a VC |
| Path | Established | Can vary | Established logical path |
| Identifier | Circuit/connection state | Destination address | VCI/label |
| Forwarding | Based on circuit | Destination lookup | VC/label lookup |
| Bursty traffic | Less efficient | Efficient | Generally can share resources efficiently |
| Failure handling | Circuit may need re-establishment | Packets can potentially be rerouted | VC may need re-establishment/rerouting |
| Typical example | Traditional telephone networks | IP | ATM, Frame Relay |

---

# 12. The Key Difference

Here's the simplest way to remember all three:

### Circuit Switching

> **"Reserve a circuit first."**

```text
A ═════════════ B
```

### Datagram Switching

> **"Send packets independently."**

```text
P1 → Path A
P2 → Path B
P3 → Path A
```

### Virtual Circuit

> **"Establish a logical path first, then send packets through it."**

```text
A ──→ R1 ──→ R2 ──→ B
     Logical VC
```

---

# 13. One Important Exam Trap

Suppose the question says:

> "Packets are guaranteed to follow the same path because a connection was established."

You should **not immediately answer circuit switching**.

It could be:

- Circuit switching
- Virtual circuit switching

You need to inspect the rest of the question.

If it says:

> **Dedicated physical resources are reserved**

→ Circuit switching.

If it says:

> **Logical connection over shared packet-switched infrastructure**

→ Virtual circuit.

---

# 14. Another Exam Trap

### Statement:

> "Virtual circuit switching is the same as circuit switching."

❌ **False.**

The major distinction is:

```text
Circuit:
Dedicated circuit/resources

Virtual Circuit:
Logical path over packet-switched/shared infrastructure
```

A VC can provide connection-oriented behavior without necessarily dedicating physical resources in the same manner as circuit switching.

---

# 15. Your Professor's Bank Example

Your PPT says:

> A bank wants predictable sequential communication but needs to use existing shared fiber infrastructure.

The intended answer is:

$$
\boxed{\text{Virtual Circuit Switching}}
$$

Why?

Because the bank can establish a **logical path** while still using the shared underlying network.

Conceptually:

```text
Branch A
   │
   ↓
Shared network
   │
   ↓
Branch B

Logical VC:
A ═══════════ B
```

The physical network remains shared.

---

# 16. One More Important Point: Ordered Delivery

Because packets belonging to a VC follow the same logical path, they generally arrive in the same order in which they were sent, assuming no unusual lower-layer reordering behavior.

For example:

```text
Sender:

P1 → P2 → P3 → P4

VC path:

P1 ─────────→
P2 ─────────→
P3 ─────────→
P4 ─────────→

Receiver:

P1 → P2 → P3 → P4
```

This contrasts with datagram switching, where different paths can produce different delays:

```text
P1 ────────→ 20 ms
P2 ────→     10 ms
P3 ───────→  15 ms

Arrival:
P2 → P3 → P1
```

So virtual circuits make **in-order delivery easier to maintain**, although don't interpret VC establishment alone as a universal guarantee of every end-to-end service property.

---

# 🧠 Final Mental Model

Imagine three different postal systems.

### Datagram

Every parcel says:

> "Deliver this to Mumbai."

Every parcel is routed independently.

```text
Parcel 1 → Route A
Parcel 2 → Route B
Parcel 3 → Route A
```

### Circuit

Before sending anything:

> "Reserve this road from Delhi to Mumbai for our communication."

```text
Delhi ═════════════ Mumbai
```

### Virtual Circuit

Before sending:

> "Create a logical route from Delhi to Mumbai."

Then all parcels carry something like:

```text
VCI = 42
```

and the network knows:

```text
VCI 42 → this established logical path
```

while the underlying roads can still be shared with other traffic.

---

with a **logical path over shared packet-switched infrastructure**?

Absolutely. Here are the **10 answers** in an exam-oriented way.

# CS 606T — Virtual Circuit Switching: Answers

### Q1. What is a virtual circuit?

**Answer:**

A **virtual circuit (VC)** is a **logical connection** established between a source and destination through a packet-switched network before data transmission begins.

Once established, packets belonging to that connection are forwarded along the established logical path.

```text
A → R1 → R2 → R3 → B
    ═════════════
     Logical VC
```

**Exam definition:**

> Virtual circuit switching is a connection-oriented packet-switching technique in which a logical path is established between the source and destination before data transmission.

---

### Q2. What are the three phases of virtual circuit switching?

**Answer:**

There are three phases:

1. **VC Setup**
2. **Data Transfer**
3. **VC Teardown**

```text
Setup
  ↓
Data Transfer
  ↓
Teardown
```

During setup, the network establishes the logical path and VC state.

During data transfer, packets use the established VC.

During teardown, the VC state is removed.

---

### Q3. Why is a virtual circuit called "virtual"?

**Answer:**

It is called **virtual** because the connection is a **logical path**, not necessarily a dedicated physical circuit.

The underlying physical links can be shared by multiple users.

```text
        Shared physical network
A ───────┐
B ───────┼──────── Network ────────
C ───────┘

Logical VC:
A ═════════════════════════ B
```

Therefore:

> **Virtual = logically established connection, not necessarily a physically dedicated connection.**

---

### Q4. What is a VCI?

**Answer:**

**VCI (Virtual Circuit Identifier)** is an identifier used to identify a particular virtual circuit.

After the VC is established, packets can carry the VCI instead of requiring the complete destination address to make the forwarding decision.

Example:

```text
+----------------+
| VCI = 17       |
+----------------+
| Data           |
+----------------+
```

A router can use:

$$
VCI=17
$$

to determine the appropriate outgoing interface and next VC identifier.

---

### Q5. Can the VCI remain different on different links?

**Answer:**

**Yes.**

A VC identifier is typically **local to a link/interface**, so a router can translate an incoming VCI into a different outgoing VCI.

Example:

```text
A ── R1 ── R2 ── B

A → R1 : VCI 10
R1 → R2: VCI 25
R2 → B : VCI 7
```

At R1:

```text
Incoming VCI 10
       ↓
      R1
       ↓
Outgoing VCI 25
```

At R2:

```text
Incoming VCI 25
       ↓
      R2
       ↓
Outgoing VCI 7
```

A forwarding table might contain:

| Incoming Interface | Incoming VCI | Outgoing Interface | Outgoing VCI |
|---|---:|---|---:|
| 1 | 10 | 2 | 25 |
| 2 | 25 | 3 | 7 |

**Important exam point:**

> VCI values need not be globally unique; they can have **local significance**.

---

### Q6. Does establishing a virtual circuit necessarily mean that dedicated physical bandwidth is reserved?

**Answer:**

**No.**

Establishing a virtual circuit primarily establishes a **logical path and forwarding state**.

The underlying physical network can still be shared by multiple connections.

```text
Physical network:
────────────────────────────
 VC1 ──┐
 VC2 ──┼── Shared links
 VC3 ──┘
────────────────────────────
```

This is an important difference from traditional circuit switching, where resources are typically reserved for the duration of the circuit.

**Exam point:**

> A VC provides a logical connection; it should not automatically be interpreted as a dedicated physical circuit.

---

### Q7. In datagram switching, packets contain destination information. What does a VC packet use for forwarding after setup?

**Answer:**

After VC setup, the packet generally uses a **Virtual Circuit Identifier (VCI)** or an equivalent connection/label identifier.

For example:

```text
Packet:

+----------------+
| VCI = 25       |
+----------------+
| Data           |
+----------------+
```

The router looks up the VCI in its VC forwarding table:

```text
VCI 25
  ↓
Outgoing interface + outgoing VCI
```

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{\text{Datagram → Destination information}}
$$

$$
\boxed{\text{Virtual Circuit → VCI/connection identifier}}
$$

---

### Q8. Which technique is represented by Setup → Data → Teardown with a dedicated physical circuit?

**Answer:**

$$
\boxed{\text{Circuit Switching}}
$$

Circuit switching establishes a dedicated circuit before data transfer.

```text
Setup
  ↓
════════════════
Dedicated path
════════════════
  ↓
Data Transfer
  ↓
Teardown
```

**Key clue:**

> **Dedicated physical resources/circuit**

→ Circuit Switching.

---

### Q9. Which technique is represented by Setup → Data → Teardown with a logical path over shared packet-switched infrastructure?

**Answer:**

$$
\boxed{\text{Virtual Circuit Switching}}
$$

The key distinction is:

```text
Circuit switching:
Dedicated physical circuit

Virtual circuit:
Logical connection over shared packet network
```

**Key clue:**

> **Logical path + shared packet-switched infrastructure**

→ Virtual Circuit Switching.

---

### Q10. A company wants connection-oriented communication but does not want to dedicate a physical link between every pair of branches. Which technique does this description point toward, and why?

**Answer:**

$$
\boxed{\text{Virtual Circuit Switching}}
$$

A virtual circuit provides a **connection-oriented logical path** while allowing the underlying physical network to be shared by multiple users/connections.

Example:

```text
Branch A ──┐
           │
           ↓
      Shared Network
           │
           ↓
Branch B ──┘

Logical VC:
A ═════════════ B
```

Therefore, the company can obtain a logical connection without requiring a dedicated physical link between every pair of branches.

---

# 🔥 The Most Important Comparison

You should now be able to answer this table without memorizing individual definitions:

| Feature | Datagram | Circuit | Virtual Circuit |
|---|---|---|---|
| Connection setup | ❌ No | ✅ Yes | ✅ Yes |
| Connection-oriented | ❌ | ✅ | ✅ |
| Logical path | Not fixed | Dedicated circuit | ✅ Established VC |
| Physical resources dedicated | ❌ | Typically ✅ | Not inherently |
| Forwarding information | Destination | Circuit state | VCI/label |
| Packets independent? | ✅ Yes | Not packet-switched in the same sense | Belong to same VC |
| Packets can use different paths? | ✅ Possible | ❌ | Generally ❌ during VC lifetime |
| Shared infrastructure | ✅ | Limited by reservation | ✅ |
| Good for bursty traffic | ✅ | ❌ Generally | ✅ Generally |

---

## 🧠 One-line memory trick

Remember:

> **Datagram = "Where are you going?"**

> **Circuit = "I reserved this path for you."**

> **Virtual Circuit = "I established a logical path for you."**

And the three keywords:

$$
\boxed{\text{Datagram → Destination}}
$$

$$
\boxed{\text{Circuit → Dedicated}}
$$

$$
\boxed{\text{Virtual Circuit → VCI + Logical Path}}
$$

At this point, you've covered the **three core switching techniques** in your professor's Chapter 1 material. The next useful step is to practice **mixed/tricky exam questions and numerical problems combining transmission delay, propagation delay, setup time, multiple packets, and multiple hops**, because that's where the three concepts start getting mixed together.

# CS 606T — Chapter 1
## Part 4: Comparing Switching Techniques + Delay Analysis

We've now learned:

1. **Internetworking**
2. **Switches and Routers**
3. **Datagram / Packet Switching**
4. **Circuit Switching**
5. **Virtual Circuit Switching**

Before moving to later chapters, there's an important topic hidden across your professor's slides and numerical questions:

> **How does switching affect delay, path selection, resource utilization, and packet delivery?**

This is where you should start thinking like a networking engineer rather than memorizing definitions.

---

# 1. The Four Main Types of Network Delay

When a packet travels through a network, its total delay can consist of several components.

```text
Source
  │
  ├── Transmission Delay
  │
  ├── Propagation Delay
  │
  ├── Queuing Delay
  │
  └── Processing Delay
  │
Destination
```

The four important delays are:

$$
\boxed{
D_{total}
=
D_{processing}
+
D_{queuing}
+
D_{transmission}
+
D_{propagation}
}
$$

Let's understand each one.

---

# 2. Transmission Delay

Transmission delay is:

> **The time required to push all bits of a packet onto the link.**

Formula:

$$
\boxed{D_{trans}=\frac{L}{R}}
$$

where:

- $L$ = packet size in bits
- $R$ = link transmission rate in bits/sec

### Example

Packet:

$$
L=8000\text{ bits}
$$

Link:

$$
R=2Mbps
$$

Then:

$$
D_{trans}
=
\frac{8000}{2\times10^6}
$$

$$
=0.004s
$$

$$
\boxed{4ms}
$$

---

# 3. Propagation Delay

Propagation delay is:

> **The time required for the signal to travel from one end of the link to the other.**

Formula:

$$
\boxed{D_{prop}=\frac{d}{v}}
$$

where:

- $d$ = physical distance
- $v$ = propagation speed

Example:

$$
d=1000km
$$

$$
v=200000km/s
$$

Then:

$$
D_{prop}
=
\frac{1000}{200000}
$$

$$
=0.005s
$$

$$
\boxed{5ms}
$$

---

# 4. Transmission vs Propagation

This distinction is extremely important.

Imagine:

```text
Sender                                      Receiver
  A=============================================B
                  1000 km
```

### Transmission delay asks:

> How long does it take to **put the entire packet onto the link?**

Depends on:

$$
\boxed{\text{Packet size + bandwidth}}
$$

### Propagation delay asks:

> Once a bit has entered the link, how long does it take to **travel to the other end?**

Depends on:

$$
\boxed{\text{Distance + propagation speed}}
$$

---

## Example

Suppose:

$$
L=10000\text{ bits}
$$

$$
R=1Mbps
$$

$$
d=1000km
$$

$$
v=200000km/s
$$

Transmission:

$$
D_{trans}
=
\frac{10000}{10^6}
=
10ms
$$

Propagation:

$$
D_{prop}
=
\frac{1000}{200000}
=
5ms
$$

Therefore:

$$
D_{total}=10+5=15ms
$$

assuming no processing or queuing delay.

---

# 5. Queuing Delay

Now imagine multiple packets arrive at a router.

```text
P1 ──┐
P2 ──┤
P3 ──┤──→ Router ──→ Output link
P4 ──┘
```

The outgoing link cannot necessarily transmit all packets simultaneously.

So packets wait in a buffer:

```text
Router buffer:

┌────┐
│ P4 │
├────┤
│ P3 │
├────┤
│ P2 │
├────┤
│ P1 │ → transmission
└────┘
```

The time a packet spends waiting is:

$$
\boxed{\text{Queuing Delay}}
$$

Unlike transmission and propagation delay, queuing delay can be **highly variable**.

It depends on:

- traffic load
- number of packets waiting
- transmission rate
- congestion

---

# 6. Processing Delay

Before forwarding a packet, a router needs to process it.

Conceptually:

```text
Packet arrives
     ↓
Check header
     ↓
Check destination
     ↓
Lookup forwarding table
     ↓
Choose output
     ↓
Forward
```

The time spent performing these operations is:

$$
\boxed{\text{Processing Delay}}
$$

It can include:

- examining packet headers
- checking errors
- looking up forwarding information
- determining outgoing interface

---

# 7. Total Delay

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{
D_{total}
=
D_{proc}
+
D_{queue}
+
D_{trans}
+
D_{prop}
}
$$

This formula is extremely important.

---

# 8. Multiple Hops

Now consider:

```text
A → R1 → R2 → B
```

There are **3 links/hops**:

```text
A → R1
R1 → R2
R2 → B
```

Suppose every link has:

$$
R=2Mbps
$$

and every packet is:

$$
L=2000bits
$$

Transmission delay per link:

$$
D_{trans}
=
\frac{2000}{2\times10^6}
$$

$$
=1ms
$$

For a **single packet**, if propagation, processing, and queuing delays are ignored:

```text
A → R1       1 ms
R1 → R2      1 ms
R2 → B       1 ms
```

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{3ms}
$$

for one packet.

---

# 9. Multiple Packets — Pipeline Effect

Now things become more interesting.

Suppose we have:

$$
P=3
$$

packets:

```text
P1
P2
P3
```

and:

$$
N=3
$$

hops.

Each packet requires:

$$
1ms
$$

transmission time per link.

### Timeline

```text
Time →

Link 1:
P1 | P2 | P3
   1    2    3 ms

Link 2:
   P1 | P2 | P3
   2    3    4 ms

Link 3:
       P1 | P2 | P3
       3    4    5 ms
```

Therefore the final packet arrives at:

$$
\boxed{5ms}
$$

---

# 10. General Formula

If:

- $P$ = number of packets
- $N$ = number of hops
- $T$ = transmission time per packet per link

and propagation/queuing/processing delays are ignored:

$$
\boxed{
D=(P+N-1)T
}
$$

Since:

$$
T=\frac{L}{R}
$$

we get:

$$
\boxed{
D=(P+N-1)\frac{L}{R}
}
$$

This is exactly the formula used in your professor's packet-switching numerical.

---

# 11. Why Isn't It $P\times N\times T$?

This is a common exam trap.

Someone might calculate:

$$
3\times3\times1=9ms
$$

But that's incorrect under the pipeline model.

Why?

Because while P1 is traveling through hop 2, P2 can already be transmitted over hop 1.

```text
Time →

Hop 1: P1 | P2 | P3
Hop 2:    P1 | P2 | P3
Hop 3:        P1 | P2 | P3
```

Multiple packets are **pipelined** through the network.

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{5ms}
$$

not 9 ms.

---

# 12. Important Comparison

### One packet

$$
D=N\times T
$$

### Multiple packets

$$
D=(P+N-1)T
$$

assuming:

- equal link transmission rates
- equal packet sizes
- store-and-forward
- negligible propagation
- negligible processing
- negligible queuing

---

# 13. Numerical Example

Suppose:

- 5 packets
- 4 hops
- each packet = 1000 bits
- link rate = 1 Mbps

Then:

$$
T=\frac{1000}{10^6}
$$

$$
T=1ms
$$

Total:

$$
D=(5+4-1)(1ms)
$$

$$
D=8ms
$$

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{8ms}
$$

---

# 14. What Happens If Propagation Delay Is Included?

Now suppose every link additionally has:

$$
D_{prop}=2ms
$$

For the previous example:

$$
P=5
$$

$$
N=4
$$

$$
D_{trans}=1ms
$$

A simplified pipeline model gives:

$$
D=(P+N-1)D_{trans}+N D_{prop}
$$

Therefore:

$$
D=(5+4-1)(1)+4(2)
$$

$$
=8+8
$$

$$
\boxed{16ms}
$$

Again, the exact formula depends on the assumptions of the problem, but this is the standard simplified model for identical links.

---

# 15. Circuit Switching vs Packet Switching: Delay Perspective

### Circuit switching

There is an initial:

$$
\boxed{Setup\ Delay}
$$

Then data transmission occurs over the established circuit.

Conceptually:

```text
Setup → Data → Teardown
```

### Datagram switching

No setup:

```text
Packet → Forward → Packet → Forward
```

But packets can experience:

- processing delay
- queuing delay
- transmission delay
- propagation delay

So the tradeoff is roughly:

```text
Circuit:
Initial setup
     ↓
More predictable communication

Packet:
No setup
     ↓
Potentially variable delay
```

---

# 16. Important Exam Concept: Bandwidth vs Delay

Suppose bandwidth increases:

$$
10Mbps \rightarrow 100Mbps
$$

What happens?

Transmission delay:

$$
D_{trans}=\frac{L}{R}
$$

decreases.

But propagation delay:

$$
D_{prop}=\frac{d}{v}
$$

doesn't change merely because bandwidth increased.

Therefore:

> **Increasing bandwidth reduces transmission delay but does not inherently reduce propagation delay.**

---

# 17. Another Important Concept: Distance vs Bandwidth

Suppose distance doubles:

$$
d\rightarrow2d
$$

Then:

$$
D_{prop}\rightarrow2D_{prop}
$$

But transmission delay remains unchanged if packet size and bandwidth remain unchanged.

---

# 18. Summary of Chapter 1 So Far

You now have the complete conceptual chain:

```text
Problem:
Networks aren't directly connected
            ↓
     Internetworking
            ↓
    Switches + Routers
            ↓
    Network Switching
            ↓
     ┌──────┴──────┐
     ↓             ↓
Connectionless  Connection-oriented
     ↓             ↓
 Datagram      ┌───┴────┐
 Switching     ↓        ↓
            Circuit   Virtual
            Switching Circuit
```

And for delay:

```text
Packet travels
     │
     ├── Processing
     │
     ├── Queuing
     │
     ├── Transmission
     │
     └── Propagation
```

with:

$$
\boxed{D_{total}=D_{proc}+D_{queue}+D_{trans}+D_{prop}}
$$

and:

$$
\boxed{D_{trans}=\frac{L}{R}}
$$

$$
\boxed{D_{prop}=\frac{d}{v}}
$$

---

## 🧪 Practice Questions

Try these next:

### Q1
A 4000-bit packet is transmitted over a 4 Mbps link. Find transmission delay.

### Q2
A signal travels 1500 km at $2\times10^5$ km/s. Find propagation delay.

### Q3
A packet travels through 5 hops. Each link has transmission delay 2 ms. Ignore all other delays. Find total delay for one packet.

### Q4
There are 4 packets and 5 hops. Each packet takes 2 ms to transmit on each link. Find the time for the entire message to arrive.

### Q5
Why isn't the answer to Q4:

$$
4\times5\times2=40ms?
$$

### Q6 — Conceptual
If bandwidth increases from 10 Mbps to 100 Mbps, which delay changes directly?

### Q7 — Conceptual
If the physical distance doubles while bandwidth remains constant, which delay changes?

### Q8 — Tricky
A router has a packet waiting in its output buffer because another packet is currently being transmitted. Which delay is responsible for this waiting time?

### Q9 — Tricky
A 10 MB file is sent over a 100 Mbps link. If the distance is doubled but bandwidth remains unchanged, does the transmission time change?

### Q10 — Advanced
Explain why packet switching can have lower resource wastage for bursty traffic but potentially higher and more variable delay than circuit switching.

After these, we can move into the **more advanced part of switching: switch architectures, buffering strategies, input/output queuing, and scheduling**, which connects directly to the later CS 606T syllabus.

## Response:
9/19/2026, 5:45:42 PM · gpt-5-6

Sure. Here are the **10 answers**, with the calculations and reasoning.

# CS 606T — Delay Analysis: Answers

### Q1. A 4000-bit packet is transmitted over a 4 Mbps link. Find transmission delay.

Formula:

$$
D_{trans}=\frac{L}{R}
$$

Given:

$$
L=4000\ bits
$$

$$
R=4Mbps=4\times10^6\ bits/s
$$

Therefore:

$$
D_{trans}
=
\frac{4000}{4\times10^6}
$$

$$
=0.001s
$$

$$
\boxed{D_{trans}=1ms}
$$

---

### Q2. A signal travels 1500 km at $2\times10^5$ km/s. Find propagation delay.

Formula:

$$
D_{prop}=\frac{d}{v}
$$

Given:

$$
d=1500km
$$

$$
v=2\times10^5km/s
$$

Therefore:

$$
D_{prop}
=
\frac{1500}{200000}
$$

$$
=0.0075s
$$

Converting to milliseconds:

$$
0.0075\times1000=7.5ms
$$

$$
\boxed{D_{prop}=7.5ms}
$$

---

### Q3. A packet travels through 5 hops. Each link has transmission delay 2 ms. Ignore all other delays. Find total delay for one packet.

There are 5 links/hops.

Each hop requires:

$$
2ms
$$

Therefore:

$$
D=5\times2
$$

$$
\boxed{D=10ms}
$$

### Answer: **10 ms**

---

### Q4. There are 4 packets and 5 hops. Each packet takes 2 ms to transmit on each link. Find the time for the entire message to arrive.

Use the pipeline formula:

$$
D=(P+N-1)T
$$

Given:

$$
P=4
$$

$$
N=5
$$

$$
T=2ms
$$

Therefore:

$$
D=(4+5-1)(2)
$$

$$
=8\times2
$$

$$
\boxed{D=16ms}
$$

### Answer: **16 ms**

---

### Q5. Why isn't the answer to Q4 $4\times5\times2=40ms$?

**Answer:**

Because the packets can be **pipelined** through the network.

The next packet can begin transmission on an earlier link while the previous packet is being transmitted on a later link.

For example:

```text
Time →

Hop 1: P1 | P2 | P3 | P4
Hop 2:    P1 | P2 | P3 | P4
Hop 3:        P1 | P2 | P3 | P4
Hop 4:            P1 | P2 | P3 | P4
Hop 5:                P1 | P2 | P3 | P4
```

The transmissions overlap.

Therefore, we don't add the transmission time of every packet on every hop sequentially.

Instead:

$$
D=(P+N-1)T
$$

$$
=(4+5-1)(2)
$$

$$
=\boxed{16ms}
$$

---

### Q6. If bandwidth increases from 10 Mbps to 100 Mbps, which delay changes directly?

**Answer:**

$$
\boxed{\text{Transmission delay}}
$$

Because:

$$
D_{trans}=\frac{L}{R}
$$

When $R$ increases, transmission delay decreases.

For example, for the same packet:

```text
10 Mbps → larger transmission delay
100 Mbps → smaller transmission delay
```

However:

$$
D_{prop}=\frac{d}{v}
$$

does not directly depend on bandwidth.

### Therefore:

> Increasing bandwidth directly reduces transmission delay, not propagation delay.

---

### Q7. If physical distance doubles while bandwidth remains constant, which delay changes?

**Answer:**

$$
\boxed{\text{Propagation delay}}
$$

Because:

$$
D_{prop}=\frac{d}{v}
$$

If:

$$
d\rightarrow2d
$$

then:

$$
D_{prop}\rightarrow2D_{prop}
$$

Transmission delay:

$$
D_{trans}=\frac{L}{R}
$$

remains unchanged because packet size and bandwidth haven't changed.

### Therefore:

> **Propagation delay doubles; transmission delay remains unchanged.**

---

### Q8. A router has a packet waiting in its output buffer because another packet is currently being transmitted. Which delay is responsible?

**Answer:**

$$
\boxed{\text{Queuing delay}}
$$

The packet has already arrived at the router but cannot immediately use the outgoing link.

It must wait:

```text id="gmb2y4"
Packet arrives
      ↓
   [Buffer]
      ↓
   WAITING
      ↓
Outgoing link becomes free
      ↓
Transmission
```

The time spent waiting in the buffer is **queuing delay**.

---

### Q9. A 10 MB file is sent over a 100 Mbps link. If the distance is doubled but bandwidth remains unchanged, does transmission time change?

**Answer:**

$$
\boxed{\text{No}}
$$

Transmission delay is:

$$
D_{trans}=\frac{L}{R}
$$

Neither:

- $L$ = file size
- $R$ = bandwidth

has changed.

Therefore transmission time remains the same.

However, propagation delay **does increase** because:

$$
D_{prop}=\frac{d}{v}
$$

and distance doubled.

### Important distinction:

> **Distance affects propagation delay, not transmission delay.**

---

### Q10. Why can packet switching have lower resource wastage for bursty traffic but potentially higher and more variable delay than circuit switching?

**Answer:**

### Packet switching

Packet switching uses **shared network resources**.

Suppose:

```text id="m7tq7w"
User A → ███──────████
User B → ──████────────
User C → █────████────
```

When User A is idle, other users can use the network capacity.

Therefore, packet switching is efficient for **bursty traffic**.

However, because multiple users share resources, packets may have to wait in queues:

```text id="4u8q8x"
P1 ──┐
P2 ──┤
P3 ──┤ → Router → Output
P4 ──┘
       ↑
    Queue
```

This creates **queuing delay**, and the amount of delay can vary depending on network load.

---

### Circuit switching

Circuit switching establishes a circuit and reserves resources:

```text id="y9e5n0"
A ═══════════════ B
    Reserved
    resources
```

This can provide more predictable performance after setup.

However, if the user becomes idle:

```text id="xj20hx"
Data:      ███───────████
Resources: ██████████████
```

the reserved resources may remain unused.

Therefore:

> **Circuit switching can waste resources during idle periods, while packet switching shares resources more efficiently but can experience variable queuing delay.**

---

# ⭐ Final Revision Sheet

You should now know these extremely well:

| Delay | Formula | Depends on |
|---|---|---|
| **Processing** | — | Router processing |
| **Queuing** | — | Network traffic/congestion |
| **Transmission** | $L/R$ | Packet size + bandwidth |
| **Propagation** | $d/v$ | Distance + propagation speed |

Total:

$$
\boxed{
D_{total}
=
D_{processing}
+
D_{queuing}
+
D_{transmission}
+
D_{propagation}
}
$$

For $P$ packets across $N$ identical hops, under the simplified pipeline assumptions:

$$
\boxed{
D=(P+N-1)\frac{L}{R}
}
$$

### The 4 most important distinctions:

$$
\boxed{\text{More data → Transmission delay increases}}
$$

$$
\boxed{\text{More distance → Propagation delay increases}}
$$

$$
\boxed{\text{More congestion → Queuing delay increases}}
$$

$$
\boxed{\text{More router processing → Processing delay increases}}
$$

And the big switching distinction:

$$
\boxed{\text{Circuit → dedicated resources}}
$$

$$
\boxed{\text{Datagram → independent packets}}
$$

$$
\boxed{\text{Virtual Circuit → established logical path}}
$$

No problem. This PPT is **important** because it adds the detailed implementation of **Virtual Circuit Switching**, and then introduces **MPLS and VPNs**. So we should add this to Chapter 1 rather than skipping it.
