# CS 606T — Chapter 1
## Part 5: Virtual Circuit Switching — Detailed Working

Your professor's material gives us a more precise model than the earlier introduction.

---

# 1. Why Virtual Circuit Switching?

Your professor summarizes the basic trade-off as:

```text
Circuit Switching
      │
      ├── Reliable / predictable
      │
      └── Inflexible resource allocation

Packet Switching
      │
      ├── Flexible resource allocation
      │
      └── Less predictable delivery

↓

Virtual Circuit Switching
              │
      ┌───────┴────────┐
      ↓                ↓
Circuit principles   Packet principles
      │                │
Established path     Buffers
Connection setup     Queuing
      │                │
      └───────┬────────┘
              ↓
       Virtual Circuit
```

The key idea is:

> **VC switching establishes a path like circuit switching, but packets are still buffered and forwarded through a shared packet-switched infrastructure.**

---

# 2. Three Important Properties

Your professor specifically mentions:

### 1. Circuit is established before data transfer

```text
Setup
  ↓
VC established
  ↓
Data transfer
```

### 2. Buffers exist at switches

Packets can wait:

```text
       Router
         │
      ┌──┴──┐
      │ P3  │
      │ P2  │ ← Buffer
      │ P1  │
      └─────┘
         │
         ↓
      Output
```

Therefore, **queuing delay can exist**.

### 3. Resource allocation is flexible

Unlike traditional circuit switching, the underlying network resources can be shared between multiple VCs.

---

# 3. Connection Setup Phase

This is the most important new part of your PPT.

During VC setup, the network creates a:

> **VC Table**

at each switch along the selected path.

Consider:

```text
A ── S1 ── S2 ── S3 ── B
```

Suppose the selected VC path is:

```text
A → S1 → S2 → S3 → B
```

Each switch maintains information about the connection.

---

# 4. What is stored in a VC Table?

Your professor gives four important fields:

| Field | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Incoming Interface | Interface through which packet arrives |
| Incoming VCI | VC identifier carried by incoming packet |
| Outgoing Interface | Interface through which packet should leave |
| Outgoing VCI | VCI that will be used on outgoing link |

Conceptually:

```text id="k9x3vy"
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                VC TABLE                     │
├────────────┬────────────┬───────────┬───────┤
│ Incoming   │ Incoming   │ Outgoing  │Outgoing│
│ Interface  │ VCI        │ Interface │ VCI    │
├────────────┼────────────┼───────────┼───────┤
│     1      │    10      │     2     │   25   │
└────────────┴────────────┴───────────┴───────┘
```

This table tells the switch:

> "If a packet comes through interface 1 with VCI 10, send it through interface 2 with VCI 25."

---

# 5. VCI — Virtual Circuit Identifier

A VCI identifies a particular connection **locally at a switch/link**.

This is extremely important.

Suppose:

```text id="n0plju"
A ── S1 ── S2 ── B
```

The VC might use:

```text
A → S1 : VCI 10
S1 → S2: VCI 25
S2 → B : VCI 7
```

Notice:

$$
10\neq25\neq7
$$

That's completely valid.

Why?

Because VCIs are **local in scope**.

---

# 6. What Does "Local Scope" Mean?

Suppose S1 has:

```text
VCI = 10
```

It does **not** mean the entire network must understand "VCI 10".

Instead:

```text id="kfx4za"
At S1:
VCI 10 → connection X

At S2:
VCI 25 → connection X

At S3:
VCI 7 → connection X
```

Each switch can assign its own VCI.

Therefore:

> **VCI values only need to be unique within the relevant local scope, not globally across the entire network.**

This is an important exam concept.

---

# 7. How Is the VCI Chosen?

During setup:

### Step 1

First, a path is selected.

```text id="t10brn"
A → S1 → S2 → S3 → B
```

### Step 2

At each link/switch, a VCI is selected that is not already being used for another connection in that local context.

For example:

```text id="d8cz1u"
S1:

VCI 5  → already used
VCI 6  → already used
VCI 7  → FREE ✓
```

So S1 may assign VCI 7.

Another switch may choose a completely different number.

---

# 8. The Complete VC Setup Example

Let's construct one.

```text id="x3ld5d"
A ── S1 ── S2 ── S3 ── B
```

Suppose the network chooses this path.

During setup, assume the following VCIs are assigned:

```text id="a4w6x2"
A → S1       VCI 12
S1 → S2      VCI 34
S2 → S3      VCI 8
S3 → B       VCI 21
```

The tables could look conceptually like:

### S1

| Incoming Interface | Incoming VCI | Outgoing Interface | Outgoing VCI |
|---|---:|---|---:|
| A-side | 12 | S2-side | 34 |

### S2

| Incoming Interface | Incoming VCI | Outgoing Interface | Outgoing VCI |
|---|---:|---|---:|
| S1-side | 34 | S3-side | 8 |

### S3

| Incoming Interface | Incoming VCI | Outgoing Interface | Outgoing VCI |
|---|---:|---|---:|
| S2-side | 8 | B-side | 21 |

---

# 9. Data Transfer Phase

Now the setup is complete.

A sends a packet.

Suppose:

```text id="h2dyc8"
Packet:

VCI = 12
Data = ...
```

The packet arrives at S1.

S1 looks at:

```text
Incoming interface = A-side
Incoming VCI = 12
```

It searches the VC table:

```text id="9m2d52"
(A-side, 12)
       ↓
     S1 VC table
       ↓
(S2-side, 34)
```

So S1 changes the packet's VCI:

```text
VCI 12 → VCI 34
```

and sends it through the S2 interface.

---

# 10. At S2

S2 receives:

```text
Incoming interface = S1-side
VCI = 34
```

It looks up:

```text id="p1g3sn"
(S1-side, 34)
        ↓
      S2 table
        ↓
(S3-side, 8)
```

So:

```text
VCI 34 → VCI 8
```

and forwards the packet.

---

# 11. At S3

S3 receives:

```text
Incoming interface = S2-side
VCI = 8
```

Lookup:

```text id="x2k7vy"
(S2-side, 8)
        ↓
      S3 table
        ↓
(B-side, 21)
```

Therefore:

```text
VCI 8 → VCI 21
```

and the packet reaches B.

---

# 12. The Whole Process

This is worth memorizing:

```text id="5x8pml"
              VC SETUP
                  │
                  ↓
        Select path A → B
                  │
                  ↓
        Assign VCIs locally
                  │
                  ↓
       Build VC tables
                  │
                  ↓
          DATA TRANSFER
                  │
                  ↓
       Packet arrives at switch
                  │
                  ↓
         Read incoming VCI
                  │
                  ↓
          Lookup VC table
                  │
                  ↓
     Find outgoing interface + VCI
                  │
                  ↓
       Replace/update VCI
                  │
                  ↓
       Forward packet
                  │
                  ↓
          Repeat at every switch
```

This is probably the **most important technical diagram from this PPT**.

---

# 13. Why Is VC Lookup Faster Than Packet Switching?

Your professor states:

> **Lookup time is better than packet switching.**

Why?

In ordinary IP forwarding, a router may need to perform a **longest-prefix match**.

For example:

```text
Destination IP:
192.168.10.25
```

The router needs to determine which prefix matches.

Potential entries:

```text
192.168.0.0/16
192.168.10.0/24
192.168.10.16/28
...
```

The router needs the **longest matching prefix**.

---

## VC Switching

VC forwarding can use an **exact match**:

```text
Incoming interface = 2
VCI = 17

        ↓

Exact table entry
        ↓

Outgoing interface = 4
Outgoing VCI = 32
```

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{\text{VC → Exact matching}}
$$

rather than:

$$
\boxed{\text{IP → Longest Prefix Matching}}
$$

This can simplify forwarding.

---

# 14. Why Are VC Packet Headers "Lightweight"?

A datagram packet generally carries a full destination address.

For example, conceptually:

```text
Destination IP = 192.168.10.25
```

A VC packet can carry something much smaller:

```text
VCI = 17
```

Therefore, the packet header can be relatively small.

Your professor calls this:

> **Light packet header and lightweight packets**

---

# 15. Fixed Routes

Your professor states:

> **Routes are fixed during the circuit setup process.**

Suppose:

```text id="lj2s0a"
A → S1 → S2 → S4 → B
```

Once the VC is established, packets belonging to that VC use that logical path.

If the route becomes unavailable:

```text id="d5x1sp"
A → S1 → S2 ✕ S4 → B
```

the VC may need to be re-established or otherwise handled by the network.

This leads directly to another point from your PPT:

> **VC switching has to recover from connection failures.**

---

# 16. The RTT Setup Cost

This is an important point that we didn't emphasize enough earlier.

Your professor says:

> **Delay of 1 RTT before sending actual data**

### RTT = Round Trip Time

Suppose:

```text id="2q2d6h"
A ─────────────→ B
     Setup

A ←───────────── B
     Reply
```

A sends the connection setup request.

The network/destination responds.

Only then can A begin normal data transmission.

So approximately:

$$
\boxed{T_{setup}\approx1RTT}
$$

under the simplified assumption in the PPT.

---

# 17. Why Is This a Disadvantage?

For a very small amount of data:

```text
Setup:       ██████████
Actual data: ██
```

The setup overhead can be significant.

For a long communication:

```text
Setup:       █
Data:        ███████████████████████████
```

the setup cost becomes relatively small compared with the total communication.

Therefore VC switching is more attractive when the connection is sufficiently long-lived to amortize the setup cost.

---

# 18. VC Switching and Queuing

Unlike traditional circuit switching, VC switches can have buffers.

```text id="b2kl1q"
             Switch
                │
       ┌────────┴────────┐
       │     BUFFER      │
       ├─────────────────┤
       │ P3              │
       │ P2              │
       │ P1              │
       └─────────────────┘
                │
                ↓
             Output
```

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{\text{VC can experience queuing delay}}
$$

This is one reason your professor says:

> **QoS is better than packet switching but less than circuit switching.**

That's a simplified course-level characterization. The exact QoS depends on the particular VC technology and whether resources/QoS mechanisms are configured.

---

# 19. Important VC Characteristics From Your PPT

Let's convert your professor's bullet points into concepts:

| Professor's Point | Meaning |
|---|---|
| 1 RTT setup delay | Setup must complete before data |
| Connection failure recovery | Established VC can break |
| Buffers | Packets may wait |
| Queuing delay | Congestion can cause waiting |
| Lightweight headers | VCI is smaller than full destination information |
| Flexible resource allocation | Physical network can be shared |
| Better QoS than basic packet switching | Established path gives more predictable behavior |
| Less QoS than circuit switching | Resources aren't necessarily dedicated |
| Fixed route | VC follows established path |
| Faster lookup | Exact VCI matching |
| Maximum table entries | Limited by available VCI/interface combinations |

---

# 20. Maximum Number of VC Table Entries

Your professor gives:

> Maximum number of entries = No. of VCIs × No. of links

Suppose a switch has:

$$
4\text{ links}
$$

and each link supports:

$$
100\text{ VCIs}
$$

Then maximum possible combinations are:

$$
4\times100=400
$$

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{400}
$$

This is a simplified upper-bound calculation based on the professor's model.

---

# 21. Exact Matching vs Longest Prefix Matching

This is worth knowing separately.

### IP forwarding

```text
Destination IP
      ↓
Longest Prefix Match
      ↓
Output interface
```

### VC forwarding

```text
Incoming Interface + VCI
          ↓
     Exact Match
          ↓
Outgoing Interface + VCI
```

So:

$$
\boxed{\text{VC → Exact match}}
$$

$$
\boxed{\text{IP → Longest-prefix match}}
$$

---

# 22. Real-World Examples

Your professor gives two examples:

### ATM

**Asynchronous Transfer Mode**

ATM uses virtual circuits and identifiers to forward fixed-size cells.

### Frame Relay

Frame Relay also uses virtual-circuit concepts and identifiers for forwarding.

You don't need to dive deeply into ATM/Frame Relay yet unless your professor later covers them.

---

# Part 6 — MPLS

Now your professor introduces:

> **Multiprotocol Label Switching**

This is a very important connection because MPLS uses concepts that resemble virtual circuits.

---

# 23. What is MPLS?

MPLS stands for:

$$
\boxed{\text{Multiprotocol Label Switching}}
$$

The key idea:

> Instead of forwarding packets solely by repeatedly examining the full destination IP address, MPLS routers can forward packets using relatively short **labels**.

Conceptually:

```text id="s8a0hq"
IP packet
   ↓
MPLS label
   ↓
Label-based forwarding
```

This resembles VC switching.

---

# 24. Why Does MPLS Look Like Virtual Circuit Switching?

Your professor explicitly says:

> MPLS combines properties of virtual circuits with flexibility and robustness of datagrams.

The similarities include:

### 1. Labels have local scope

Just like VCIs.

### 2. Short labels

Instead of repeatedly working with a full destination address.

### 3. Label-based forwarding

Routers inspect the label to decide what to do.

So:

```text id="wz4f5j"
Virtual Circuit:

VCI → lookup → outgoing interface + VCI

MPLS:

Label → lookup → outgoing interface + label
```

---

# 25. MPLS Router Terminology

Two terms from your PPT are important.

### LER

$$
\boxed{\text{Label Edge Router}}
$$

The router at the edge of an MPLS network.

Example:

```text id="b7dr46"
IP Network
    │
    ↓
   LER
    │
    ↓
MPLS Network
```

---

### LSR

$$
\boxed{\text{Label Switch Router}}
$$

Routers inside the MPLS network that forward packets based on labels.

Your professor's example:

```text id="zjaf4m"
R1 → R2 → R3 → R4
```

may have:

```text
R1 = LER
R2 = LSR
R3 = LSR
R4 = LSR
```

depending on the topology and role.

---

# 26. Label Switching

Suppose:

```text id="xq07fd"
R1 → R2 → R3 → R7
```

An incoming packet has:

```text
Label = 20
```

R2 looks at:

```text
Incoming label = 20
```

and performs a lookup:

```text id="3y8o9c"
20 → outgoing interface + label 35
```

Then:

```text
Label 20
   ↓
R2
   ↓
Label 35
```

This process is called:

> **Label swapping**

---

# 27. Label Swapping

This is one of the most important MPLS concepts.

```text id="g6s8b1"
        R1             R2             R3
        │              │              │
Packet  │              │              │
Label 10 ─────────────→│              │
                       │              │
                   Label 10           │
                       │              │
                       └─→ Label 25 ─→│
                                      │
                                      ↓
```

The label changes at each hop.

So:

$$
\boxed{\text{MPLS uses label swapping}}
$$

This is analogous to VCI translation in VC switching.

---

# 28. Explicit Routing

Now we reach another important MPLS feature.

Normal IP routing may choose a path based on routing algorithms.

But suppose the network administrator wants:

```text id="rvz7bw"
R1 → R3 → R6 → R7
```

for traffic from R1 to R7.

And:

```text id="8ahb6b"
R2 → R3 → R4 → R5 → R7
```

for traffic from R2 to R7.

These are **explicit routes**.

MPLS can support this kind of traffic engineering.

---

# 29. Why Is Explicit Routing Useful?

Suppose normal routing produces:

```text id="a3h8lz"
R1 → R2 → R4 → R7
```

But perhaps this route has:

- congestion
- insufficient capacity
- undesirable latency
- policy constraints

An explicitly engineered path might instead be:

```text id="x3kq4a"
R1 → R3 → R6 → R7
```

MPLS can help implement such traffic-engineered paths.

---

# 30. Why Use MPLS?

Your professor gives three major reasons.

### 1. Support IP capabilities on devices

MPLS can provide label-based forwarding within the network.

### 2. Explicit routing

Traffic can be directed along precomputed/engineered paths that differ from ordinary IP routing choices.

### 3. VPN services

MPLS is widely used to support certain provider-based VPN architectures.

---

# 31. MPLS and VPNs

Your PPT says:

> VPNs provide a dedicated path for certain network packets using the concept of tunnels.

The key idea is **tunneling**.

Imagine:

```text id="1uj4cc"
Office A
   │
   ↓
======== Provider Network ========
   │
   ↓
Office B
```

Instead of treating the entire provider network as something the customer needs to understand, traffic can be carried through a logical tunnel.

Conceptually:

```text id="5wx8t7"
A ═════════════════════════ B
       Logical tunnel
```

The underlying physical infrastructure remains shared.

---

# 32. Important Distinction: VPN vs Physical Dedicated Line

A VPN does **not necessarily mean a physically dedicated cable**.

Instead:

> It provides logical isolation/connectivity over shared infrastructure.

This is conceptually similar to the "virtual" idea in virtual circuits.

---

## 🔥 One particularly important exam question

If your professor asks:

> **"Explain how a packet is forwarded in a Virtual Circuit network."**

Your answer should be:

```text
1. A VC is established during setup.
          ↓
2. A path is selected from source to destination.
          ↓
3. Each switch creates a VC-table entry.
          ↓
4. The entry maps:
   (Incoming Interface, Incoming VCI)
          ↓
   (Outgoing Interface, Outgoing VCI)
          ↓
5. During data transfer, the switch reads the incoming VCI.
          ↓
6. It performs an exact lookup in the VC table.
          ↓
7. It replaces the VCI with the outgoing VCI.
          ↓
8. It forwards the packet through the outgoing interface.
          ↓
9. The process repeats at every switch.
```

That is a **very strong 5–10 mark answer** because it demonstrates the actual mechanism rather than simply defining VC switching.

# CS 606T — MPLS
## Label Distribution, Label Tables & Label Swapping

---

# 1. First: Why do we need MPLS?

In normal IP forwarding, a router receives a packet and examines the **destination IP address**.

For example:

```text
Destination IP:
10.20.30.40
       ↓
Router
       ↓
Routing table
       ↓
Longest Prefix Match
       ↓
Outgoing interface
```

This works, but MPLS introduces another approach:

```text
IP packet
   ↓
MPLS Label
   ↓
Label lookup
   ↓
Outgoing interface
```

So instead of repeatedly making forwarding decisions based on the full IP destination, MPLS routers can use a **short label**.

The fundamental idea is:

$$
\boxed{\text{IP routing determines the path; MPLS labels help forward along that path}}
$$

---

# 2. What is an MPLS Label?

An MPLS label is a short identifier attached to a packet.

Conceptually:

```text
┌───────────────────────────────┐
│ IP Header                     │
├───────────────────────────────┤
│ MPLS Label                    │
├───────────────────────────────┤
│ Data                          │
└───────────────────────────────┘
```

The label tells an MPLS router which forwarding action to perform.

For example:

```text
Incoming Label = 25

        ↓

MPLS forwarding table

        ↓

Outgoing Interface = 3
Outgoing Label = 41
```

So the router performs:

$$
\boxed{25\rightarrow41}
$$

This is called **label swapping**.

---

# 3. Very Important: Labels Have Local Scope

This is similar to the VCI concept we just studied.

Suppose:

```text
R1 → R2 → R3 → R4
```

The labels could be:

```text
R1 → R2 : Label 10
R2 → R3 : Label 25
R3 → R4 : Label 7
```

There is no requirement that every router use label 10.

Instead:

```text
R1:
10

R2:
25

R3:
7
```

Each label is meaningful within the local MPLS forwarding context.

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{\text{MPLS labels have local significance}}
$$

This is one of the similarities between:

**Virtual Circuit Switching** and **MPLS**.

---

# 4. What is an LER?

Your professor's PPT introduces:

$$
\boxed{\text{LER = Label Edge Router}}
$$

An LER is located at the **edge** of an MPLS network.

Example:

```text
                MPLS Network

          ┌─────────────────────┐
          │                     │
Internet → R1 → R2 → R3 → R4 → Internet
           ↑                 ↑
          LER               LER
```

The edge router receives traffic entering the MPLS domain and can classify it and impose an MPLS label.

---

# 5. What is an LSR?

Inside the MPLS network we have:

$$
\boxed{\text{LSR = Label Switch Router}}
$$

For example:

```text
        MPLS domain

LER        LSR       LSR       LER
 R1  ─────  R2  ───── R3  ───── R4
```

R2 and R3 forward packets primarily based on MPLS labels.

---

# 6. LER vs LSR

Remember this simple distinction:

| Device | Role |
|---|---|
| **LER** | At MPLS edge |
| **LSR** | Inside MPLS network |
| **LER** | Adds/removes MPLS labels as appropriate |
| **LSR** | Performs label-based forwarding |

Think:

```text
Outside → LER → LSR → LSR → LER → Outside
```

---

# 7. What is a Label Distribution Protocol?

Now we reach the important part from your PPT.

Your professor says:

> The router allocates a label for each prefix in its routing table and advertises both the label and the prefix to neighboring routers.

This requires routers to communicate label information.

One protocol used for this is:

$$
\boxed{\text{LDP = Label Distribution Protocol}}
$$

---

# 8. Why Do Routers Need to Distribute Labels?

Imagine:

```text
R1 → R2 → R3 → R4
```

Suppose the destination network is:

$$
10.10.0.0/16
$$

R3 needs to know what label should be used when forwarding traffic toward that destination.

So routers distribute information such as:

```text
Destination Prefix → Label
```

Conceptually:

```text
10.10.0.0/16 → Label 50
```

This information is shared with neighboring MPLS routers.

---

# 9. Important Distinction: IP Routing vs Label Distribution

Don't mix these two.

### IP routing

Answers:

> **Where should traffic go?**

For example:

```text
10.10.0.0/16
      ↓
Next hop = R3
```

### Label distribution

Answers:

> **Which label should be used for this forwarding context?**

For example:

```text
10.10.0.0/16
      ↓
Label = 50
```

Together they allow MPLS forwarding.

---

# 10. Let's Build a Network

Consider:

```text
          R1
          |
          |
          R2
          |
          |
          R3
          |
          |
          R4
```

Suppose:

- R1 = LER
- R2 = LSR
- R3 = LSR
- R4 = LER

And destination network is attached to R4:

```text
R1 → R2 → R3 → R4 → Destination
```

Suppose R4 advertises information about:

```text
Prefix = 192.168.50.0/24
```

and associates a label with it.

For learning purposes, suppose:

```text
192.168.50.0/24 → Label 30
```

---

# 11. Label Tables

An MPLS router maintains forwarding information that conceptually maps:

$$
\boxed{
\text{Incoming Label}
\rightarrow
\text{Outgoing Label + Interface}
}
$$

For example:

| Incoming Label | Outgoing Interface | Outgoing Label |
|---:|---|---:|
| 20 | R3 | 30 |
| 25 | R4 | 40 |
| 31 | R2 | 15 |

This means:

```text
Incoming label 20
       ↓
     lookup
       ↓
Outgoing interface = R3
Outgoing label = 30
```

---

# 12. Label Swapping

Now let's see the actual forwarding.

Suppose R2 receives:

```text
Packet
Label = 20
```

R2 searches its MPLS forwarding table:

```text
Incoming Label 20
        ↓
Outgoing Interface = R3
Outgoing Label = 30
```

Therefore:

```text
20 → 30
```

The packet is sent to R3 with label 30.

---

# 13. At R3

Suppose R3's table says:

| Incoming Label | Outgoing Interface | Outgoing Label |
|---:|---|---:|
| 30 | R4 | 45 |

R3 receives:

```text
Label = 30
```

Then:

```text
30 → 45
```

Packet moves toward R4.

---

# 14. Complete Label-Swapping Example

Let's put everything together:

```text
        Label 20       Label 30       Label 45
R1 ─────────────→ R2 ─────────────→ R3 ─────────────→ R4
                  │                  │
                swap               swap
               20→30              30→45
```

At R2:

$$
20\rightarrow30
$$

At R3:

$$
30\rightarrow45
$$

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{20\rightarrow30\rightarrow45}
$$

The packet is following the MPLS forwarding path.

---

# 15. What Exactly Does the Router Look At?

This is important.

Suppose R2 receives:

```text
Incoming interface = 1
Incoming label = 20
```

The router can use the incoming label and interface/context to find the appropriate forwarding entry.

Conceptually:

```text
(Incoming Interface, Incoming Label)
                ↓
          MPLS Table
                ↓
(Outgoing Interface, Outgoing Label)
```

This is very similar to what you learned for Virtual Circuit Switching:

```text
(Incoming Interface, Incoming VCI)
                ↓
             VC Table
                ↓
(Outgoing Interface, Outgoing VCI)
```

That's why MPLS is often taught alongside virtual circuits.

---

# 16. VC Switching vs MPLS

This comparison is extremely useful for your exam.

| Feature | Virtual Circuit | MPLS |
|---|---|---|
| Identifier | VCI | Label |
| Identifier scope | Local | Local |
| Forwarding | VCI lookup | Label lookup |
| Identifier changes at hops | Yes | Yes |
| Path can be established/engineered | Yes | Yes |
| Uses forwarding tables | Yes | Yes |
| Exact label lookup | Yes | Yes |
| Main purpose | Virtual connection | Label-based forwarding/traffic engineering/VPN support |

Conceptually:

```text
VC:

VCI 10
 ↓
Switch
 ↓
VCI 25
 ↓
Switch
 ↓
VCI 7

MPLS:

Label 10
 ↓
LSR
 ↓
Label 25
 ↓
LSR
 ↓
Label 7
```

Very similar mechanism.

---

# 17. Destination-Based Forwarding in MPLS

Your professor specifically included this phrase.

Let's understand it.

Suppose R1 has the following routing information:

| Prefix | Next Hop |
|---|---|
| 10.0.0.0/8 | R2 |
| 20.0.0.0/8 | R3 |
| 30.0.0.0/8 | R4 |

MPLS can associate labels with these forwarding destinations/prefixes.

Conceptually:

| Prefix | Label |
|---|---:|
| 10.0.0.0/8 | 15 |
| 20.0.0.0/8 | 25 |
| 30.0.0.0/8 | 35 |

The label is then used for forwarding within the MPLS domain.

---

# 18. Why Does the Label Need to Change?

This is a very common question.

Why not simply assign:

```text
Destination → Label 100
```

and keep label 100 throughout the entire network?

Because MPLS labels have **local significance**.

Different routers can assign labels independently.

For example:

```text
R1 → R2
Label 10

R2 → R3
Label 50

R3 → R4
Label 7
```

Therefore each router performs a local mapping:

$$
\boxed{10\rightarrow50\rightarrow7}
$$

This is **label swapping**.

---

# 19. Explicit Routing

Now let's connect this with another bullet in your PPT.

Suppose the network looks like:

```text
             R3 ─── R6
            /         \
R1 ────────              R7
            \           /
             R4 ─ R5
```

There may be multiple possible routes.

For traffic:

```text
R1 → R7
```

we might want:

$$
R1\rightarrow R3\rightarrow R6\rightarrow R7
$$

while another flow:

$$
R2\rightarrow R7
$$

might use:

$$
R2\rightarrow R3\rightarrow R4\rightarrow R5\rightarrow R7
$$

MPLS can support these **explicitly engineered paths**.

---

# 20. Why Is This Useful?

Because normal IP routing might select a different path.

For example:

```text
Normal IP:
R1 → R2 → R4 → R7
```

But network engineering might want:

```text
MPLS:
R1 → R3 → R6 → R7
```

because of considerations such as:

- traffic distribution
- capacity
- congestion
- administrative policy
- QoS requirements

The important exam phrase is:

$$
\boxed{\text{MPLS supports explicit routing / traffic engineering}}
$$

---

# 21. Complete MPLS Flow

Now combine everything.

### Step 1 — IP routing information exists

Routers learn destination prefixes.

```text
Prefix → next hop
```

### Step 2 — Labels are assigned

A router associates labels with forwarding destinations.

```text
Prefix → Label
```

### Step 3 — Labels are distributed

Routers communicate label information with neighboring routers.

```text
LDP
 ↓
Label information
```

### Step 4 — MPLS forwarding begins

A packet enters through an LER.

```text
IP packet
   ↓
LER
   ↓
MPLS label imposed
```

### Step 5 — LSRs forward by label

```text
Label 10
   ↓
LSR
   ↓
Label 25
   ↓
LSR
   ↓
Label 40
```

### Step 6 — Packet exits MPLS domain

At the edge:

```text
LSR → LER → destination
```

The MPLS label is removed as appropriate and normal IP forwarding can continue.

---

# 22. Important Terminology

You should be comfortable with these:

### MPLS

**Multiprotocol Label Switching**

### LER

**Label Edge Router**

### LSR

**Label Switch Router**

### LDP

**Label Distribution Protocol**

### Label

Short forwarding identifier.

### Label swapping

Replacing:

$$
\boxed{\text{Incoming Label} \rightarrow \text{Outgoing Label}}
$$

### Label table

Maps incoming label/context to forwarding action.

### Explicit routing

Forcing/engineering traffic along a specified path rather than simply using the path selected by ordinary IP routing.

---

# 23. Exam-Level Numerical

Suppose:

```text
R1 → R2 → R3 → R4
```

The MPLS forwarding tables are:

### R2

| Incoming Label | Outgoing Interface | Outgoing Label |
|---:|---|---:|
| 10 | R3 | 25 |

### R3

| Incoming Label | Outgoing Interface | Outgoing Label |
|---:|---|---:|
| 25 | R4 | 40 |

A packet enters R2 with:

$$
Label=10
$$

### At R2:

$$
10\rightarrow25
$$

### At R3:

$$
25\rightarrow40
$$

Therefore:

```text
R2: 10 → 25
R3: 25 → 40
```

Final label reaching R4:

$$
\boxed{40}
$$

---

# 24. Tricky Conceptual Question

### Question:

> If MPLS labels are locally significant, can two different links use the same label number?

### Answer:

**Yes.**

For example:

```text
R1 → R2 : Label 10

R3 → R4 : Label 10
```

This is not inherently a conflict because the labels have different local contexts.

The important point is:

$$
\boxed{\text{Label value alone does not imply global uniqueness}}
$$

---

# 25. Another Tricky Question

### Q:

> Does MPLS eliminate IP routing?

### Answer:

**No.**

MPLS works with routing information and can use IP routing information to establish forwarding state.

A simplified view is:

```text
IP routing
    ↓
Determine destination/reachability
    ↓
MPLS label distribution / forwarding state
    ↓
Label-based forwarding
```

So MPLS is **not simply a replacement for IP routing**.

---

# 26. VC vs MPLS — The Key Connection

This is probably the most important conceptual connection between the two PPTs.

### Virtual Circuit:

```text
Path
 ↓
VCI assigned
 ↓
VC tables created
 ↓
VCI lookup
 ↓
VCI swapping
```

### MPLS:

```text
Forwarding path
 ↓
Labels assigned/distributed
 ↓
Label forwarding state
 ↓
Label lookup
 ↓
Label swapping
```

Therefore, your professor's statement that MPLS:

> "combines some of the properties of virtual circuits with the flexibility and robustness of datagrams"

should now make sense.

---

# ⭐ Final Mental Model

Remember MPLS using this:

```text
                 MPLS
                  │
        ┌─────────┴──────────┐
        ↓                    ↓
   Edge Router           Core Router
      LER                    LSR
        │                    │
        ↓                    ↓
 Add/Remove label       Swap label
        │                    │
        └─────────┬──────────┘
                  ↓
           Label forwarding
                  │
                  ↓
          Explicit paths
          Traffic Engineering
                  │
                  ↓
                 VPN
```

And the single most important forwarding equation is:

$$
\boxed{
(Incoming\ Label,\ Context)
\rightarrow
(Outgoing\ Interface,\ Outgoing\ Label)
}
$$

For example:

$$
\boxed{10\rightarrow25\rightarrow40\rightarrow7}
$$

That's **MPLS label swapping**.

---

With this, we have now covered the MPLS material you provided at a deeper level:

- MPLS concept
- Why MPLS is related to VC switching
- Labels
- Local label scope
- LER
- LSR
- Destination-based forwarding
- Label Distribution Protocol
- Prefix-to-label concept
- MPLS forwarding tables
- Label lookup
- Label swapping
- Explicit routing
- Traffic engineering
- MPLS/VPN connection
- VC vs MPLS comparison

## One important correction/clarification

Your professor's Linux-router numerical has:

$$
\frac{40\text{ Bytes}}{2\text{ Gbps}}\approx160\text{ ns}
$$

Strictly speaking, if we convert **40 bytes = 320 bits**, then:

$$
\frac{320}{2\times10^9}
=160\text{ ns}
$$

So that calculation is correct.

Then:

$$
T_{\text{CPU}}=\frac{200}{2\times10^9}
=100\text{ ns}
$$

and because the packet is transferred **NIC → memory** and then **memory → NIC**:

$$
T_{\text{total}}
=160+100+160
=420\text{ ns}
$$

The resulting throughput is:

$$
\frac{320\text{ bits}}{420\text{ ns}}
\approx 761.9\text{ Mbps}
$$

So the lecture's approximately **760 Mbps** is correct.

This numerical is particularly important because it can easily be modified into an exam question.

Since you previously wanted **exam-oriented preparation rather than just reading notes**, let's start with **Topic 1: Router Fundamentals** and I'll teach it in this pattern:

> **Concept → intuitive explanation → diagram → example → tricky point → exam question → numerical/application question**

Then after completing all 15 topics, we can create a **large CS 606T Router Architectures question bank** containing theoretical, conceptual, numerical, diagram-based, scenario-based, and difficult questions.

# CS 606T — Router Architectures
## Topic 2: Forwarding Functions

Now we move to the next topic after **Routing vs Forwarding + Routing Table vs Forwarding Table**.

The key idea is:

> **A router receives a packet → checks it → modifies some fields → decides where it should go → possibly fragments it → sends it onward.**

---

# 1. What happens when a packet arrives?

Suppose a packet arrives at router R1:

```text
Host A
   |
   ↓
[Ingress Interface]
   |
   ↓
[Packet Processing]
   |
   ↓
[Forwarding Table Lookup]
   |
   ↓
[Output Interface]
   |
   ↓
Host B
```

The router performs several operations.

From your PPT, the important forwarding functions are:

1. IP Header Validation
2. Packet Lifetime Control
3. Checksum Restoration
4. Route Lookup
5. Fragmentation

And additional functions include:

6. Packet Classification
7. Packet Translation
8. Traffic Prioritization

Let's understand each.

---

# 2. IP Header Validation

When a packet arrives, the router first checks whether its IP header is valid.

For example, it checks:

- IP version
- Header length
- Header correctness
- Checksum

### Why?

The router should not forward a corrupted or malformed packet.

Conceptually:

```text
Incoming packet
      ↓
Is IP header valid?
      ↓
   YES     NO
    ↓       ↓
Continue   Drop
```

---

# 3. Packet Lifetime Control — TTL

This is extremely important.

Every IP packet has a field called:

> **TTL = Time To Live**

It prevents a packet from circulating forever in the network.

Suppose:

```text
A → R1 → R2 → R3 → B
```

The packet might initially have:

```text
TTL = 5
```

After R1:

```text
TTL = 4
```

After R2:

```text
TTL = 3
```

After R3:

```text
TTL = 2
```

So, approximately:

$$
TTL_{new}=TTL_{old}-1
$$

at each router hop.

---

# 4. Why do we need TTL?

Consider a routing loop:

```text
        ┌───────┐
        ↓       |
A → R1 → R2 → R3
     ↑           |
     └───────────┘
```

If there were no TTL, a packet caught in the loop could circulate indefinitely.

TTL provides a mechanism to eventually destroy it.

When TTL reaches zero, the router discards the packet and normally sends an ICMP Time Exceeded message back toward the source.

---

# 5. Checksum Restoration

There is an important connection here.

The router decrements TTL.

But TTL is part of the IP header.

Therefore, changing TTL changes the header contents.

So the IP header checksum must be updated/recomputed.

Conceptually:

```text
Original packet
TTL = 10
Checksum = C1

        ↓ Router

TTL = 9
Checksum = C2
```

Therefore:

> **TTL modification → IP header checksum must be updated.**

### Exam trap

A common mistake is saying:

> "The router changes TTL but checksum remains unchanged."

❌ Incorrect.

---

# 6. Route Lookup / Forwarding Lookup

Now the router needs to answer:

> **Where should this packet go next?**

The router examines the destination IP address.

For example:

```text
Destination IP = 192.168.5.20
```

The router performs a lookup in its forwarding table.

Example:

| Destination Prefix | Next Hop | Output Interface |
|---|---|---|
| 192.168.1.0/24 | R2 | 1 |
| 192.168.5.0/24 | R3 | 2 |
| 10.0.0.0/8 | R4 | 3 |

For:

```text
Destination = 192.168.5.20
```

the router finds:

```text
192.168.5.0/24
```

and sends the packet through:

```text
Output Interface = 2
```

---

# 7. Important: Routing Table vs Forwarding Table

Remember the previous topic.

### Routing process

Determines paths and produces:

```text
Routing Table
```

Then:

```text
Routing Table
      ↓
Forwarding Table
      ↓
Fast packet forwarding
```

The forwarding lookup must be extremely fast because routers can process huge numbers of packets per second.

---

# 8. Fragmentation

This is another important exam topic.

Suppose an incoming packet is:

```text
Packet size = 4000 bytes
```

but the outgoing link can carry only:

```text
MTU = 1500 bytes
```

The packet is too large for the outgoing link.

The router may need to divide it into smaller fragments.

```text
Original packet
┌───────────────────────────────┐
│          4000 bytes           │
└───────────────────────────────┘

              ↓

      Fragmentation

 ┌───────────┐ ┌───────────┐ ┌───────────┐
 │ Fragment1 │ │ Fragment2 │ │ Fragment3 │
 └───────────┘ └───────────┘ └───────────┘
```

Each fragment can then fit within the outgoing link's MTU.

---

# 9. Why does fragmentation occur?

Because:

$$
MTU_{outgoing} < Packet\ Size
$$

For example:

```text
Incoming packet = 4000 bytes
Outgoing MTU = 1500 bytes
```

Therefore:

```text
4000 > 1500
```

and fragmentation may be necessary.

---

# 10. Packet Classification

Modern routers don't always treat every packet identically.

They may classify packets based on information such as:

- Source IP
- Destination IP
- Source port
- Destination port
- Protocol
- Other packet fields

Example:

```text
Netflix traffic
      ↓
High priority

Background file transfer
      ↓
Lower priority
```

The classification allows the router to apply different policies.

---

# 11. Packet Translation — NAT

Your PPT gives the example of NAT.

Suppose many devices inside a home network use private addresses:

```text
192.168.1.10
192.168.1.11
192.168.1.12
```

They may share one public IP address:

```text
Public IP
203.x.x.x
```

The gateway/router performs **Network Address Translation (NAT)**.

Conceptually:

```text
Private Network
       |
       ↓
     Router
     NAT
       |
       ↓
Internet
```

The router translates addresses/ports so multiple internal hosts can communicate using shared public addressing.

---

# 12. Traffic Prioritization

Different applications may require different service levels.

For example:

```text
Voice/video
    ↓
Low delay required

Email
    ↓
Less delay-sensitive

Large backup
    ↓
Can tolerate delay
```

A router can therefore prioritize packets.

This is related to:

> **QoS — Quality of Service**

The router can use queues and scheduling policies to decide which packet should be transmitted first.

---

# 13. Put Everything Together

When a packet arrives:

```text
              PACKET ARRIVES
                    │
                    ↓
          IP Header Validation
                    │
                    ↓
              TTL Processing
                    │
                    ↓
          Checksum Update
                    │
                    ↓
          Destination Lookup
                    │
                    ↓
            Packet Classification
                    │
                    ↓
       ┌────────────┴────────────┐
       │                         │
   Fragmentation             NAT/Other
       │                         │
       └────────────┬────────────┘
                    ↓
             Queue / Schedule
                    ↓
              Output Interface
```

Not every packet necessarily undergoes every possible operation; the exact processing depends on the router configuration, packet, protocol, and network conditions.

---

# 14. Fast Exam Comparison

| Function | Purpose |
|---|---|
| Header validation | Check packet/header correctness |
| TTL processing | Prevent indefinite packet looping |
| Checksum update | Reflect changes to IP header |
| Forwarding lookup | Determine output interface/next hop |
| Fragmentation | Split packet when necessary for MTU |
| Classification | Identify packets according to rules |
| NAT | Translate network addresses/ports |
| Traffic prioritization | Provide differentiated treatment/QoS |

---

# 15. Important Exam Questions

You should be able to answer these without notes:

### Q1.
What happens when an IP packet arrives at a router?

### Q2.
Why is TTL decremented at every router?

### Q3.
Why must the IP checksum be updated after TTL modification?

### Q4.
What happens when TTL becomes zero?

### Q5.
What is fragmentation?

### Q6.
Why does a router perform fragmentation?

### Q7.
What is MTU?

### Q8.
Explain packet classification.

### Q9.
What is NAT and why is it performed by routers?

### Q10.
How does traffic prioritization support QoS?

---

## 🔥 Tricky Question

Suppose:

```text
Packet size = 4000 bytes
Outgoing MTU = 1500 bytes
TTL = 5
```

The packet arrives at a router.

**Question:** What are the two independent issues the router has to deal with?

Think about it before looking at the answer.

### Answer

**Issue 1 — TTL**

The router decrements:

$$
5 \rightarrow 4
$$

and updates the IP header checksum.

**Issue 2 — MTU**

Since:

$$
4000 > 1500
$$

the packet may need fragmentation before being transmitted over the outgoing link.

So these are **two separate forwarding functions**:

```text
TTL processing
+
Fragmentation
```

---

### One subtle point to remember

Don't confuse:

**Routing** with **forwarding**.

> **Routing:** "What path should packets take?"

> **Forwarding:** "This packet has arrived; which interface should I send it through?"

That distinction is foundational for the rest of this chapter.

**Next topic: Router Architecture — Line Cards, Switching Fabric/Backplane, and Routing Processor.**

9/19/2026, 7:19:08 PM · gpt-5-6

Sure. Here are the **answers to all 10 exam questions** from Topic 2, written in an exam-ready way.

# CS 606T — Router Architectures
## Topic 2: Forwarding Functions — Answers

### Q1. What happens when an IP packet arrives at a router?

When an IP packet arrives at a router, the router performs several forwarding operations:

1. Validates the IP header.
2. Decrements the TTL value.
3. Updates/recomputes the IP header checksum.
4. Examines the destination IP address.
5. Performs a forwarding-table lookup.
6. Determines the appropriate output interface/next hop.
7. Performs additional functions such as classification, NAT, QoS, or fragmentation if required.
8. Queues and schedules the packet for transmission.
9. Sends the packet through the selected output interface.

---

### Q2. Why is TTL decremented at every router?

**TTL (Time To Live)** prevents packets from circulating indefinitely in the network due to routing loops.

At every router:

$$
TTL_{new}=TTL_{old}-1
$$

When the TTL expires, the router discards the packet and normally sends an **ICMP Time Exceeded** message to the sender.

**Example:**

```text
Source → R1 → R2 → R3

TTL = 5
       ↓
R1 → 4
R2 → 3
R3 → 2
```

---

### Q3. Why must the IP checksum be updated after TTL modification?

The TTL field is part of the IP header.

When the router changes:

$$
TTL: 10\rightarrow9
$$

the contents of the IP header have changed.

Therefore, the IP header checksum must also be updated so that the modified header can be validated correctly.

**Key point:**

> TTL changes → IP header changes → checksum must be updated.

---

### Q4. What happens when TTL becomes zero?

When the packet's TTL expires, the router **discards the packet** instead of forwarding it.

The router normally generates an:

> **ICMP Time Exceeded**

message and sends it toward the source.

This prevents packets caught in routing loops from remaining in the network indefinitely.

---

### Q5. What is fragmentation?

**Fragmentation** is the process of dividing a large IP packet into smaller packets called **fragments** so that they can be transmitted over a link whose MTU is smaller than the original packet.

For example:

```text
Packet = 4000 bytes
Outgoing MTU = 1500 bytes

4000 bytes
    ↓
┌─────────┐ ┌─────────┐ ┌─────────┐
│Fragment │ │Fragment │ │Fragment │
└─────────┘ └─────────┘ └─────────┘
```

The fragments can then travel across the smaller-MTU link.

---

### Q6. Why does a router perform fragmentation?

A router may need to fragment a packet when:

$$
Packet\ Size > Outgoing\ Link\ MTU
$$

For example:

$$
4000 > 1500
$$

Therefore, the original packet cannot fit into a single outgoing frame/packet transmission unit and fragmentation may be required, subject to the applicable IP fragmentation rules.

---

### Q7. What is MTU?

**MTU = Maximum Transmission Unit**

It is the maximum size of an IP packet that can be carried by a particular network link/interface without requiring fragmentation at that point.

For example:

```text
Ethernet MTU ≈ 1500 bytes
```

If an outgoing interface has an MTU of 1500 bytes and a packet is larger than that, the router must handle the MTU constraint appropriately.

### Important distinction:

**MTU ≠ bandwidth**

- MTU → maximum packet size
- Bandwidth → data transmission capacity per second

---

### Q8. Explain packet classification.

**Packet classification** means identifying packets according to specific packet-header fields and applying appropriate rules or policies.

A router may classify packets using:

- Source IP address
- Destination IP address
- Source port
- Destination port
- Protocol
- Other relevant fields

For example:

```text
Video traffic       → High priority
Voice traffic       → High priority
Email               → Normal priority
Background backup   → Low priority
```

Packet classification is commonly used for **QoS, filtering, security, and traffic management**.

---

### Q9. What is NAT and why is it performed by routers?

**NAT = Network Address Translation**

NAT allows private/internal addresses to be translated to public addresses when communicating with external networks.

For example:

```text
PC1 → 192.168.1.10 ─┐
PC2 → 192.168.1.11 ─┼→ Router/NAT → Internet
PC3 → 192.168.1.12 ─┘
                         |
                    Public IP
```

A router/gateway maintains the necessary translation state so that multiple internal hosts can communicate through shared public addressing.

NAT is commonly used at network boundaries, especially in IPv4 networks.

---

### Q10. How does traffic prioritization support QoS?

Traffic prioritization gives different packets different levels of service according to application or policy requirements.

For example:

| Traffic | Priority |
|---|---|
| Voice | High |
| Video | High |
| Web browsing | Normal |
| Large backup | Low |

The router can place packets into different queues and use a scheduling algorithm to determine which queue gets transmission service.

This helps provide QoS characteristics such as:

- lower delay for important traffic
- controlled bandwidth allocation
- reduced congestion impact
- differentiated service

---

# ⭐ 5 Very Important One-Line Answers

These are worth memorizing for MCQs/short answers:

**TTL:** Prevents packets from circulating indefinitely.

**Checksum:** Detects errors in the IP header; it must be updated when relevant header fields change.

**MTU:** Maximum packet size that can be transmitted over a particular link/interface without fragmentation at that point.

**Packet classification:** Identifying packets based on header/flow information to apply policies.

**NAT:** Translates network addresses (and commonly transport-layer ports) between address domains.

---

## 🔥 One Integrated Exam Question

**Question:** A packet arrives at a router with TTL = 1 and size = 2000 bytes. The outgoing interface has MTU = 1500 bytes. Explain what the router needs to consider.

**Answer:**

First, the router decrements:

$$
TTL=1\rightarrow0
$$

Since the TTL has expired, the router **discards the packet** and normally generates an ICMP Time Exceeded message.

Therefore, the packet is **not forwarded**, and the MTU/fragmentation issue does not result in transmission of this packet.

This is a good exam trap: **don't immediately start calculating fragments before checking whether the packet can even be forwarded.**

# CS 606T — Router Architectures
## Topic 3: Router Architecture

Now we move to **Router Architecture — Functional Perspective + Hardware Perspective**.

This is an important topic because it connects the abstract functions of a router to its actual internal components.

---

# 1. Basic Router Architecture

From your PPT, a router has three major functional components:

```text
                 ┌──────────────────────┐
                 │   Routing Processor   │
                 │                      │
                 │ Routing protocols    │
                 │ Routing table        │
                 └──────────┬───────────┘
                            │
                            ↓
      ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
      │          Switching Fabric            │
      │             / Backplane              │
      └───────────────┬──────────────────────┘
                      │
          ┌───────────┴───────────┐
          ↓                       ↓
   ┌─────────────┐         ┌─────────────┐
   │ Input Line   │         │ Output Line │
   │ Card         │         │ Card        │
   │              │         │             │
   │ Ingress      │         │ Egress      │
   │ processing   │         │ processing  │
   └─────────────┘         └─────────────┘
```

The three major components are:

1. **Line Cards**
2. **Switching Fabric / Backplane**
3. **Routing Processor**

---

# 2. Line Cards

A **line card** is associated with a router interface and handles packets entering or leaving the router.

Your PPT says that the line card:

- parses packet/IP information
- makes forwarding decisions
- handles queueing
- may discard packets
- contains memory for packets waiting to be forwarded
- houses the port/network interface

Think of it as the router's **interface-level packet processing unit**.

---

# 3. Ingress vs Egress

Two important words:

### Ingress

Means:

> **Packet entering the router**

```text
Network
   ↓
[Ingress Line Card]
   ↓
Router
```

### Egress

Means:

> **Packet leaving the router**

```text
Router
   ↓
[Egress Line Card]
   ↓
Network
```

So:

```text
Ingress = IN
Egress  = OUT
```

This is a very common exam/MCQ concept.

---

# 4. Switching Fabric / Backplane

The switching fabric is the internal mechanism that transfers packets from the selected input/ingress line card to the appropriate output/egress line card.

```text
Ingress LC
    │
    ↓
┌───────────────────┐
│ Switching Fabric  │
│ / Backplane       │
└───────────────────┘
    │
    ↓
Egress LC
```

It is essentially the router's **internal packet transportation system**.

### Important:

The switching fabric does **not primarily decide the destination route**.

The forwarding logic determines the appropriate output.

The switching fabric then provides the internal path to that output.

---

# 5. Routing Processor

The routing processor is responsible for the router's **control-plane functions**.

It:

- runs routing protocols
- exchanges routing information with neighboring routers
- maintains routing information
- constructs/updates the routing table
- handles router configuration and management

For example, routing protocols may determine:

```text
Destination Network
        ↓
Best path
        ↓
Routing Table
        ↓
Forwarding Table
```

---

# 6. Very Important Distinction

You should remember:

| Component | Main responsibility |
|---|---|
| Line Card | Packet/interface processing |
| Switching Fabric | Move packet internally |
| Routing Processor | Routing/control functions |

A common exam trap is:

> "The switching fabric calculates the best route."

❌ No.

The routing/control functions determine the route.

The switching fabric transfers packets between line cards.

---

# 7. Complete Packet Journey

Suppose:

```text
Host A → Router R1 → Host B
```

Inside R1:

```text
                ROUTER
────────────────────────────────────────

Incoming packet
      ↓
┌───────────────┐
│ Ingress Line  │
│ Card          │
└───────┬───────┘
        │
        │ Forwarding lookup
        ↓
┌────────────────────┐
│ Switching Fabric   │
│ / Backplane        │
└─────────┬──────────┘
          │
          ↓
┌────────────────┐
│ Egress Line    │
│ Card           │
└────────┬───────┘
         │
         ↓
      Host B
```

---

# 8. Hardware Perspective

Your professor also gives another view:

> How is a router actually implemented using hardware?

The simplified architecture is:

```text
Network
   ↓
NIC
   ↓
PCI Bus
   ↓
Main Memory
   ↓
CPU
   ↓
Main Memory
   ↓
PCI Bus
   ↓
Output NIC
   ↓
Network
```

Let's understand it.

---

# 9. Step 1 — Packet Arrives at NIC

The packet first arrives at the router's network interface card.

```text
Network
   ↓
[NIC]
```

The NIC receives the packet from the physical network.

---

# 10. Step 2 — Packet Moves to Memory

The packet is transferred from the NIC toward main memory through the system bus, represented in your lecture by the **PCI bus**.

```text
NIC
 ↓
PCI Bus
 ↓
Memory
```

The bus bandwidth becomes important for router performance.

---

# 11. Step 3 — Packet Processing

The CPU performs processing such as:

- examining headers
- checksum/TTL processing
- destination address processing
- forwarding lookup
- determining the output interface

Conceptually:

```text
Memory
   ↓
CPU
   ↓
Packet processing
```

---

# 12. Step 4 — Packet Goes to Output NIC

After processing:

```text
Memory
   ↓
PCI Bus
   ↓
Output NIC
   ↓
Network
```

So the complete simplified process is:

```text
Input NIC
    ↓
Memory
    ↓
CPU
    ↓
Memory
    ↓
Output NIC
```

This is extremely important because it leads directly to the **Linux PC router performance numerical** in your next section.

---

# 13. Why Can This Architecture Be Slow?

Look at the packet path:

```text
NIC
 ↓
Memory
 ↓
CPU
 ↓
Memory
 ↓
NIC
```

The packet is being moved multiple times.

The bus is also shared by these transfers.

Therefore, even if the CPU is very fast, the **bus/memory transfer can become the bottleneck**.

This is exactly what your professor demonstrates using:

- PCI bandwidth = 2 Gbps
- CPU = 2 GHz
- 200 instructions
- packet/header size = 40 bytes

---

# 14. Functional vs Hardware Perspective

This distinction is important.

### Functional perspective

Focuses on **what the router components do**:

```text
Line Cards
Switching Fabric
Routing Processor
```

### Hardware perspective

Focuses on **how packet processing occurs physically**:

```text
NIC
 ↓
PCI Bus
 ↓
Memory
 ↓
CPU
 ↓
Memory
 ↓
NIC
```

---

# 15. Exam Comparison

| Functional Component | Main Function |
|---|---|
| Routing Processor | Routing protocols and routing table |
| Line Card | Interface-level packet processing |
| Switching Fabric | Internal packet transfer |
| Line-card memory | Buffer packets |
| Network Interface | Connect router to external network |

---

# 16. Important Conceptual Question

### Why is the routing processor not necessarily involved in every packet forwarding decision?

Because modern routers separate **control-plane** and **data-plane** operations.

The routing processor can calculate routes and maintain routing information, while the forwarding path uses the resulting forwarding information to process packets at high speed.

Conceptually:

```text
        CONTROL PLANE
              │
       Routing Processor
              │
        Routing Table
              ↓
       Forwarding Table
              │
              ↓
        DATA PLANE
              │
         Line Cards
              │
       Switching Fabric
              │
        Packet Forwarding
```

This separation is fundamental to high-performance routers.

---

# 17. Exam Questions From This Topic

Try these before looking at answers:

### Q1.
What are the three major functional components of a router?

### Q2.
What is the function of a line card?

### Q3.
Differentiate between ingress and egress.

### Q4.
What is a switching fabric?

### Q5.
What is the role of the routing processor?

### Q6.
Trace the journey of a packet through a router.

### Q7.
Differentiate between the functional and hardware perspectives of router architecture.

### Q8.
Why can the PCI bus become a bottleneck in a PC-based router?

### Q9.
Why is the routing processor not required to perform the complete routing calculation for every incoming packet?

### Q10.
Differentiate between the control plane and data plane.

---

## ⭐ Most Important Diagram to Remember

For an exam, draw this when asked **"Explain router architecture"**:

```text
                         ┌─────────────────────┐
                         │ Routing Processor   │
                         │                     │
                         │ Routing Protocols   │
                         │ Routing Table       │
                         └──────────┬──────────┘
                                    │
                             Forwarding Info
                                    │
                                    ↓
        ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────────┐
IN ───→ │ Ingress Line │ ──→ │  Switching   │ ──→ │ Egress Line  │ ───→ OUT
        │ Card         │     │   Fabric     │     │ Card         │
        └──────────────┘     └──────────────┘     └──────────────┘
```

**Next topic is the Linux PC-based Router Performance numerical**, where we will derive the **420 ns processing time and ~760 Mbps throughput**, and then solve variations that your professor can ask in the exam.

Sure. Here are the **answers to all 10 questions** from Topic 3, in an exam-ready format.

# CS 606T — Router Architectures
## Topic 3: Router Architecture — Answers

### Q1. What are the three major functional components of a router?

The three major functional components are:

1. **Line Cards** — handle packet reception, forwarding-related processing, queueing, and transmission.
2. **Switching Fabric / Backplane** — transfers packets from ingress line cards to the appropriate egress line cards.
3. **Routing Processor** — implements routing protocols and maintains the routing information/routing table.

---

### Q2. What is the function of a line card?

A **line card** connects a router to a network interface and handles packet processing at that interface.

Its functions include:

- Receiving incoming packets.
- Parsing packet/IP headers.
- Performing forwarding-related processing.
- Maintaining packet buffers/queues.
- Deciding whether packets should be forwarded or discarded.
- Sending packets toward the appropriate output interface.

In short:

> **Line card = interface-level packet processing and buffering.**

---

### Q3. Differentiate between ingress and egress.

| Ingress | Egress |
|---|---|
| Packet entering the router | Packet leaving the router |
| Input side | Output side |
| Performs incoming packet processing | Performs outgoing packet processing |
| Determines forwarding information | Queues/schedules packet for transmission |

Simple way to remember:

$$
\boxed{\text{Ingress = IN}}
$$

$$
\boxed{\text{Egress = EXIT}}
$$

---

### Q4. What is a switching fabric?

A **switching fabric**, also called a **switching backplane**, is the internal mechanism of a router that transfers packets from an ingress line card to the appropriate egress line card.

```text
Ingress Line Card
       |
       ↓
Switching Fabric
       |
       ↓
Egress Line Card
```

Its main purpose is:

> **Fast internal transfer of packets between input and output interfaces.**

---

### Q5. What is the role of the routing processor?

The **routing processor** performs the router's control-plane functions.

Its major responsibilities include:

- Running routing protocols.
- Exchanging routing information with neighboring routers.
- Maintaining the routing table.
- Processing routing updates.
- Responding to topology changes.
- Performing router configuration and management functions.

Conceptually:

$$
\text{Routing Protocols}
\rightarrow
\text{Routing Table}
\rightarrow
\text{Forwarding Information}
$$

---

### Q6. Trace the journey of a packet through a router.

A simplified packet journey is:

```text
Incoming Network
       ↓
Ingress Line Card
       ↓
Header / Packet Processing
       ↓
Forwarding Table Lookup
       ↓
Switching Fabric
       ↓
Egress Line Card
       ↓
Queue / Scheduling
       ↓
Outgoing Network
```

### Explanation:

1. Packet arrives at the **ingress interface**.
2. The ingress line card examines the packet.
3. The destination address is used for forwarding lookup.
4. The appropriate output interface is determined.
5. The switching fabric transfers the packet internally.
6. The egress line card receives the packet.
7. The packet may be queued and scheduled.
8. The packet is transmitted through the output interface.

---

### Q7. Differentiate between the functional and hardware perspectives of router architecture.

### Functional perspective

Focuses on **what the router components do**:

- Line cards
- Switching fabric
- Routing processor

### Hardware perspective

Focuses on the physical processing path:

```text
Input NIC
    ↓
PCI Bus
    ↓
Main Memory
    ↓
CPU
    ↓
Main Memory
    ↓
PCI Bus
    ↓
Output NIC
```

| Functional Perspective | Hardware Perspective |
|---|---|
| Describes logical components | Describes physical implementation |
| Line cards | NICs |
| Switching fabric | Internal bus/backplane |
| Routing processor | CPU |
| Packet buffers | Main/interface memory |

---

### Q8. Why can the PCI bus become a bottleneck in a PC-based router?

In the simplified PC-based router architecture, packets must be transferred through the bus.

A packet may follow:

```text
Input NIC
   ↓
PCI Bus
   ↓
Memory
   ↓
CPU
   ↓
Memory
   ↓
PCI Bus
   ↓
Output NIC
```

Therefore, the packet uses the bus multiple times.

Even if the CPU is very fast, the bus bandwidth may limit the rate at which packets can be transferred.

For example, your lecture considers:

$$
PCI\ bandwidth = 2\,Gbps
$$

while the resulting router throughput is approximately:

$$
760\,Mbps
$$

Thus:

> **The PCI bus can become the bottleneck because packet movement across the bus consumes significant processing time.**

---

### Q9. Why is the routing processor not required to perform the complete routing calculation for every incoming packet?

Because routing and forwarding are separated.

The routing processor performs relatively slower **control-plane** operations such as:

- Running routing protocols.
- Calculating routes.
- Maintaining routing information.

The resulting information is used to construct the **forwarding table**.

Then packet forwarding can happen rapidly using that forwarding information.

```text
        Routing Processor
               ↓
        Routing Algorithm
               ↓
         Routing Table
               ↓
       Forwarding Table
               ↓
      Fast Packet Forwarding
```

This separation prevents every packet from requiring a complete routing calculation.

---

### Q10. Differentiate between the control plane and data plane.

| Control Plane | Data Plane |
|---|---|
| Determines how traffic should be routed | Actually forwards packets |
| Runs routing protocols | Performs packet forwarding |
| Maintains routing information | Uses forwarding table |
| Handles topology changes | Handles high-rate packet processing |
| Generally less time-critical per packet | Highly performance-critical |

### Example

The control plane determines:

> "To reach network X, use interface 3."

The data plane then performs this operation for incoming packets:

> "Destination matches network X → send through interface 3."

---

# ⭐ Very Important Exam Answer

If asked:

### **"Explain the architecture of a router."**

You can write:

> A router consists primarily of **line cards, a switching fabric/backplane, and a routing processor**. Line cards perform packet reception, forwarding-related processing, buffering, and transmission. The switching fabric provides the internal path for transferring packets from ingress line cards to the appropriate egress line cards. The routing processor implements routing protocols, maintains routing information, and constructs the routing table. The forwarding information derived from routing is then used for high-speed packet forwarding.

Then draw:

```text
                         ┌─────────────────────┐
                         │ Routing Processor   │
                         │ Routing Protocols   │
                         │ Routing Table       │
                         └──────────┬──────────┘
                                    │
                                    ↓
        ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────────┐
IN ───→ │ Ingress Line │ ──→ │  Switching   │ ──→ │ Egress Line  │ ───→ OUT
        │ Card         │     │   Fabric     │     │ Card         │
        └──────────────┘     └──────────────┘     └──────────────┘
```

That combination of **definition + component functions + diagram** is a strong exam answer.

---

## 🔥 One subtle distinction to remember

Don't write:

> **Routing processor forwards every packet.**

Instead:

> **Routing processor determines/maintains routing information, while the forwarding/data plane uses forwarding information to forward packets at high speed.**

This distinction becomes very important when we reach **fast path vs slow path** later in this chapter.

# CS 606T — Advanced Computer Networks
## Topic 4: Linux PC-Based Router Performance

This is a **very important numerical topic** from your PPT. Your professor can ask direct numericals, modified numericals, conceptual questions, or "what if" scenarios.

---

# 1. Why study a Linux PC as a router?

A normal Linux PC can be configured to work as a router.

A simplified architecture is:

```text
Incoming Network
       ↓
    Input NIC
       ↓
    PCI Bus
       ↓
   Main Memory
       ↓
      CPU
       ↓
   Main Memory
       ↓
    PCI Bus
       ↓
   Output NIC
       ↓
Outgoing Network
```

The question is:

> **How fast can this PC-based router process and forward packets?**

The main performance metric is **throughput**.

---

# 2. What is Throughput?

Throughput tells us how much data the router can process and forward per unit time.

It can be expressed as:

### Packets per second

$$
\boxed{pps = packets/second}
$$

or

### Bits per second

$$
\boxed{bps = bits/second}
$$

For example:

$$
760\,Mbps
$$

means approximately 760 million bits can be processed per second.

---

# 3. Given Example from Your PPT

Your professor gives:

- PCI Bus bandwidth = **2 Gbps**
- CPU frequency = **2 GHz**
- Number of instructions = **200**
- Packet/header size = **40 bytes**

We need to calculate:

1. Time to transfer packet from NIC → memory
2. CPU processing time
3. Time to transfer memory → NIC
4. Total processing time
5. Router throughput

Let's derive it carefully.

---

# 4. Step 1 — NIC → Memory Transfer Time

Given:

$$
Packet\ size = 40\ bytes
$$

Convert bytes to bits:

$$
40\times8=320\ bits
$$

PCI bandwidth:

$$
2\,Gbps = 2\times10^9\ bits/sec
$$

Therefore:

$$
T_{transfer}=
\frac{320}{2\times10^9}
$$

$$
=160\times10^{-9}\ sec
$$

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{T_{transfer}=160\,ns}
$$

---

# 5. Step 2 — CPU Processing Time

Given:

$$
Instructions=200
$$

CPU frequency:

$$
2\,GHz=2\times10^9\ cycles/sec
$$

Assuming one instruction takes one CPU cycle in this simplified model:

$$
T_{CPU}=
\frac{200}{2\times10^9}
$$

$$
=100\,ns
$$

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{T_{CPU}=100\,ns}
$$

### Important assumption

The PPT is simplifying the calculation by treating the given instruction count as directly corresponding to CPU cycles.

In a real CPU, one instruction does not necessarily equal exactly one cycle.

---

# 6. Step 3 — Memory → Output NIC

The packet needs to travel back across the bus toward the output NIC.

Therefore, another:

$$
\boxed{160\,ns}
$$

is required.

---

# 7. Step 4 — Total Time

Total:

$$
T_{total}
=
T_{NIC\rightarrow Memory}
+
T_{CPU}
+
T_{Memory\rightarrow NIC}
$$

Therefore:

$$
T_{total}
=
160+100+160
$$

$$
\boxed{T_{total}=420\,ns}
$$

---

# 8. Step 5 — Calculate Throughput

Your PPT calculates throughput using:

$$
Throughput=
\frac{Packet\ Size}{Total\ Processing\ Time}
$$

Using:

$$
40\ bytes
$$

and:

$$
420\,ns
$$

Convert 40 bytes to bits:

$$
40\times8=320\ bits
$$

Therefore:

$$
Throughput=
\frac{320}{420\times10^{-9}}
$$

$$
\approx761.9\times10^6
$$

So:

$$
\boxed{Throughput\approx762\,Mbps}
$$

Your PPT rounds this to approximately:

$$
\boxed{760\,Mbps}
$$

---

# 9. The Important Observation

The PCI bus is:

$$
2\,Gbps
$$

But router throughput is only approximately:

$$
760\,Mbps
$$

So:

$$
760 < 2000
$$

The router cannot utilize the entire PCI bus bandwidth.

Why?

Because a significant amount of time is spent simply moving data.

From the total 420 ns:

$$
160+160=320\,ns
$$

is spent on data transfers.

Only:

$$
100\,ns
$$

is spent on CPU processing.

Therefore:

$$
\frac{320}{420}\approx76.2\%
$$

of the total time is spent transferring data in this simplified model.

---

# 10. Why is Throughput Approximately Half the Bus Bandwidth?

Your PPT makes the observation:

$$
Router\ Throughput\approx\frac{PCI\ Bus\ Bandwidth}{2}
$$

Why?

Because the packet effectively needs two bus transfers:

```text
Input NIC
   ↓
PCI Bus
   ↓
Memory
   ↓
CPU
   ↓
Memory
   ↓
PCI Bus
   ↓
Output NIC
```

So the same data crosses the bus approximately twice.

Ignoring CPU processing:

$$
Throughput\approx
\frac{2\,Gbps}{2}
$$

$$
\boxed{\approx1\,Gbps}
$$

The actual simplified example gets around **760 Mbps** because CPU processing time is also included.

---

# 11. General Formula

For this simplified model:

Let:

- $S$ = packet size in bits
- $B$ = bus bandwidth in bits/sec
- $I$ = number of instructions
- $F$ = CPU frequency

Then:

### Input transfer time

$$
T_{in}=\frac{S}{B}
$$

### CPU time

$$
T_{CPU}=\frac{I}{F}
$$

### Output transfer time

$$
T_{out}=\frac{S}{B}
$$

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{
T_{total}=
\frac{2S}{B}+\frac{I}{F}
}
$$

And throughput:

$$
\boxed{
Throughput=\frac{S}{T_{total}}
}
$$

where $S$ is in **bits**.

---

# 12. Example Variation

Suppose:

- Bus = 4 Gbps
- Packet = 80 bytes
- CPU = 2 GHz
- Instructions = 400

Then:

$$
S=80\times8=640\ bits
$$

Transfer time:

$$
T_{transfer}=
\frac{640}{4\times10^9}
=160\,ns
$$

CPU:

$$
T_{CPU}=
\frac{400}{2\times10^9}
=200\,ns
$$

Total:

$$
160+200+160=520\,ns
$$

Throughput:

$$
\frac{640}{520\times10^{-9}}
\approx1.231\,Gbps
$$

So:

$$
\boxed{Throughput\approx1.23\,Gbps}
$$

---

# 13. Very Important Conceptual Question

### What limits the throughput?

There are potentially two bottlenecks:

### 1. Bus bottleneck

If packet movement takes too much time:

$$
T_{bus}\gg T_{CPU}
$$

then the bus limits performance.

### 2. CPU bottleneck

If packet processing takes too much time:

$$
T_{CPU}\gg T_{bus}
$$

then CPU processing becomes the bottleneck.

Therefore:

> **The slowest/most time-consuming component in the packet-processing path limits throughput.**

---

# 14. How Can We Improve Linux PC Router Performance?

Your PPT gives three important solutions.

## Solution 1 — Reduce Data Copying

Instead of transferring the entire packet repeatedly:

```text
NIC → Memory → CPU → Memory → NIC
```

we can reduce the amount of data copied.

For example:

> Copy only the packet header to memory for processing and avoid unnecessary copying of the complete packet.

This reduces bus usage.

---

# 15. Solution 2 — Parallelize Packet Processing

Use:

- Multiple CPUs
- Multiple cores
- Parallel packet processing

Instead of:

```text
Packet 1
   ↓
CPU
   ↓
Packet 2
   ↓
CPU
```

we can process multiple packets concurrently.

```text
Packet 1 → Core 1
Packet 2 → Core 2
Packet 3 → Core 3
Packet 4 → Core 4
```

This can increase packet-processing capacity.

---

# 16. Solution 3 — Faster Switching Fabric

Use efficient internal data paths/backplanes.

The goal is to avoid unnecessary contention and access delays.

```text
Slow internal path
      ↓
Lower throughput

Faster parallel paths
      ↓
Higher throughput
```

This idea leads directly into the next major topic:

> **Switching Fabrics**

---

# 17. Exam-Trap Question

### Q: If the CPU frequency is doubled, will router throughput necessarily double?

**No.**

Why?

Because CPU processing is only one component of total processing time.

Suppose:

$$
T_{total}=T_{bus}+T_{CPU}
$$

If bus transfer time dominates, making the CPU faster will provide only limited improvement.

This is an example of a **bottleneck**.

---

# 18. Another Important Scenario

Suppose:

```text
CPU processing = 20 ns
Bus transfers = 400 ns
```

Then:

$$
T_{total}=420\,ns
$$

If CPU processing becomes:

$$
20\rightarrow10\,ns
$$

new total:

$$
410\,ns
$$

Only a small improvement occurs.

But if we reduce bus transfer time:

$$
400\rightarrow200\,ns
$$

then:

$$
T_{total}=220\,ns
$$

Much larger improvement.

Therefore:

> **Optimize the actual bottleneck, not necessarily the fastest component.**

---

# 19. What You Should Remember for the Exam

Memorize this flow:

```text
NIC
 ↓
PCI Bus
 ↓
Memory
 ↓
CPU
 ↓
Memory
 ↓
PCI Bus
 ↓
NIC
```

And this formula:

$$
\boxed{
T_{total}=
\frac{2S}{B}+\frac{I}{F}
}
$$

Then:

$$
\boxed{
Throughput=
\frac{S}{T_{total}}
}
$$

where $S$ is packet size in bits.

---

## 🎯 Likely Exam Questions

This topic can produce:

1. **Direct numerical** — calculate total packet processing time.
2. **Throughput numerical** — calculate Mbps/Gbps.
3. **Modified numerical** — change CPU/bus/packet size.
4. **Bottleneck question** — identify whether CPU or bus is limiting.
5. **Conceptual** — why throughput is lower than PCI bandwidth.
6. **Optimization scenario** — what happens if CPU is doubled?
7. **Design question** — suggest ways to improve performance.
8. **Derivation** — derive the throughput formula.
9. **Comparison** — two PC-router configurations, determine which is faster.
10. **Trick numerical** — unit conversions between bytes, bits, ns, GHz, and Gbps.

**Next topic after this is Switching Fabrics**, which is one of the most important sections of this chapter: **shared backplanes, switched backplanes, crossbar switches, scheduling, HOL blocking, output queuing, VOQ, and Parallel Iterative Matching (PIM).**
