# CS 606T — Advanced Computer Networks
## Topic 5: Switching Fabrics

This is a **high-weight topic** in your PPT because it combines architecture, packet movement, scheduling, performance, and several important numerical/conceptual questions.

---

# 1. What is a Switching Fabric?

A **switching fabric** is the internal mechanism of a router that provides a path between an **ingress line card** and an **egress line card**.

Think of it as the router's internal transportation system.

```text
                    ROUTER
        ┌─────────────────────────────┐
        │                             │
Packet →│ Ingress Line Card           │
        │        │                    │
        │        ↓                    │
        │  ┌───────────────┐          │
        │  │   Switching   │          │
        │  │    Fabric     │          │
        │  └───────┬───────┘          │
        │          ↓                  │
        │  Egress Line Card           │→ Packet
        │                             │
        └─────────────────────────────┘
```

It is also called:

> **Switching Backplane**

---

# 2. Where Does Switching Fabric Fit?

Recall the packet path:

```text
Incoming Packet
      ↓
Ingress Line Card
      ↓
Forwarding Lookup
      ↓
Switching Fabric
      ↓
Egress Line Card
      ↓
Output Queue
      ↓
Outgoing Link
```

The ingress line card determines **where the packet needs to go**.

The switching fabric provides the **internal path to that destination**.

---

# 3. Main Functions of Switching Fabric

A good switching fabric should:

### 1. Transfer packets between line cards

```text
Input → Fabric → Output
```

### 2. Support multiple transfers simultaneously

Ideally:

```text
Input 1 → Output 1
Input 2 → Output 2
Input 3 → Output 3
```

at the same time.

### 3. Provide fair bandwidth allocation

No input/output should be unfairly starved.

### 4. Avoid unnecessary packet reordering

Packets belonging to the same flow should ideally maintain their order.

### 5. Support priority

Higher-priority traffic should receive appropriate service.

---

# 4. Three Primary Performance Metrics

Your PPT identifies three major metrics:

$$
\boxed{Throughput}
$$

$$
\boxed{Latency}
$$

$$
\boxed{Path\ Diversity}
$$

Let's understand them.

---

## Throughput

How much traffic can the switching fabric transfer per unit time?

Example:

$$
100\,Gbps
$$

means the fabric can transfer approximately 100 billion bits per second under the relevant conditions.

---

## Latency

How long does it take to move a packet through the fabric?

Lower latency is generally desirable for delay-sensitive traffic.

---

## Path Diversity

The ability to provide multiple internal paths between inputs and outputs.

More path diversity can help:

- parallel transfers
- load distribution
- fault tolerance

---

# 5. Switching Fabric Architecture

Your PPT describes four important components.

```text
                 SWITCHING FABRIC
        ┌─────────────────────────────┐
        │                             │
Input → │ Fabric Input Interface     │
        │        ↓                    │
        │ Fabric Input Buffer        │
        │        ↓                    │
        │      Scheduler              │
        │        ↓                    │
        │ Fabric Output Interface    │
        │        ↓                    │
        │ Fabric Output Buffer       │
        │                             │
        └─────────────────────────────┘
```

---

# 6. Fabric Input Interface

The **fabric input interface** connects the ingress processing portion of the line card to the switching fabric.

It also coordinates with the scheduler regarding packet availability.

In simple terms:

> It tells the fabric that packets are waiting to be transferred.

---

# 7. Fabric Input Buffer

Packets may arrive faster than the switching fabric can immediately transfer them.

Therefore, temporary storage is required.

That is the:

> **Fabric input buffer**

```text
Packets arrive
      ↓
Input Buffer
      ↓
Waiting for fabric
```

The buffer helps prevent immediate packet loss when the fabric cannot serve a packet immediately.

---

# 8. Fabric Output Interface

After the fabric transfers the packet, the output interface receives it and passes it toward the egress processing module.

```text
Switching Fabric
       ↓
Output Interface
       ↓
Egress Line Card
```

---

# 9. Fabric Output Buffer

The packet may arrive at the output faster than the outgoing interface can transmit it.

Therefore, it may need to wait.

The:

> **Fabric output buffer**

stores such packets temporarily.

```text
Fabric
  ↓
Output Buffer
  ↓
Transmission
```

---

# 10. Packet Flow Through a Router

Let's combine everything.

### Ingress

The incoming packet is processed:

- IP header located
- Header validated
- Relevant information extracted
- Forwarding lookup performed
- Packet classification may occur
- Packet placed in appropriate buffer

Then:

```text
Ingress
   ↓
Forwarding Engine
   ↓
Input Buffer
```

---

# 11. Scheduling

Now comes a very important component:

> **Scheduler**

The scheduler decides:

> Which input should communicate with which output during a given time slot?

For example:

```text
Inputs              Outputs

I1 ───────────────→ O2
I2 ───────────────→ O4
I3 ───────────────→ O1
I4 ───────────────→ O3
```

The scheduler creates these connections.

---

# 12. What is a Timeslot?

A **timeslot** is a fixed interval during which a particular set of input-output transfers occurs.

For example:

```text
Timeslot 1:
I1 → O1
I2 → O3

Timeslot 2:
I1 → O2
I3 → O4

Timeslot 3:
I2 → O1
I4 → O3
```

The scheduler determines the configuration for each timeslot.

---

# 13. Shared Backplane

Now we come to the first major type of switching fabric.

## Shared Backplane

A shared backplane uses a **shared medium** to transfer packets between line cards.

Example:

```text
        Input 1
           │
        Input 2
           │
        Input 3
           │
           ↓
     ┌───────────┐
     │Shared Bus │
     └───────────┘
       │   │   │
       ↓   ↓   ↓
      O1  O2  O3
```

All line cards share the same internal resource.

---

# 14. Limitation of Shared Backplane

Because the medium is shared:

> Multiple transfers cannot freely occur simultaneously on the same shared resource.

Therefore, aggregate throughput is limited.

Shared backplanes are typically associated with lower-bandwidth router designs.

Example:

> **Shared bus**

---

# 15. Switched Backplane

A **switched backplane** allows multiple transfers to occur simultaneously.

```text
I1 ─────→ O1
I2 ─────→ O2
I3 ─────→ O3
```

This increases aggregate throughput.

The key component is:

> **Scheduler**

The scheduler determines which input-output pairs are connected during each timeslot.

---

# 16. Shared vs Switched Backplane

| Shared Backplane | Switched Backplane |
|---|---|
| Shared medium | Multiple internal paths |
| Limited simultaneous transfers | Supports parallel transfers |
| Lower aggregate throughput | Higher aggregate throughput |
| Simpler | More complex |
| Example: shared bus | Example: crossbar |

---

# 17. Crossbar Switch

This is one of the most important concepts.

An:

$$
\boxed{N\times N}
$$

crossbar has:

- N input buses
- N output buses
- $N^2$ crosspoints

For example:

### 4 × 4 crossbar

$$
4^2=16
$$

crosspoints.

```text
             Outputs
          O1  O2  O3  O4
          │   │   │   │
I1 ───────●───●───●───●
I2 ───────●───●───●───●
I3 ───────●───●───●───●
I4 ───────●───●───●───●
```

Each dot is a crosspoint.

---

# 18. What is a Crosspoint?

A crosspoint represents a possible connection between:

$$
Input_i
$$

and:

$$
Output_j
$$

For example:

$$
(i,j)=(2,3)
$$

means:

> Input 2 can be connected to Output 3.

The crosspoint can be:

- ON
- OFF

---

# 19. Why is Crossbar Fast?

Suppose:

```text
I1 → O1
I2 → O2
I3 → O3
I4 → O4
```

These can occur simultaneously.

Therefore:

$$
4\text{ packets}
$$

can potentially be transferred during one timeslot.

This is much better than a single shared bus where transfers compete for a shared medium.

---

# 20. But Crossbar Has a Problem

Suppose:

```text
I1 → O1
I2 → O1
I3 → O3
I4 → O4
```

Both I1 and I2 want the same output:

```text
        → O1
I1 ─────┤
I2 ─────┘
```

But O1 can normally receive only one packet per timeslot.

Therefore:

```text
I1 → O1  ✓
I2 → O1  ✗
```

One packet must wait.

This creates contention.

---

# 21. Another Problem — Idle Inputs

Suppose only:

```text
I1 → O1
```

has traffic.

Then:

```text
I2 → nothing
I3 → nothing
I4 → nothing
```

Their available transmission opportunities remain unused.

Therefore, the crossbar may have unused capacity.

This contributes to lower utilization.

---

# 22. Why Do We Need a Scheduler?

The scheduler decides which connections should be active.

For example:

```text
Requests:

I1 → O1
I2 → O1
I3 → O3
I4 → O4
```

Scheduler might choose:

```text
I1 → O1
I3 → O3
I4 → O4
```

and reject/postpone:

```text
I2 → O1
```

for this timeslot.

---

# 23. Important Constraint

In a basic crossbar schedule:

> An input cannot simultaneously send to multiple outputs during the same timeslot.

And:

> An output cannot simultaneously receive from multiple inputs during the same timeslot.

Therefore, a valid schedule is essentially a set of **non-conflicting input-output matches**.

---

# 24. Crossbar Utilization

Suppose:

$$
N=4
$$

Then there are 4 possible transfers per timeslot.

Suppose over 3 timeslots:

$$
Maximum\ opportunities=3\times4=12
$$

but only 8 packets are actually transferred.

Utilization:

$$
\frac{8}{12}\times100
$$

$$
\boxed{66.67\%}
$$

This type of numerical can easily appear in an exam.

---

# 25. Why Can Utilization Be Low?

Your PPT identifies:

1. **Head-of-Line Blocking**
2. **Input Blocking**
3. **Output Blocking**
4. Idle inputs/outputs
5. Contention for the same output

The most important one is:

> **HOL — Head-of-Line Blocking**

---

# 26. Head-of-Line Blocking

Suppose every input has a FIFO queue.

```text
Input 1:
[P1 → O1] [P2 → O2] [P3 → O3]

Input 2:
[P4 → O2] [P5 → O3]

Input 3:
[P6 → O3]
```

The first packet in each queue is the **head-of-line packet**.

Suppose:

```text
P1 → O1
P4 → O2
P6 → O3
```

Everything works.

But consider:

```text
Input 1:
[P1 → O1] [P2 → O2]

Input 2:
[P3 → O1] [P4 → O3]
```

Suppose P1 wins access to O1.

Then P3 cannot use O1 in the same timeslot.

But more importantly:

```text
Input 2:
[P3 → O1] [P4 → O3]
```

P4 wants O3, which is free.

However, because P3 is stuck at the **head** of the FIFO queue, P4 cannot move forward.

Therefore:

> A packet at the head of a queue can block packets behind it even when their desired outputs are free.

This is **Head-of-Line Blocking**.

---

# 27. Why is HOL Blocking Bad?

Because it reduces switch utilization.

Some output ports may remain idle even though packets are waiting at input queues that could otherwise use those outputs.

```text
Output O3 = FREE

But:

Input queue:
[P1 → O1] [P2 → O3]

P1 is blocked
      ↓
P2 cannot move
      ↓
O3 remains idle
```

This wastes available switching capacity.

---

# 28. Three Important Queueing Strategies

To address HOL blocking, we study:

### 1. Input Queueing

Queues at input ports.

### 2. Output Queueing

Queues at output ports.

### 3. Virtual Output Queueing — VOQ

Multiple queues at each input, one for each output.

These are extremely important for exams.

---

# 29. Input Queueing

Each input has one FIFO queue.

```text
Input 1 → [P1][P2][P3]
Input 2 → [P4][P5][P6]
Input 3 → [P7][P8][P9]
```

Simple to implement.

But:

> **It suffers from HOL blocking.**

---

# 30. Output Queueing

Instead of queues at inputs, packets are immediately transferred toward their destination output queues.

```text
             ┌→ O1 Queue
Inputs ──────┼→ O2 Queue
             └→ O3 Queue
```

Now packets don't get stuck behind packets destined for another output at the input.

Therefore:

> **HOL blocking is eliminated.**

---

# 31. Problem with Output Queueing

Suppose there are:

$$
N
$$

input ports.

All N inputs simultaneously send packets to the same output.

Then:

```text
I1 ─┐
I2 ─┤
I3 ─┤──→ O1
... │
IN ─┘
```

The output queue must receive N packets in the time normally needed for one packet arrival.

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{\text{Switch fabric must operate approximately }N\text{ times faster}}
$$

than the input line rate in the worst case.

---

# 32. Example

Suppose:

$$
N=4
$$

and every input link operates at:

$$
1\,Gbps
$$

Then the output side may need to absorb:

$$
4\times1=4\,Gbps
$$

in the worst case.

Thus the switching fabric/output queue would need very high speed.

---

# 33. Output Queueing — Pros and Cons

### Advantages

- Eliminates HOL blocking.
- Theoretically allows 100% throughput under appropriate conditions.

### Disadvantages

- Fabric may need to operate N times faster.
- Output queues need extremely high-speed operation.
- Expensive/difficult to implement.

---

# 34. Virtual Output Queueing — VOQ

VOQ is a very important solution.

Instead of:

```text
Input 1:
[P1][P2][P3]
```

we create separate queues based on destination output.

For a 4-output switch:

```text
Input 1:

VOQ1 → packets for O1
VOQ2 → packets for O2
VOQ3 → packets for O3
VOQ4 → packets for O4
```

Diagram:

```text
                Input 1
             ┌────────────┐
             │ VOQ → O1   │
             │ VOQ → O2   │
             │ VOQ → O3   │
             │ VOQ → O4   │
             └────────────┘
```

---

# 35. Why Does VOQ Solve HOL Blocking?

Consider:

```text
Input 1:

VOQ1: [P1 → O1]
VOQ2: [P2 → O2]
```

Suppose O1 is currently busy.

With a single FIFO:

```text
[P1 → O1] [P2 → O2]
```

P2 could be blocked by P1.

With VOQ:

```text
VOQ1 → O1  BLOCKED
VOQ2 → O2  FREE
```

The scheduler can select P2.

Therefore:

> Packets destined for different outputs don't block one another merely because they arrived behind another packet.

---

# 36. Number of VOQs

For an:

$$
N\times N
$$

switch:

Each input needs:

$$
N
$$

queues.

There are:

$$
N
$$

inputs.

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{N^2}
$$

total VOQs.

### Example

For a:

$$
8\times8
$$

switch:

$$
8^2=64
$$

VOQs.

---

# 37. VOQ Advantages

### Advantage 1

Eliminates HOL blocking in the basic input-queueing architecture.

### Advantage 2

Can theoretically achieve:

$$
\boxed{100\%}
$$

throughput under suitable assumptions with an appropriate scheduler.

### Advantage 3

Doesn't require the switching fabric to run N times faster like pure output queueing.

---

# 38. VOQ Disadvantage

The major disadvantage is:

> **Scheduler complexity.**

The scheduler must examine many queues and determine which input-output pairs can be simultaneously matched.

For an:

$$
N\times N
$$

switch, there are:

$$
N^2
$$

VOQs.

As N increases, scheduling becomes increasingly difficult.

---

# 39. Enter PIM

This leads us to:

> **Parallel Iterative Matching — PIM**

PIM is a scheduling algorithm designed to quickly find a large set of valid input-output matches.

The basic goal is:

> Maximize the number of packets transferred during each timeslot while respecting input/output conflicts.

---

# 40. Basic Idea of PIM

Suppose:

```text
Inputs:
I1 I2 I3 I4

Outputs:
O1 O2 O3 O4
```

There are many requests:

```text
I1 → O1
I1 → O2
I2 → O1
I3 → O3
I4 → O4
```

The scheduler needs to select a conflict-free set.

For example:

```text
I1 → O2
I2 → O1
I3 → O3
I4 → O4
```

All four transfers can happen simultaneously.

PIM uses multiple iterations to converge toward a large/maximal matching quickly.

---

# 41. Why "Parallel"?

Because matching decisions for multiple inputs/outputs can be considered in parallel rather than processing every request sequentially.

This makes PIM suitable for high-speed switches.

---

# 42. Why "Iterative"?

One iteration may not produce a sufficiently good matching.

So the scheduler repeats the matching process:

```text
Requests
   ↓
Iteration 1
   ↓
Partial Matching
   ↓
Iteration 2
   ↓
Improved Matching
   ↓
...
   ↓
Final Matching
```

The objective is to obtain a large/maximal matching within the available scheduling time.

---

# 43. The Big Picture

You should understand the evolution:

```text
Input Queueing
      ↓
HOL Blocking
      ↓
Problem
      ↓
Output Queueing
      ↓
HOL eliminated
      ↓
But requires N× speed
      ↓
Problem
      ↓
Virtual Output Queueing
      ↓
HOL eliminated
      ↓
But scheduler becomes complex
      ↓
PIM
      ↓
Fast scheduling / large matching
```

This chain is **very important for theory questions**.

---

# 44. Important Comparison Table

| Architecture | HOL Blocking | Speed Requirement | Complexity |
|---|---|---|---|
| Input Queueing | Yes | Normal | Low |
| Output Queueing | No | ~N× input speed | High hardware speed |
| VOQ | No | Normal-ish fabric speed | High scheduler complexity |

---

# 45. Exam-Level Conceptual Question

### Why doesn't output queueing suffer from HOL blocking?

Because packets are not forced to wait behind packets destined for another output at the same input FIFO queue.

They are directly placed into the queue corresponding to their destination output.

---

# 46. Another Exam Trap

### Is VOQ the same as output queueing?

**No.**

### Output Queueing

Queues are physically maintained at:

```text
OUTPUT
```

### VOQ

Multiple destination-specific queues are maintained at:

```text
INPUT
```

Remember:

$$
\boxed{VOQ =\ Virtual\ Output\ Queues\ at\ Inputs}
$$

---

# 47. What You Must Know Before Moving On

For this topic, make sure you understand:

### Fundamentals
- Switching fabric
- Backplane
- Throughput
- Latency
- Path diversity

### Architecture
- Fabric input interface
- Fabric input buffer
- Fabric scheduler
- Fabric output interface
- Fabric output buffer

### Fabric types
- Shared backplane
- Switched backplane

### Crossbar
- $N\times N$
- $N^2$ crosspoints
- simultaneous transfers
- scheduler
- contention

### Queueing
- Input queueing
- HOL blocking
- Output queueing
- VOQ

### Scheduling
- Timeslots
- Matching
- Parallel Iterative Matching

---

## ⭐ Most Important Formulae

For an:

$$
N\times N
$$

crossbar:

$$
\boxed{\text{Number of crosspoints}=N^2}
$$

For VOQ:

$$
\boxed{\text{Number of VOQs}=N^2}
$$

Worst-case output queueing speed requirement:

$$
\boxed{\text{Fabric speed}\approx N\times\text{input line rate}}
$$

Utilization:

$$
\boxed{
Utilization=
\frac{\text{Actual transfers}}
{\text{Maximum possible transfers}}
\times100\%
}
$$

---

The **next subtopic in your PPT is specifically the Crossbar scheduling/utilization and HOL Blocking section**, including the **throughput under HOL blocking and PIM**. We should solve those numericals and exam-style scenarios before considering this chapter section complete.

## CS 606T — Advanced Computer Networks
# Topic 6: Crossbar Scheduling, HOL Blocking & PIM

We have already covered the architecture of a crossbar. Now let's go deeper into **how packets are actually scheduled**, why utilization becomes low, and how **Parallel Iterative Matching (PIM)** addresses the problem.

---

# 1. Why Do We Need Scheduling?

Consider a 4 × 4 crossbar:

```text
             Outputs
          O1  O2  O3  O4
          │   │   │   │
I1 ───────┼───┼───┼───┼
I2 ───────┼───┼───┼───┼
I3 ───────┼───┼───┼───┼
I4 ───────┼───┼───┼───┼
```

Suppose packets request:

```text
I1 → O1
I2 → O1
I3 → O2
I4 → O3
```

We have a conflict:

```text
I1 ──→ O1
I2 ──→ O1
```

Both cannot use O1 during the same timeslot.

Therefore, a **scheduler** must decide which connections are allowed.

---

# 2. What is a Match?

A **match** is a selected input-output pair.

For example:

```text
I1 → O1
I3 → O2
I4 → O3
```

is a valid match because:

- I1 is used once
- I3 is used once
- I4 is used once
- O1 is used once
- O2 is used once
- O3 is used once

So three packets can be transferred simultaneously.

---

# 3. What is a Maximal Matching?

A matching is **maximal** if you cannot add another valid input-output pair without violating the constraints.

Example:

```text
I1 → O1
I2 → O2
I3 → O3
```

If I4 and O4 are both available and:

```text
I4 → O4
```

is possible, then the previous matching wasn't maximal.

Add it:

```text
I1 → O1
I2 → O2
I3 → O3
I4 → O4
```

Now no additional pair can be added.

---

# 4. Maximum vs Maximal Matching

This is an important theoretical distinction.

### Maximum matching

A matching containing the **largest possible number of pairs**.

### Maximal matching

A matching to which **no additional pair can be added**.

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{\text{Maximum} \Rightarrow \text{Maximal}}
$$

but:

$$
\boxed{\text{Maximal} \not\Rightarrow \text{Maximum}}
$$

A maximal matching doesn't necessarily contain the maximum possible number of matches.

---

# 5. Example

Suppose requests are:

```text
I1 → O1
I1 → O2
I2 → O1
I2 → O2
```

One possible matching:

```text
I1 → O1
```

This is **not maximal**, because we can add:

```text
I2 → O2
```

So:

```text
I1 → O1
I2 → O2
```

is maximal and also maximum.

---

# 6. What is the Scheduler Trying to Achieve?

Ideally:

> Transfer as many packets as possible during every timeslot.

For an $N\times N$ switch:

Maximum number of simultaneous transfers:

$$
\boxed{N}
$$

provided that there are enough packets and no conflicts.

For a 4 × 4 switch:

$$
\boxed{4}
$$

packets can potentially be transferred per timeslot.

---

# 7. Crossbar Utilization

Suppose a 4 × 4 crossbar operates for 3 timeslots.

Maximum possible transfers:

$$
4\times3=12
$$

Suppose only 8 packets are actually transferred.

Then:

$$
Utilization=
\frac{8}{12}\times100
$$

$$
\boxed{66.67\%}
$$

This is exactly the type of calculation your PPT illustrates.

---

# 8. Another Example

A 6 × 6 switch operates for 10 timeslots.

Maximum possible transfers:

$$
6\times10=60
$$

Suppose actual transfers:

$$
45
$$

Then:

$$
Utilization=
\frac{45}{60}\times100
$$

$$
\boxed{75\%}
$$

---

# 9. Why Isn't Utilization 100%?

There are several reasons:

- Multiple inputs request the same output.
- Some inputs don't have packets.
- Some outputs don't have packets destined for them.
- HOL blocking.
- Scheduling algorithm may not find the best possible matching.

The most important problem with simple input FIFO queues is:

$$
\boxed{\text{Head-of-Line Blocking}}
$$

---

# 10. HOL Blocking — Detailed Example

Consider two input queues:

```text
Input 1:
[P1 → O1] [P2 → O2]

Input 2:
[P3 → O1] [P4 → O3]
```

Suppose:

```text
O1 is requested by P1 and P3
```

Only one can win.

Assume:

```text
P1 → O1
```

is selected.

Then P3 waits.

But look at Input 2:

```text
[P3 → O1] [P4 → O3]
```

P4 wants O3.

O3 is free.

But P4 cannot move because P3 is at the head.

Therefore:

```text
P3 blocks P4
```

even though:

```text
O3 is available
```

This is HOL blocking.

---

# 11. The Core Idea of HOL Blocking

Remember this sentence for the exam:

> **A packet at the head of an input FIFO queue can prevent packets behind it from being transmitted even when their desired output is available.**

---

# 12. Why HOL Reduces Throughput

Suppose a switch has:

```text
I1 → O1
I2 → O1
I3 → O3
```

If I1 wins O1:

```text
I1 → O1 ✓
I2 → O1 ✗
I3 → O3 ✓
```

Fine.

But now suppose:

```text
I2:
[P → O1] [Q → O3]
```

P is blocked.

Therefore Q cannot use O3.

So:

```text
O3 = idle
```

even though there is a packet waiting for it.

This is wasted capacity.

---

# 13. Input Blocking vs Output Blocking

### Input blocking

An input cannot send a packet because another packet/constraint prevents it.

HOL is an important example.

### Output blocking

An output cannot accept multiple packets simultaneously.

Example:

```text
I1 ─┐
I2 ─┤
I3 ─┼──→ O1
I4 ─┘
```

All four want O1.

But only one can normally be served in a timeslot.

---

# 14. Solution 1 — Output Queueing

Instead of:

```text
Input → FIFO
```

we use:

```text
Output → Queue
```

Packets are transferred toward their destination output queues.

This removes the HOL problem because packets destined for different outputs don't wait behind each other at the input.

---

# 15. Output Queueing Numerical

Consider:

$$
N=4
$$

Each input operates at:

$$
1\,Gbps
$$

Worst case:

```text
I1 ─┐
I2 ─┤
I3 ─┼──→ O1
I4 ─┘
```

The output may receive:

$$
4\times1=4\,Gbps
$$

Therefore, the fabric may need approximately:

$$
\boxed{4\,Gbps}
$$

capacity.

General formula:

$$
\boxed{Fabric\ speed\approx N\times input\ rate}
$$

---

# 16. Solution 2 — Virtual Output Queueing

Instead of one FIFO queue at each input:

```text
Input 1:
[P1][P2][P3][P4]
```

create:

```text
Input 1:

VOQ1 → packets for O1
VOQ2 → packets for O2
VOQ3 → packets for O3
VOQ4 → packets for O4
```

Now packets for different outputs can be independently scheduled.

---

# 17. VOQ Example

Suppose:

```text
Input 1:

VOQ1: P1 → O1
VOQ2: P2 → O2

Input 2:

VOQ1: P3 → O1
VOQ3: P4 → O3
```

Suppose O1 is busy.

The scheduler can still select:

```text
P2 → O2
P4 → O3
```

instead of forcing them to wait behind packets destined for O1.

---

# 18. Number of VOQs

For an:

$$
N\times N
$$

switch:

Each input has:

$$
N
$$

VOQs.

There are N inputs.

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{N^2}
$$

total VOQs.

Example:

### 16 × 16 switch

$$
16^2=256
$$

VOQs.

---

# 19. The New Problem with VOQ

VOQ solves HOL blocking, but now the scheduler has a much harder problem.

It must examine:

$$
N^2
$$

queues and determine a conflict-free matching very quickly.

For a high-speed router, this decision may have to be made for every timeslot.

Therefore:

> **Scheduler complexity becomes the major challenge.**

This is where **PIM** becomes important.

---

# 20. Parallel Iterative Matching — PIM

PIM stands for:

$$
\boxed{\text{Parallel Iterative Matching}}
$$

Its objective is:

> Find a large set of conflict-free input-output matches quickly.

The key ideas are:

- Parallel
- Iterative
- Matching

---

# 21. Basic PIM Process

Conceptually:

```text
Requests
   ↓
Iteration 1
   ↓
Initial matches
   ↓
Iteration 2
   ↓
Additional/improved matches
   ↓
Iteration 3
   ↓
Better matching
   ↓
Final schedule
```

The scheduler performs multiple iterations within the scheduling period.

---

# 22. Why Not Simply Calculate the Perfect Matching?

Because routers operate at extremely high speed.

Imagine an enormous:

$$
N\times N
$$

switch.

Computing a mathematically optimal matching from scratch for every timeslot may be too expensive or slow.

PIM instead tries to:

> Quickly obtain a good/maximal matching.

This gives a practical trade-off between:

- scheduling quality
- scheduling speed

---

# 23. PIM — High-Level Operation

A simplified view:

### Step 1 — Request

Each input requests outputs for which it has packets.

Example:

```text
I1 → O1, O2
I2 → O1
I3 → O2, O3
```

### Step 2 — Grant

Outputs consider requests and grant one of them.

### Step 3 — Accept

Inputs choose among grants.

### Step 4 — Repeat

The process can be repeated for additional iterations.

The exact PIM algorithm contains randomized selection mechanisms to avoid persistent unfairness.

---

# 24. Why Multiple Iterations?

Imagine the first iteration produces:

```text
I1 → O1
I3 → O3
```

but:

```text
I2 → O2
```

wasn't selected.

Another iteration can examine remaining unmatched requests and potentially establish:

```text
I2 → O2
```

Thus more available capacity can be utilized.

---

# 25. PIM and Throughput

The goal is to approach:

$$
\boxed{100\%}
$$

throughput when traffic conditions permit.

But be careful:

> Don't write that PIM **always guarantees** 100% throughput.

The scheduling algorithm, traffic pattern, implementation, and timing constraints matter.

For an exam, say:

> PIM attempts to achieve a high-throughput, near-maximal matching at high speed.

---

# 26. Important Comparison

| Feature | Input Queue | Output Queue | VOQ |
|---|---|---|---|
| Queue location | Input | Output | Input |
| Single FIFO? | Yes | Output-based | No, multiple queues |
| HOL blocking | Yes | No | Eliminated |
| Fabric speed | Normal | ~N× | Normal-ish |
| Scheduler complexity | Lower | Different architecture | High |
| Scalability | Better implementation simplicity | Hardware speed challenge | Scheduler challenge |

---

# 27. Numerical Question

### Question

An 8 × 8 crossbar operates for 20 timeslots. The maximum number of packet transfers per timeslot is 8. If 136 packets are successfully transferred, calculate utilization.

Maximum possible:

$$
8\times20=160
$$

Actual:

$$
136
$$

Therefore:

$$
Utilization=
\frac{136}{160}\times100
$$

$$
\boxed{85\%}
$$

---

# 28. Scenario-Based Question

### Scenario

A router has 32 input ports and 32 output ports. Each input maintains a single FIFO queue. Packets behind a blocked packet cannot be transmitted even if their destinations are free.

### Identify the problem.

$$
\boxed{\text{HOL Blocking}}
$$

### Which architecture can solve this?

$$
\boxed{\text{Virtual Output Queueing}}
$$

### How many VOQs?

$$
32^2
$$

$$
\boxed{1024}
$$

---

# 29. Another Scenario

A router designer wants to eliminate HOL blocking by using output queues.

There are 16 input ports, each operating at 10 Gbps.

Worst-case aggregate arrival rate at one output:

$$
16\times10
$$

$$
\boxed{160\,Gbps}
$$

So the output-side fabric/queueing system may need to sustain approximately:

$$
\boxed{160\,Gbps}
$$

in the worst case.

---

# 30. The Complete Conceptual Chain

This is probably the **most important thing to understand from today's topic**:

```text
              CROSSBAR
                 │
                 ↓
          Input Queueing
                 │
                 ↓
          HOL Blocking
                 │
                 ↓
       Reduced Utilization
                 │
        ┌────────┴────────┐
        ↓                 ↓
 Output Queueing          VOQ
        │                 │
        ↓                 ↓
   No HOL Blocking    No HOL Blocking
        │                 │
        ↓                 ↓
    N× speed         Complex Scheduler
                          │
                          ↓
                         PIM
                          │
                          ↓
             Fast Conflict-Free Matching
```

---

# ⭐ What You Should Be Able to Answer

After this topic, you should be comfortable with questions such as:

1. What is a crossbar switch?
2. Why does an $N\times N$ crossbar have $N^2$ crosspoints?
3. What is a timeslot?
4. Why is scheduling required?
5. What is a matching?
6. Difference between maximal and maximum matching.
7. Calculate crossbar utilization.
8. Explain HOL blocking with an example.
9. Why does input queueing suffer from HOL blocking?
10. Why does output queueing eliminate HOL blocking?
11. Why does output queueing require an $N$-times faster fabric?
12. What is VOQ?
13. Why does VOQ eliminate HOL blocking?
14. How many VOQs are required for an $N\times N$ switch?
15. What is the drawback of VOQ?
16. What is PIM?
17. Why does PIM use multiple iterations?
18. Compare input queueing, output queueing and VOQ.
19. Solve throughput/utilization numericals.
20. Analyze a router design scenario and select the appropriate queueing architecture.

**This completes the Crossbar → HOL → Output Queueing → VOQ → PIM portion of the switching-fabric material you provided.** The next topic in the PPT sequence is **Fast Path vs Slow Path**, followed by the remaining router architecture concepts.

Sure. Here are **exam-ready answers to all 20 questions** from the Crossbar Scheduling, HOL Blocking, Output Queueing, VOQ and PIM topic.

# CS 606T — Switching Fabrics
## 20 Important Questions with Answers

### Q1. What is a Crossbar Switch?

**Answer:**  
A crossbar switch is a switched-backplane architecture that provides direct connections between input and output ports through controllable crosspoints.

For an $N\times N$ crossbar:

- $N$ input ports
- $N$ output ports
- $N^2$ crosspoints

A scheduler determines which input-output connections are active during each timeslot.

---

### Q2. Why does an $N\times N$ crossbar have $N^2$ crosspoints?

**Answer:**

Each of the $N$ inputs can potentially connect to each of the $N$ outputs.

Therefore:

$$
N\times N=N^2
$$

Hence,

$$
\boxed{\text{Crosspoints}=N^2}
$$

Example:

For a $4\times4$ crossbar:

$$
4^2=16
$$

crosspoints.

---

### Q3. What is a timeslot?

**Answer:**  
A **timeslot** is a fixed time interval during which the scheduler establishes a particular set of input-output connections and packets are transferred across the switching fabric.

For example:

```text
Timeslot 1:
I1 → O1
I2 → O3

Timeslot 2:
I1 → O2
I3 → O4
```

The connections can change from one timeslot to another.

---

### Q4. Why is scheduling required in a crossbar?

**Answer:**  
Scheduling is required because multiple inputs may request the same output, or an input may request multiple outputs simultaneously.

For example:

```text
I1 → O1
I2 → O1
```

Both cannot normally transmit to $O1$ in the same timeslot.

The scheduler selects a **conflict-free set of input-output connections**.

Its goal is to maximize the number of simultaneous transfers while maintaining input/output constraints.

---

### Q5. What is a matching?

**Answer:**  
A **matching** is a set of input-output connections in which:

- Each input is connected to at most one output.
- Each output receives from at most one input.

Example:

```text
I1 → O1
I2 → O3
I4 → O2
```

is a valid matching because no input or output is used more than once.

---

### Q6. Difference between maximal and maximum matching.

**Answer:**

| Maximum Matching | Maximal Matching |
|---|---|
| Contains the largest possible number of matches | Cannot add another valid match |
| Globally largest | Locally cannot be extended |
| Always maximal | May not be maximum |

Important relationship:

$$
\boxed{\text{Maximum matching is always maximal}}
$$

but:

$$
\boxed{\text{Maximal matching is not necessarily maximum}}
$$

---

### Q7. Calculate crossbar utilization.

**Question:**  
A $4\times4$ crossbar operates for 3 timeslots. A maximum of 4 packets can be transferred per timeslot. If 8 packets are actually transferred, calculate utilization.

**Answer:**

Maximum possible transfers:

$$
4\times3=12
$$

Actual transfers:

$$
8
$$

Therefore:

$$
Utilization=
\frac{8}{12}\times100
$$

$$
\boxed{66.67\%}
$$

---

### Q8. Explain HOL blocking with an example.

**Answer:**  
**Head-of-Line (HOL) blocking** occurs when a packet at the front of an input FIFO queue prevents packets behind it from being transmitted, even when those packets are destined for currently available outputs.

Example:

```text
Input 1:
[P1 → O1] [P2 → O2]
```

Suppose $O1$ is busy.

Then $P1$ cannot transmit.

Although $O2$ is free, $P2$ cannot move because it is behind $P1$.

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{P1\text{ blocks }P2}
$$

This is HOL blocking.

---

### Q9. Why does input queueing suffer from HOL blocking?

**Answer:**  
In input queueing, packets are commonly stored in a single FIFO queue at each input.

Because FIFO requires packets to be processed in order, a blocked head packet prevents packets behind it from being considered, even if their destination outputs are free.

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{\text{Input FIFO} \rightarrow \text{HOL Blocking}}
$$

---

### Q10. Why does output queueing eliminate HOL blocking?

**Answer:**  
In output queueing, packets are placed into queues associated with their destination outputs.

Therefore, packets destined for different outputs do not have to wait behind one another in a single input FIFO queue.

Example:

```text
P1 → O1
P2 → O2
```

If $O1$ is busy, $P2$ can still enter the queue for $O2$.

Thus:

$$
\boxed{\text{Output Queueing eliminates HOL blocking}}
$$

---

### Q11. Why does output queueing require an $N$-times faster fabric?

**Answer:**  
Consider an $N\times N$ switch.

In the worst case, all $N$ input ports may simultaneously send packets to the same output.

```text
I1 ─┐
I2 ─┤
I3 ─┼──→ O1
... │
IN ─┘
```

If each input operates at $R$ Gbps, the output may need to receive:

$$
N\times R
$$

Gbps.

Therefore, the switching fabric may need approximately:

$$
\boxed{N\times}
$$

the input line rate.

---

### Q12. What is Virtual Output Queueing (VOQ)?

**Answer:**  
Virtual Output Queueing is a technique in which each input maintains a separate queue for each possible output.

For an $N\times N$ switch, each input has $N$ queues.

Example for a 4-output switch:

```text
Input 1:

VOQ1 → packets for O1
VOQ2 → packets for O2
VOQ3 → packets for O3
VOQ4 → packets for O4
```

The scheduler can independently select packets from these queues.

---

### Q13. Why does VOQ eliminate HOL blocking?

**Answer:**  
In ordinary input FIFO queueing:

```text
[P1 → O1] [P2 → O2]
```

If $O1$ is blocked, $P2$ is also blocked.

With VOQ:

```text
VOQ1 → P1 → O1
VOQ2 → P2 → O2
```

If $O1$ is busy, the scheduler can still select $P2$ from VOQ2.

Therefore, packets destined for different outputs do not block one another.

$$
\boxed{\text{VOQ eliminates HOL blocking}}
$$

---

### Q14. How many VOQs are required for an $N\times N$ switch?

**Answer:**

Each of the $N$ inputs maintains $N$ queues.

Therefore:

$$
N\times N=N^2
$$

Hence:

$$
\boxed{N^2\text{ VOQs}}
$$

Example:

For an $8\times8$ switch:

$$
8^2=64
$$

VOQs.

For a $32\times32$ switch:

$$
32^2=1024
$$

VOQs.

---

### Q15. What is the drawback of VOQ?

**Answer:**  
The major drawback is **scheduler complexity**.

The scheduler has to examine many VOQs and select a conflict-free set of input-output connections within a very short timeslot.

For an $N\times N$ switch:

$$
N^2
$$

VOQs exist.

As $N$ increases, scheduling becomes increasingly complex.

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{\text{VOQ reduces HOL but increases scheduling complexity}}
$$

---

### Q16. What is PIM?

**Answer:**  
PIM stands for:

$$
\boxed{\text{Parallel Iterative Matching}}
$$

It is a scheduling algorithm used in input-queued/VOQ-based switches to quickly find a large set of conflict-free input-output matches.

Its objective is to:

- maximize packet transfers per timeslot,
- reduce unused switching capacity,
- perform scheduling quickly.

---

### Q17. Why does PIM use multiple iterations?

**Answer:**  
A single iteration may not find a sufficiently large matching.

Therefore, PIM repeats the matching process to find additional compatible input-output connections.

Conceptually:

```text
Requests
   ↓
Iteration 1
   ↓
Initial Matching
   ↓
Iteration 2
   ↓
Improved Matching
   ↓
...
   ↓
Final Matching
```

Multiple iterations allow the algorithm to approach a larger/maximal matching while still operating quickly.

---

### Q18. Compare Input Queueing, Output Queueing and VOQ.

**Answer:**

| Feature | Input Queueing | Output Queueing | VOQ |
|---|---|---|---|
| Queue location | Input | Output | Input |
| HOL blocking | Yes | No | No |
| Fabric speed | Normal | Approximately $N\times$ input rate in worst case | Normal-ish |
| Number of queues | $N$ | Output-dependent | $N^2$ |
| Scheduler complexity | Relatively low | Different architecture | High |
| Main problem | HOL blocking | Very high speed requirement | Complex scheduling |

The important progression is:

$$
\boxed{
Input\ Queue
\rightarrow
HOL
\rightarrow
VOQ
\rightarrow
Complex\ Scheduling
\rightarrow
PIM
}
$$

---

### Q19. Solve throughput/utilization numerical.

**Question:**  
An $8\times8$ crossbar operates for 20 timeslots. The maximum number of transfers per timeslot is 8. If 136 packets are successfully transferred, calculate utilization.

**Answer:**

Maximum possible transfers:

$$
8\times20=160
$$

Actual transfers:

$$
136
$$

Therefore:

$$
Utilization=
\frac{136}{160}\times100
$$

$$
\boxed{85\%}
$$

---

### Q20. Analyze this router scenario.

**Question:**  
A router has 32 input ports and 32 output ports. Each input maintains a single FIFO queue. Packets behind a blocked packet cannot be transmitted even if their destinations are free.

1. Identify the problem.
2. Which architecture can solve it?
3. How many VOQs are required?

**Answer:**

### 1. Problem

The problem is:

$$
\boxed{\text{Head-of-Line Blocking}}
$$

because a blocked packet at the head of a FIFO queue prevents packets behind it from being transmitted.

### 2. Solution

Use:

$$
\boxed{\text{Virtual Output Queueing (VOQ)}}
$$

### 3. Number of VOQs

There are:

$$
32\times32
$$

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{1024\text{ VOQs}}
$$

---

# 🔥 5 Questions I Would Especially Prepare for the Exam

If your professor wants **conceptual + application-based questions**, pay special attention to these:

### 1.
> Explain HOL blocking using a suitable input/output queue example. Why does it reduce crossbar utilization?

### 2.
> Compare input queueing, output queueing, and VOQ. Explain the major trade-offs of each.

### 3.
> An $N\times N$ switch uses output queueing. Explain why the switching fabric may need to operate at $N$ times the input line rate.

### 4.
> For an $N\times N$ crossbar, derive the number of crosspoints and number of VOQs.

$$
\boxed{\text{Crosspoints}=N^2}
$$

$$
\boxed{\text{VOQs}=N^2}
$$

### 5.
> Explain why PIM is required in a VOQ-based switch and how iterative matching improves switch utilization.

These five cover the **core reasoning** behind almost the entire Crossbar/HOL/VOQ/PIM section.

# CS 606T — Advanced Computer Networks
## Topic 7: Fast Path vs Slow Path

This is the **last remaining section from the Router Architectures PPT you provided**. After this, we can move to the next chapter/topic.

---

# 1. Why Fast Path and Slow Path?

A router performs many different tasks.

Some tasks must happen **for almost every packet**, and they must happen extremely quickly.

Other tasks happen **occasionally** and are not required for every packet.

So router processing is broadly divided into:

$$
\boxed{\text{Fast Path}}
$$

and

$$
\boxed{\text{Slow Path}}
$$

---

# 2. Fast Path

The **Fast Path** handles operations that are critical to real-time packet forwarding.

The main goal is:

> **Process and forward packets as quickly as possible.**

Typical fast-path operations from your PPT:

- IP header processing
- Error/checksum checking
- TTL update
- Packet forwarding
- Packet classification
- Packet queuing
- Packet scheduling

---

# 3. Fast Path — Packet Flow

A simplified flow is:

```text
Incoming Packet
       ↓
IP Header Processing
       ↓
Checksum / Header Validation
       ↓
TTL Processing
       ↓
Forwarding Lookup
       ↓
Packet Classification
       ↓
Queue Selection
       ↓
Scheduling
       ↓
Output Interface
```

These operations are performed at very high speed because packets can arrive continuously.

---

# 4. IP Header Processing

When a packet reaches the router, the router examines the IP header.

It may check:

- IP version
- Header length
- Packet length
- Source address
- Destination address
- TTL
- Protocol information
- Checksum, where applicable

The router needs this information to determine how the packet should be handled.

---

# 5. TTL Processing

TTL means:

> **Time To Live**

When an IP packet passes through a router, the TTL is decremented.

For example:

```text
Before router:

TTL = 5

       ↓ Router

After router:

TTL = 4
```

If the TTL reaches zero, the packet is discarded rather than being forwarded indefinitely.

The router can generate an ICMP error message to inform the sender.

---

# 6. Why Is TTL Important?

Suppose a routing problem creates a loop:

```text id="r3v1c8"
Router A → Router B
    ↑          ↓
    └──────────┘
```

Without TTL, a packet could circulate indefinitely.

TTL prevents this.

Example:

```text id="5y2j9c"
Packet:
TTL = 3

A → B
TTL = 2

B → A
TTL = 1

A → B
TTL = 0

Discard
```

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{\text{TTL prevents packets from circulating forever}}
$$

---

# 7. Checksum Processing

The router may need to validate and update header checksum information.

Because TTL changes:

```text
TTL before = 5
TTL after  = 4
```

the relevant checksum must also be updated/recomputed for protocols where the header checksum applies.

Your PPT summarizes this as:

> Check header → decrement TTL → restore/recompute checksum.

---

# 8. Packet Forwarding

This is one of the most important fast-path operations.

The router takes:

$$
Destination\ IP
$$

and performs a forwarding lookup.

Conceptually:

```text id="vjj6t3"
Destination IP
      ↓
Forwarding Table Lookup
      ↓
Next Hop / Output Interface
      ↓
Switching Fabric
```

The packet is then sent toward the appropriate output.

---

# 9. Packet Classification

Routers may need to distinguish packets based on fields such as:

- Source IP
- Destination IP
- Source port
- Destination port
- Protocol
- Other packet/header fields

For example:

```text id="xxi6kk"
VoIP packet      → High Priority Queue
Video packet     → Medium Priority Queue
Normal web data  → Normal Queue
```

Classification determines which rules should apply to the packet.

---

# 10. Packet Queuing

If packets arrive faster than an outgoing link can transmit them, they must wait.

Example:

```text id="l5lq2o"
Packets arriving
       ↓
┌──────────────┐
│ Packet Queue │
│ P1           │
│ P2           │
│ P3           │
│ P4           │
└──────────────┘
       ↓
Output Link
```

The queue provides temporary storage.

---

# 11. Packet Scheduling

When multiple packets are waiting, the router needs to determine:

> Which packet should be transmitted next?

Different scheduling algorithms can provide different service characteristics.

For example, a high-priority packet might be selected before a normal-priority packet.

This is especially important for:

- voice
- video
- interactive traffic
- QoS-sensitive applications

---

# 12. Why Is Fast Path Called "Fast"?

Because these operations are:

- performed very frequently,
- latency-sensitive,
- directly related to forwarding,
- required to sustain high packet throughput.

Therefore, router hardware is often designed to accelerate these operations.

Conceptually:

```text id="f7g9om"
Packet arrives
      ↓
FAST PATH
      ↓
Process quickly
      ↓
Forward
```

---

# 13. Slow Path

Now consider operations that don't need to be performed for every normal packet.

These are placed in the:

$$
\boxed{\text{Slow Path}}
$$

The slow path handles less frequent or more complex control/management operations.

Your PPT lists:

- Routing protocol keep-alive messages
- Routing table updates
- ARP processing
- Fragmentation and reassembly
- Advanced IP processing
- Source routing
- Timestamping
- ICMP error generation

---

# 14. Routing Protocol Keep-Alive Messages

Routers exchange control messages to maintain routing relationships with neighboring routers.

For example, a routing protocol may periodically send messages such as:

```text
Router A → Router B
     "Are you still available?"
```

These are not ordinary data packets being forwarded toward an end host.

They are part of the routing/control process.

Therefore, they belong to the slower control-processing path.

---

# 15. Routing Table Updates

Routing protocols continuously monitor network conditions.

If topology changes:

```text id="k8i0xw"
Before:

A → B → C

After link failure:

A → B   X   C
      ↓
    alternate path
```

the routing protocol may calculate a new route.

The routing table is then updated.

This is not required for every packet.

Therefore, it is a slow-path/control-plane activity.

---

# 16. ARP Processing

ARP stands for:

> **Address Resolution Protocol**

It maps an IPv4 address to a MAC address on a local network.

For example:

```text id="4xyqka"
IP:
192.168.1.10

        ↓ ARP

MAC:
AA:BB:CC:DD:EE:FF
```

ARP processing involves control messages and is therefore categorized in your PPT under the slow path.

---

# 17. Fragmentation and Reassembly

Suppose an incoming packet is larger than what the outgoing link can carry because of a smaller MTU.

Example:

```text id="6r7z8j"
Incoming packet:
4000 bytes

Outgoing MTU:
1500 bytes
```

The packet may need to be fragmented under IPv4 conditions.

Fragmentation involves additional processing.

Similarly, reassembly requires maintaining information about fragments.

Therefore, your PPT places these under the slow path.

---

# 18. Advanced IP Processing

Some advanced IP features require additional processing.

Your PPT gives examples:

### Source routing

The sender specifies routing information that influences the path.

### Timestamping

Information about timing can be inserted/processed.

### ICMP error generation

The router may generate ICMP messages in response to certain packet-processing errors.

These are not necessarily required for every normal packet.

Hence they are categorized as slow-path functions.

---

# 19. Fast Path vs Slow Path

This comparison is extremely important.

| Fast Path | Slow Path |
|---|---|
| Real-time packet processing | Control/exceptional processing |
| Very frequent | Less frequent |
| Must be very fast | Can tolerate relatively more processing time |
| Packet forwarding | Routing table updates |
| Packet classification | Routing protocol processing |
| Packet queuing | ARP processing |
| Packet scheduling | Fragmentation/reassembly |
| TTL/checksum processing | Advanced IP processing |
| Hardware acceleration commonly used | More software/control processing |

---

# 20. Important Conceptual Question

### Q: Is "slow path" necessarily a physically slow processor?

**No.**

"Slow path" does **not** mean:

> "The router intentionally processes it at a low CPU speed."

It means:

> These operations are not part of the high-speed, per-packet forwarding path and generally do not have the same stringent real-time processing requirement.

This distinction is important in exams.

---

# 21. Fast Path vs Routing

A common confusion is:

> Is routing itself fast path?

Generally, distinguish:

### Routing

Determines the network topology and calculates paths.

```text
Routing Protocol
      ↓
Routing Table
      ↓
Forwarding Table
```

This is control-plane processing.

### Forwarding

Actually moves packets:

```text
Packet
  ↓
Destination lookup
  ↓
Output interface
```

This is the fast path.

So remember:

$$
\boxed{\text{Routing decides the path}}
$$

while:

$$
\boxed{\text{Forwarding moves the packet}}
$$

---

# 22. Scenario Question

### Scenario

A router receives 1 million packets per second. For every packet, it needs to:

1. Examine the IP header.
2. Decrement TTL.
3. Perform forwarding lookup.
4. Select an output queue.

Which path handles these operations?

### Answer:

$$
\boxed{\text{Fast Path}}
$$

because these are frequent, per-packet forwarding operations.

---

# 23. Scenario Question

### Scenario

A link between two routers fails. The routing protocol detects the failure and calculates a new route.

Which path handles this?

### Answer:

$$
\boxed{\text{Slow Path / Control Plane}}
$$

because topology changes and routing-table updates are control operations rather than ordinary per-packet forwarding.

---

# 24. Scenario Question

A packet arrives with TTL = 1.

The router processes it.

What happens?

The router decrements:

$$
TTL:1\rightarrow0
$$

The packet is not forwarded further.

An ICMP error can be generated to inform the sender.

The processing involves both:

- fast-path TTL handling
- slow/control handling for ICMP error generation, as categorized in your PPT.

---

# 25. Exam Trick

### Question:

> Which operation is more likely to be optimized in router hardware: routing protocol update or forwarding lookup?

Answer:

$$
\boxed{\text{Forwarding lookup}}
$$

because forwarding occurs for packets at very high rates and is latency-sensitive.

Routing protocol updates occur much less frequently.

---

# 26. Another Important Connection

Now connect all the topics we've studied:

```text id="4a7m9g"
                  ROUTER
                    │
          ┌─────────┴─────────┐
          ↓                   ↓
      CONTROL PLANE       DATA PLANE
          │                   │
       Routing             Forwarding
          │                   │
   Routing Table       Forwarding Table
                              │
                              ↓
                         Fast Path
                              │
                              ↓
                       Switching Fabric
                              │
                              ↓
                         Output Queue
```

This is the architecture you should have in your head.

---

# 27. One Important Correction to Keep in Mind

Your professor's PPT categorizes **fragmentation/reassembly** and **ICMP error generation** under slow path.

For exam purposes, follow your professor's classification.

In real router implementations, the exact division between fast and slow path can vary depending on hardware, software architecture, packet type, and implementation.

---

# ⭐ Final Exam Summary

### Fast Path

Think:

> **"Packet is already here; process it and forward it quickly."**

$$
\boxed{
Header
\rightarrow
TTL
\rightarrow
Checksum
\rightarrow
Forwarding
\rightarrow
Classification
\rightarrow
Queue
\rightarrow
Schedule
}
$$

### Slow Path

Think:

> **"Something requiring control, exception, or more complex processing happened."**

$$
\boxed{
Routing\ Updates
+
ARP
+
Fragmentation
+
Advanced\ IP
+
ICMP
}
$$

---

### First topic: Random Variables

Before jumping into M/M/1, you should be comfortable with the probability concepts because the queueing formulas depend on them.

The first important distinction is:

$$
\boxed{\text{Discrete RV} \rightarrow PMF}
$$

$$
\boxed{\text{Continuous RV} \rightarrow PDF}
$$

For example, in a router:

- Number of packets arriving in 1 second → **Discrete**
- Packet transmission time → **Continuous**
- Number of packets waiting in a buffer → **Discrete**
- Time until the next packet arrives → **Continuous**

Then we'll build toward:

$$
\text{Poisson arrivals}
\rightarrow
\text{Exponential service}
\rightarrow
\text{M/M/1}
\rightarrow
\text{Little's Law}
\rightarrow
\text{M/M/m}
\rightarrow
\text{Switching-fabric throughput}
$$

# CS 606T — Advanced Computer Networks
## Chapter: Queuing Theory
### Topic 1: Random Variables

We’ll start from the absolute basics, but I’ll keep the explanation connected to **networking and queueing**, because later we need these concepts for Poisson processes and M/M/1.

---

# 1. What is a Random Variable?

A **random variable (RV)** is a function that assigns a numerical value to every possible outcome of a random experiment.

The important point:

> A random variable is **not itself random**. The outcome of the experiment is random; the RV simply converts that outcome into a number.

### Example 1 — Coin

Experiment:

> Toss a coin.

Sample space:

$$
S=\{H,T\}
$$

Define:

$$
X=
\begin{cases}
1 & \text{if Head}\\
0 & \text{if Tail}
\end{cases}
$$

So:

| Outcome | X |
|---|---:|
| H | 1 |
| T | 0 |

Here, $X$ is a random variable.

---

# 2. Networking Example

Suppose we observe a router for **1 second**.

Let:

$$
X=\text{number of packets arriving during that second}
$$

Possible values:

$$
X=0,1,2,3,4,\ldots
$$

We don't know beforehand how many packets will arrive.

For example:

- 0 packets
- 1 packet
- 5 packets
- 100 packets

Therefore, $X$ is a random variable.

This particular RV is **discrete**.

---

# 3. Discrete Random Variable

A random variable is **discrete** if its possible values are **countable**.

Examples:

### Network examples

- Number of packets arriving
- Number of packets in a queue
- Number of active connections
- Number of dropped packets
- Number of users connected to a router
- Number of packets transmitted in a time slot

For example:

$$
X=\text{number of packets in buffer}
$$

Possible values:

$$
0,1,2,3,\ldots
$$

These can be counted.

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{X\text{ is discrete}}
$$

---

# 4. Continuous Random Variable

A continuous random variable can take values over a continuous interval.

For example:

$$
T=\text{time until the next packet arrives}
$$

Possible values could be:

$$
0.1,\;0.15,\;0.153,\;0.1537,\;0.15372,\ldots
$$

There are infinitely many possibilities between any two values.

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{T\text{ is continuous}}
$$

### Network examples

- Packet transmission time
- Packet propagation delay
- Waiting time
- Service time
- Inter-arrival time
- Queueing delay

---

# 5. Very Important Exam Distinction

| Discrete | Continuous |
|---|---|
| Countable values | Uncountably many values |
| Number of packets | Packet delay |
| Number of customers | Waiting time |
| Number of connections | Service time |
| Number of dropped packets | Inter-arrival time |
| Uses PMF | Uses PDF |

Remember:

$$
\boxed{\text{Counting} \rightarrow \text{Discrete}}
$$

$$
\boxed{\text{Measuring time/length/etc.} \rightarrow \text{Continuous}}
$$

---

# 6. PMF — Probability Mass Function

For a **discrete random variable**, we use a **PMF**.

PMF tells us:

$$
\boxed{P(X=x)}
$$

That means:

> Probability that $X$ takes exactly the value $x$.

### Example

Suppose the number of coffees ordered by a customer is:

| X | P(X=x) |
|---:|---:|
| 1 | 0.65 |
| 2 | 0.25 |
| 3 | 0.10 |

Then:

$$
P(X=1)=0.65
$$

$$
P(X=2)=0.25
$$

$$
P(X=3)=0.10
$$

And:

$$
0.65+0.25+0.10=1
$$

Therefore it is a valid PMF.

---

# 7. Conditions for a PMF

For a discrete RV:

### Condition 1

$$
P(X=x)\geq0
$$

Probability cannot be negative.

### Condition 2

$$
\sum_x P(X=x)=1
$$

The probabilities of **all possible outcomes must sum to 1**.

### Example

Suppose:

$$
P(X=0)=0.2
$$

$$
P(X=1)=0.5
$$

$$
P(X=2)=0.3
$$

Then:

$$
0.2+0.5+0.3=1
$$

So this is a valid PMF.

---

# 8. PDF — Probability Density Function

For a **continuous random variable**, we use a **PDF**.

Suppose:

$$
T=\text{packet waiting time}
$$

and $f(t)$ is its PDF.

For continuous variables:

$$
\boxed{P(T=t)=0}
$$

This is extremely important.

You might think:

> Why is the probability of exactly 3 seconds zero?

Because there are infinitely many possible values around 3:

$$
2.999,\;2.9999,\;3,\;3.0001,\;3.001,\ldots
$$

A single point has zero area.

Instead, we calculate the probability over an **interval**.

$$
P(a\leq T\leq b)
=
\int_a^b f(t)\,dt
$$

So:

$$
\boxed{\text{Probability = Area under PDF}}
$$

---

# 9. PMF vs PDF — Extremely Important

This is a common exam question.

### PMF

For discrete $X$:

$$
\boxed{P(X=x)}
$$

gives the probability of an exact value.

Example:

$$
P(X=5)=0.2
$$

This is meaningful.

---

### PDF

For continuous $X$:

$$
\boxed{P(X=x)=0}
$$

Instead:

$$
\boxed{P(a<X<b)=\int_a^b f(x)\,dx}
$$

---

# 10. Example — Continuous Networking Variable

Suppose packet waiting time $T$ is continuous.

Question:

> What is the probability that the packet waits between 2 ms and 5 ms?

If the PDF is $f(t)$:

$$
P(2\leq T\leq5)
=
\int_2^5 f(t)\,dt
$$

You **do not** calculate:

$$
P(T=2)+P(T=5)
$$

That's wrong.

---

# 11. CDF

Another important concept.

The **Cumulative Distribution Function** is:

$$
\boxed{F_X(x)=P(X\leq x)}
$$

It gives the probability that the random variable is less than or equal to $x$.

### Example

Suppose:

$$
X=\text{number of packets arriving}
$$

and:

$$
P(X=0)=0.2
$$

$$
P(X=1)=0.5
$$

$$
P(X=2)=0.3
$$

Then:

$$
F(1)=P(X\leq1)
$$

Therefore:

$$
F(1)=P(X=0)+P(X=1)
$$

$$
=0.2+0.5
$$

$$
\boxed{F(1)=0.7}
$$

---

# 12. Important Difference: PMF vs CDF

PMF:

$$
P(X=x)
$$

CDF:

$$
P(X\leq x)
$$

Example:

$$
P(X=2)=0.3
$$

but:

$$
P(X\leq2)=1
$$

because:

$$
0.2+0.5+0.3=1
$$

---

# 13. The Distributions in Your PPT

Your professor specifically mentions:

### Discrete

1. Bernoulli
2. Binomial
3. Poisson

### Continuous

1. Uniform
2. Exponential

These are **very important** because they appear later in queueing.

Especially:

$$
\boxed{\text{Poisson} \rightarrow \text{packet arrivals}}
$$

and

$$
\boxed{\text{Exponential} \rightarrow \text{service/inter-arrival times}}
$$

This leads directly to:

$$
\boxed{\text{M/M/1 Queue}}
$$

where:

- First **M** → Markovian/Poisson arrivals
- Second **M** → exponential service
- **1** → one server

---

# 14. Quick Exam Check

Try these without looking at the answers.

### Q1
The number of packets arriving at a router in one second is:

A. Continuous RV  
B. Discrete RV

### Q2
Packet propagation delay is:

A. Discrete RV  
B. Continuous RV

### Q3
For a continuous random variable:

$$
P(X=5)=?
$$

### Q4
Which function is used for a discrete random variable?

A. PDF  
B. PMF

### Q5
What does

$$
F_X(10)
$$

represent?

### Q6
A buffer contains 0, 1, 2, 3, ... packets. Is the number of packets a discrete or continuous RV?

### Q7
If:

$$
P(X=0)=0.2,\quad P(X=1)=0.3,\quad P(X=2)=0.5
$$

find:

$$
P(X\leq1)
$$

### Q8
For a continuous RV, how do you calculate:

$$
P(2<X<5)?
$$

---

## ⭐ What you should remember from Topic 1

The core chain is:

$$
\boxed{
\text{Random Experiment}
\rightarrow
\text{Random Variable}
\rightarrow
\begin{cases}
\text{Discrete}\rightarrow PMF\\
\text{Continuous}\rightarrow PDF
\end{cases}
}
$$

And for this networking course:

$$
\boxed{\text{Packets/customers} \rightarrow \text{Discrete}}
$$

$$
\boxed{\text{Time/delay/service time} \rightarrow \text{Continuous}}
$$

Next, we should cover **Bernoulli → Binomial → Poisson random variables**, including the formulas and networking numericals.

# CS 606T — Advanced Computer Networks
## Queuing Theory — Topic 2: Bernoulli, Binomial & Poisson Random Variables

These three are **very important** because they form the foundation for understanding **Poisson traffic arrivals** and eventually the **M/M/1 queue**.

---

# 1. Bernoulli Random Variable

A Bernoulli experiment has exactly **two possible outcomes**:

- Success
- Failure

Define:

$$
X=
\begin{cases}
1 & \text{Success}\\
0 & \text{Failure}
\end{cases}
$$

If probability of success is $p$, then:

$$
P(X=1)=p
$$

and

$$
P(X=0)=1-p
$$

### Example — Packet transmission

Suppose a packet is successfully transmitted with probability $0.9$.

Define:

$$
X=
\begin{cases}
1 & \text{packet successfully transmitted}\\
0 & \text{packet lost}
\end{cases}
$$

Then:

$$
P(X=1)=0.9
$$

$$
P(X=0)=0.1
$$

This is a **Bernoulli random variable**.

---

# 2. Mean and Variance of Bernoulli RV

For:

$$
X\sim Bernoulli(p)
$$

Mean:

$$
\boxed{E[X]=p}
$$

Variance:

$$
\boxed{Var(X)=p(1-p)}
$$

### Example

If:

$$
p=0.8
$$

then:

$$
E[X]=0.8
$$

and:

$$
Var(X)=0.8(0.2)=0.16
$$

---

# 3. Binomial Random Variable

Now suppose we perform the **same Bernoulli experiment $n$ times**.

Let:

$$
X=\text{number of successes in }n\text{ trials}
$$

Then:

$$
\boxed{X\sim Binomial(n,p)}
$$

The PMF is:

$$
\boxed{
P(X=k)=
{n\choose k}p^k(1-p)^{n-k}
}
$$

where:

$$
k=0,1,2,\ldots,n
$$

---

# 4. Why the Combination?

Suppose we have 5 packet transmissions and want exactly 3 successful transmissions.

There are several possible arrangements:

$$
SSSFF
$$

$$
SSFSF
$$

$$
SSF SF
$$

etc.

The number of ways to select which 3 of the 5 positions are successes is:

$$
{5\choose3}
$$

Therefore:

$$
P(X=3)
=
{5\choose3}p^3(1-p)^2
$$

---

# 5. Networking Numerical — Binomial

Suppose a network sends **10 packets**.

Each packet has probability $0.9$ of being successfully delivered.

What is the probability that **exactly 8 packets** are delivered successfully?

Here:

$$
n=10
$$

$$
p=0.9
$$

$$
k=8
$$

Therefore:

$$
P(X=8)
=
{10\choose8}(0.9)^8(0.1)^2
$$

$$
=45(0.9)^8(0.1)^2
$$

$$
\boxed{P(X=8)\approx0.1937}
$$

So approximately:

$$
\boxed{19.37\%}
$$

---

# 6. Mean and Variance of Binomial

If:

$$
X\sim Binomial(n,p)
$$

then:

$$
\boxed{E[X]=np}
$$

and:

$$
\boxed{Var(X)=np(1-p)}
$$

### Example

10 packets, success probability $0.9$:

$$
E[X]=10(0.9)=9
$$

So on average:

$$
\boxed{9\text{ packets succeed}}
$$

Variance:

$$
Var(X)=10(0.9)(0.1)=0.9
$$

---

# 7. Bernoulli vs Binomial

This is a very common theoretical question.

| Bernoulli | Binomial |
|---|---|
| One trial | $n$ trials |
| Two outcomes | Counts successes |
| $X=0$ or $1$ | $X=0,1,\ldots,n$ |
| Parameter $p$ | Parameters $n,p$ |

You can think of:

$$
\boxed{\text{Bernoulli = one trial}}
$$

$$
\boxed{\text{Binomial = repeated Bernoulli trials}}
$$

---

# 8. Poisson Random Variable

Now we reach the **most important distribution for your networking queueing chapter**.

Suppose we don't care about individual packet success/failure.

Instead, we care about:

> How many packets arrive during a given time interval?

For example:

- packets arriving per second
- customers arriving per minute
- calls arriving per hour
- requests arriving at a server

These can often be modeled using a **Poisson random variable**.

---

# 9. Poisson PMF

If:

$$
X\sim Poisson(\lambda)
$$

then:

$$
\boxed{
P(X=k)=
e^{-\lambda}\frac{\lambda^k}{k!}
}
$$

where:

- $\lambda$ = average number of events during the interval
- $k=0,1,2,\ldots$

---

# 10. Understanding $\lambda$

This is extremely important.

Suppose:

> A router receives an average of 100 packets per second.

Then:

$$
\lambda=100
$$

for a **1-second interval**.

For 5 seconds:

$$
\lambda=100\times5
$$

$$
\boxed{\lambda=500}
$$

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{\lambda=\text{rate}\times\text{time}}
$$

---

# 11. Networking Numerical

Suppose packets arrive at a router according to a Poisson distribution with rate:

$$
\lambda=5\text{ packets/sec}
$$

Find the probability of exactly 3 packets arriving during one second.

We have:

$$
k=3
$$

$$
\lambda=5
$$

Therefore:

$$
P(X=3)
=
e^{-5}\frac{5^3}{3!}
$$

$$
=
e^{-5}\frac{125}{6}
$$

$$
\boxed{P(X=3)\approx0.1404}
$$

So approximately:

$$
\boxed{14.04\%}
$$

---

# 12. Probability of Zero Arrivals

This is especially useful in networking.

Using:

$$
P(X=k)=e^{-\lambda}\frac{\lambda^k}{k!}
$$

put:

$$
k=0
$$

Then:

$$
P(X=0)
=
e^{-\lambda}\frac{\lambda^0}{0!}
$$

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{P(X=0)=e^{-\lambda}}
$$

### Example

Packets arrive at:

$$
\lambda=4/sec
$$

Probability of no packets arriving during one second:

$$
P(X=0)=e^{-4}
$$

$$
\boxed{\approx0.0183}
$$

So there is approximately a:

$$
\boxed{1.83\%}
$$

chance of no arrivals.

---

# 13. Probability of At Least One Arrival

This is another common numerical.

We want:

$$
P(X\geq1)
$$

Instead of adding:

$$
P(X=1)+P(X=2)+P(X=3)+\cdots
$$

use the complement:

$$
P(X\geq1)=1-P(X=0)
$$

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{P(X\geq1)=1-e^{-\lambda}}
$$

For:

$$
\lambda=4
$$

$$
P(X\geq1)=1-e^{-4}
$$

$$
\boxed{\approx0.9817}
$$

---

# 14. Poisson Mean and Variance

For:

$$
X\sim Poisson(\lambda)
$$

we have:

$$
\boxed{E[X]=\lambda}
$$

and:

$$
\boxed{Var(X)=\lambda}
$$

This is a very important property.

Unlike Binomial:

$$
Var(X)=np(1-p)
$$

For Poisson:

$$
\boxed{\text{Mean}=\text{Variance}=\lambda}
$$

---

# 15. Poisson vs Binomial

This is a favorite conceptual question.

### Binomial

We have:

- fixed number of trials $n$
- each trial has success probability $p$
- count successes

$$
P(X=k)=
{n\choose k}p^k(1-p)^{n-k}
$$

### Poisson

We have:

- events occurring over time/space
- average rate $\lambda$
- count events in an interval

$$
P(X=k)=e^{-\lambda}\frac{\lambda^k}{k!}
$$

---

# 16. Poisson as an Approximation to Binomial

When:

$$
n\rightarrow\text{large}
$$

and:

$$
p\rightarrow0
$$

while:

$$
np=\lambda
$$

remains finite, Binomial can be approximated by Poisson.

So:

$$
\boxed{Binomial(n,p)\approx Poisson(np)}
$$

### Example

Suppose:

$$
n=1000
$$

$$
p=0.002
$$

Then:

$$
np=1000(0.002)=2
$$

Therefore:

$$
Binomial(1000,0.002)
\approx
Poisson(2)
$$

This approximation is very useful when $n$ is large and $p$ is small.

---

# 17. Why Poisson Matters So Much in Networking

Suppose:

$$
\lambda=1000\text{ packets/sec}
$$

A queueing model can treat packet arrivals as:

$$
N(t)\sim Poisson(\lambda t)
$$

For a 10 ms interval:

$$
t=0.01
$$

Therefore:

$$
\lambda t=1000(0.01)=10
$$

So:

$$
\boxed{N(0.01)\sim Poisson(10)}
$$

This tells us the probability distribution of the number of packets arriving during those 10 ms.

This becomes the foundation of the **Poisson process**.

---

# 18. Three Distributions You Must Know

### Bernoulli

One experiment:

$$
\boxed{X\in\{0,1\}}
$$

### Binomial

Number of successes in $n$ trials:

$$
\boxed{
P(X=k)=
{n\choose k}p^k(1-p)^{n-k}
}
$$

### Poisson

Number of events in an interval:

$$
\boxed{
P(X=k)=
e^{-\lambda}\frac{\lambda^k}{k!}
}
$$

---

# ⭐ Exam-Tricky Questions

### Q1
A router receives packets at an average rate of 200 packets/sec. What is the Poisson parameter for 50 ms?

$$
\lambda t=200(0.05)
$$

$$
\boxed{\lambda=10}
$$

---

### Q2
For a Poisson RV with parameter $\lambda=5$, what are its mean and variance?

$$
\boxed{E[X]=5}
$$

$$
\boxed{Var(X)=5}
$$

---

### Q3
A Poisson random variable has:

$$
P(X=0)=?
$$

Answer:

$$
\boxed{e^{-\lambda}}
$$

---

### Q4
If packet arrivals are Poisson with rate 50 packets/sec, what is the probability of no packet arriving during 20 ms?

$$
\lambda t=50(0.02)=1
$$

Therefore:

$$
P(X=0)=e^{-1}
$$

$$
\boxed{\approx0.3679}
$$

---

### Q5 — Conceptual

> The number of packets arriving in one second is discrete, but the time between two consecutive packet arrivals is continuous. Explain.

**Answer:** Number of arrivals is a count:

$$
0,1,2,\ldots
$$

so it is discrete. Inter-arrival time can take any nonnegative real value, so it is continuous.

This distinction becomes **very important in the Poisson process**.

---

# 🔥 One Important Connection

You should remember this chain:

$$
\boxed{
\text{Poisson RV}
\rightarrow
\text{Poisson Process}
}
$$

A Poisson RV tells us:

> **How many events occur during an interval?**

A Poisson process tells us:

> **How events occur over time.**

And then:

$$
\boxed{
\text{Poisson Process}
\rightarrow
\text{Exponential Inter-arrival Time}
}
$$

and eventually:

$$
\boxed{
\text{Poisson Arrivals + Exponential Service}
\rightarrow
M/M/1
}
$$

So the next topic is **Exponential Random Variable + Memoryless Property**, which is particularly important because your PPT explicitly derives the memoryless property and uses it in Markov/queueing models.

## Next Topic: Throughput Analysis of Switching Fabric with Input Queuing

This is the **last major topic in the Queuing Theory material you shared**, and it connects directly with your previous **Crossbar + Input Queuing + HOL Blocking** topic.

---

# 1. Why do we need throughput analysis?

Consider an **N × N switching fabric**.

- There are `N` input ports.
- There are `N` output ports.
- Packets arrive at input queues.
- In every time slot, the scheduler decides which packets can cross the fabric.
- An output can receive **at most one packet per slot**.

The question is:

> **What fraction of the maximum possible throughput can an input-queued switch achieve?**

Because of **HOL (Head-of-Line) blocking**, the answer is surprisingly less than 100%.

---

# 2. Assumptions

The analysis in your PPT assumes:

1. Fixed-length packets.
2. Slotted time.
3. There are `N` input ports and `N` output ports.
4. Every packet's destination is equally likely among the `N` output ports.
5. Each input has an input queue.
6. Only the **HOL packet** of each input queue is considered for scheduling.

Suppose:

```text
             Outputs
           O1 O2 O3 O4
            |  |  |  |
I1 ---------+--+--+--+
I2 ---------+--+--+--+
I3 ---------+--+--+--+
I4 ---------+--+--+--+
```

In each time slot, multiple packets can cross simultaneously **provided they don't request the same output**.

---

# 3. What happens in one time slot?

Suppose there are 4 inputs:

```text
Input 1 → Output 1
Input 2 → Output 1
Input 3 → Output 3
Input 4 → Output 4
```

We have:

```text
I1 → O1
I2 → O1
I3 → O3
I4 → O4
```

Both `I1` and `I2` want `O1`.

But an output can accept only **one packet** in a slot.

Therefore:

```text
O1 ← one packet
O3 ← one packet
O4 ← one packet
```

Maximum packets transmitted = **3**

Instead of 4.

The losing packet remains in the input queue.

---

# 4. Definition of ρ

Let

$$
\rho
$$

represent the **per-output-port throughput/utilization**.

If:

$$
\rho = 0.586
$$

then each output is successfully receiving packets at approximately:

$$
58.6\%
$$

of its maximum capacity.

For an `N × N` switch:

$$
E[F] = N\rho
$$

where:

- `F` = number of packets transferred in one slot
- `N` = number of outputs
- `ρ` = per-port throughput

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{\text{Switch Throughput}=N\rho}
$$

---

# 5. Introducing $B_i$

Your PPT defines:

> $B_{i,m}$ = number of packets meant for output `i` but not transmitted in slot `m`.

For a particular output:

```text
Several HOL packets
       ↓
I1 → O1
I2 → O1
I3 → O1
I4 → O1
       ↓
Only ONE can be transmitted
```

The remaining packets are blocked.

So we analyze the number of packets waiting for each output.

The PPT obtains:

$$
E[B_i] = \frac{\rho^2}{2(1-\rho)}
$$

for the relevant queueing model.

---

# 6. Deriving switch throughput

We have:

$$
F_m=N-\sum_{i=1}^{N}B_{i,m}
$$

Taking expectation:

$$
E[F]
=
N-\sum_{i=1}^{N}E[B_i]
$$

Since all output ports are statistically identical:

$$
E[B_1]=E[B_2]=\cdots=E[B_N]
$$

Therefore:

$$
E[F]=N-NE[B_i]
$$

or

$$
\boxed{E[F]=N(1-E[B_i])}
$$

But we also know:

$$
E[F]=N\rho
$$

Therefore:

$$
N\rho=N(1-E[B_i])
$$

Cancel `N`:

$$
\rho=1-E[B_i]
$$

Using the queueing expression gives the equation whose solution is:

$$
\boxed{\rho\approx0.586}
$$

Thus:

$$
\boxed{\text{Maximum throughput}\approx58.6\%}
$$

under these assumptions.

---

# 7. Why is 58.6% important?

This is a **very important exam result**.

Even though the switch physically has enough capacity to potentially transfer:

$$
N
$$

packets per slot, input queuing with HOL blocking prevents full utilization.

Approximately:

$$
\boxed{58.6\%}
$$

of the ideal throughput is achieved under the classic random-destination HOL model.

This is one of the major reasons why **Virtual Output Queuing (VOQ)** is used.

---

# 8. Connection with HOL Blocking

Remember your previous topic:

### Input Queuing

Each input has one FIFO queue:

```text
Input 1:

[Packet → O1] [Packet → O3] [Packet → O2]
      ↑
     HOL
```

Suppose the first packet wants `O1`, but `O1` is busy.

Then:

```text
[Packet → O1] [Packet → O3] [Packet → O2]
      BLOCKED
                  ↓
             cannot move
```

Even though the second packet could potentially go to free `O3`, it cannot overtake the first packet.

That's **HOL blocking**.

---

# 9. Why VOQ solves this problem

Instead of one FIFO queue:

```text
Input 1
   |
   +---- Queue for O1
   |
   +---- Queue for O2
   |
   +---- Queue for O3
   |
   +---- Queue for O4
```

Now suppose:

```text
O1 ← busy
O3 ← free
```

The packet waiting for `O3` can still be transmitted.

Therefore, packets behind a blocked packet don't necessarily have to wait.

For an `N × N` switch:

$$
\boxed{N^2}
$$

VOQs are required.

---

# 10. Important comparison

| Architecture | Main issue | Theoretical throughput |
|---|---|---:|
| Input Queuing | HOL blocking | ~58.6% under classic assumptions |
| Output Queuing | Requires very fast fabric | 100% |
| Virtual Output Queuing | Complex scheduler | Can approach 100% |
| Crossbar + VOQ | Scheduling complexity | Very high |

---

# 11. Exam Numerical

### Question

An `8 × 8` input-queued switching fabric operates under the classical random-destination model. Assume the maximum per-port throughput due to HOL blocking is approximately `0.586`.

Find:

1. Per-port throughput
2. Total average packets transmitted per slot
3. Percentage of ideal switch capacity utilized

### Solution

Given:

$$
N=8
$$

and

$$
\rho=0.586
$$

### 1. Per-port throughput

$$
\boxed{\rho=0.586}
$$

or

$$
58.6\%
$$

### 2. Total throughput

$$
E[F]=N\rho
$$

$$
=8(0.586)
$$

$$
=4.688
$$

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{E[F]\approx4.688\text{ packets/slot}}
$$

### 3. Utilization

Maximum possible:

$$
8\text{ packets/slot}
$$

Actual:

$$
4.688
$$

Therefore:

$$
\frac{4.688}{8}\times100
=
58.6\%
$$

$$
\boxed{58.6\%}
$$

---

# 12. Very important conceptual question

### Q: Why can't an input-queued crossbar achieve 100% throughput?

**Answer:**

Because of **Head-of-Line blocking**. A packet at the head of an input FIFO may be waiting for a busy output. Packets behind it may be destined for free outputs, but they cannot overtake the HOL packet. Consequently, some output transmission opportunities remain unused.

---

# 13. Scenario-based question

### Scenario

A router has a crossbar switching fabric with input FIFO queues. During a particular slot:

```text
I1 → O1
I2 → O1
I3 → O3
I4 → O4
```

Explain why all four packets cannot be transmitted simultaneously.

### Answer

`I1` and `I2` both request the same output `O1`. Since an output can accept only one packet during a time slot, the scheduler can select only one of these packets.

The other packet remains at the head of its input queue and may cause HOL blocking.

Therefore, only:

```text
I1 → O1
I3 → O3
I4 → O4
```

can be transmitted.

Total:

$$
\boxed{3\text{ packets}}
$$

---

# 14. What you should remember for the exam

### Core formulas

$$
\boxed{E[F]=N\rho}
$$

and

$$
\boxed{E[F]=N(1-E[B_i])}
$$

Therefore:

$$
\boxed{\rho=1-E[B_i]}
$$

Under the classical input-queued HOL model:

$$
\boxed{\rho\approx0.586}
$$

So:

$$
\boxed{\text{Maximum throughput}\approx58.6\%}
$$

---
